# Episode 1: The Ledger

## The Tree

November 1769. The Santa Clara Valley, Alta California.

The fog had burned off by midday, and the plain ran empty to the hills. Gaspar de Portolá’s soldiers made camp on the bank of a creek whose name they had not yet fixed in writing. Above them stood a redwood unlike any tree the diarists had seen on the march north from San Diego: one trunk split into two, rising like a tall stick against the sky. Someone in the party would call it *el palo alto*—the high tree—and the name would outlive the empire that sent them here.

Father Juan Crespí, a Franciscan priest traveling with the expedition, kept the kind of journal Madrid expected from a reconnaissance: distances, water, fuel, the disposition of the land for settlement or defense. His entries for the November halt describe a level plain wooded with oak and live oak and a good stream running through it, land he called "so very good and mellow that it can not be excelled." The tall redwood the soldiers named *palo alto* comes down through the expedition's tradition more than through his page; later editors and local historians tie that halt to the tree that still stands beside San Francisquito Creek, on ground that will later be a university town named for the stick itself.

Portolá had not come to plant a colony. Spain had no surplus population to send. José de Gálvez, inspector general of New Spain, had ordered the push north after expelling the Jesuits in 1767—a Bourbon administrator’s solution to a strategic problem: Russians moving down the Pacific coast, British ships in the north Pacific, and a Spanish claim on paper that had never been occupied in force.



The expedition was the first human instrument of that policy on the ground. Portolá commanded the soldiers. Junípero Serra, already aging, would found missions behind the military line. Crespí wrote the country into existence for readers who would never see it.

What they saw was useful. The valley floor offered grass and water. The creek gave a line on a map. The tall redwood gave a landmark visible from a distance—a natural surveyor’s mark before anyone in California owned a surveyor’s chain. In a territory where possession would always be argued with reference to trees, bends in water, and hand-drawn sketches, a tree like this was already a kind of deed.

They did not stay. The expedition moved on toward Monterey and the Bay, searching for the harbor Spain’s charts promised. They would stumble onto San Francisco Bay from the land side and fail, at first, to recognize what they had found. But they had paused long enough for ink to fix this place in the record, and the record is the subject of this episode.

The redwood still stands, fenced and braced, on the edge of a campus where the street signs carry the names of men who ordered missions built and grants signed. Twelve feet up and lit at night, the signs are easy not to read. The tree is harder to ignore. It was never a secret. It was only subliminal.



Portolá’s men broke camp and marched away. The Ohlone people whose villages lay in the valley did not appear in Crespí’s pages as negotiators or proprietors. They appear, when they appear at all, as part of the landscape being assessed—potential neophytes for a system not yet built here, labor for missions still on paper. That omission is also a record. Whoever keeps the journal decides who counts as a person with a name worth preserving.

Within eight years Franciscans would found Mission Santa Clara de Asís on this plain, close enough that the tree would mark the edge of a world remade by registers and rations. The creek would become a boundary in grants and diseños. The tall stick would become a postal address, then a railroad stop, then a university seal.

But in November 1769 none of that exists. There is only fog lifting, soldiers cooking, a priest writing, and a tree that is already doing work no one has yet paid for—holding a line on the ground that strangers will still be fighting over two centuries later.

The expedition left the valley. The diary remained.

## The rivals who never came

The Russian-American Company established Fort Ross on the coast north of the Bay
in 1812, roughly sixty miles from the Presidio, and farmed and hunted there for
three decades. It was a commercial outpost rather than an invasion, and it was
sold in 1841 to John Sutter — the same Sutter on whose land the gold would be
found seven years later, which is the kind of connection this book will keep
encountering and should not overstate.

\newpage



## Baptismal Register

1777–1800s. Mission Santa Clara and the coast chain.



The street runs from El Camino Real to Serra Street, across the front of the
campus, and the sign says Galvez.

It is named for José de Gálvez (1720–1787), inspector general of New Spain — a
Bourbon administrator, not a priest. In 1767 he carried out the royal decree
expelling the Jesuits from the Spanish empire. The next year, under Carlos III,
he went to Baja California and appointed the two men who would actually walk
north: Gaspar de Portolá as military commander, and Junípero Serra, a
Franciscan, as religious leader of the Alta California expedition. The missions
were Gálvez's idea. Serra executed them. David Starr Jordan, the university's
first president, spent years naming the campus after the Spanish and Mexican
officials he wanted students to remember; Galvez Street is that decision, still
in force, at the front door.

That is the first artifact. The second is a line in a book those men caused to
be kept.

The entry gives a date, a Spanish name, a note of parentage, and the name of the
priest who performed the rite. What it does not give is the name the person had
the day before.

Mission baptismal registers are the founding documents of California, and they
are administrative records rather than spiritual ones. They were kept because
the system required them: a baptism created an obligation, assigned a location,
and produced a person who could be counted, assigned to work, pursued if he
left, and accounted for at the end of the year. The registers were reviewed by
superiors. They were the basis of reporting to Mexico City and to Madrid.

California's first form of power was the power to write people down. The campus
still writes the man who ordered it done.

## A system, not a settlement

Spain came north from Baja California in 1769 and did not send settlers, because
it did not have any to send. What it sent instead was a system with three parts.

The presidio supplied force: a garrison, usually small, frequently unpaid, and
chronically short of everything. The pueblo supplied provisions, in theory,
though the civilian towns established at San José and Los Angeles remained tiny
for decades. And the mission supplied labour and record, which made it the
component that actually did the work.

Twenty-one missions were eventually founded along the coast, linked by the road
that came to be called El Camino Real. Each was an agricultural enterprise —
grain, cattle, hides, tallow, wine — run by two or three Franciscan priests
using Indigenous labour, and each maintained registers of baptisms, marriages,
and burials that in many cases survive.

The strategic purpose was never conversion alone. Spain was in California
because Russian fur traders were moving down the Pacific coast and British ships
were in the north Pacific, and the empire had a claim it had not occupied.
Occupation required people, people required an economy, and the fastest
available route to both was to convert the existing population into a resident
agricultural workforce. The theological project and the imperial one arrived on
the same ships and were not, at the level of policy, easily separable.

## 320 Dolores Street

Mission San Francisco de Asís was founded in June 1776, a few days before a
document was signed on the other coast asserting that governments derive their
just powers from the consent of the governed.

The building is still there, at 320 Dolores Street. It is the oldest intact
structure in San Francisco. It survived the earthquake of 1906 when almost
nothing around it did, and it is worth walking into, because the scale is
instructive: it is small, thick-walled, and plain, and it looks exactly like
what it was, which is an administrative outpost with a chapel in it.

The Presidio was established a few weeks later at the tip of the peninsula,
guarding the entrance to the bay. Force and record, sited within a few miles of
each other, and between them the whole apparatus of Spanish California.

What Spain was actually claiming was the harbour. The Golden Gate is a narrow
entrance to one of the largest protected anchorages in the world, and the empire
that held it would control access to everything behind it. The mission and the
presidio are instrumentation around a passage, and the passage is the
reason either of them exists.

## What the labour actually was

The mission economy ran on Indigenous labour that was not free to leave.

Once baptised, a neophyte was considered subject to mission discipline.
Departure without permission was treated as flight, and soldiers were sent to
retrieve those who left. Work was assigned, rations were distributed, and
punishment for infractions was corporal and administered by the priests. Housing
was communal and, for unmarried women and girls, locked at night.

Contemporary observers noticed. Visitors from other European powers, including
French and Russian expeditions with no particular sympathy for Indigenous
Californians, recorded the arrangement in terms ranging from approving to
appalled. The Franciscans defended it in their own correspondence, which means
the terms of the debate are preserved and the arrangement is not in dispute.

The demographic outcome is the central fact of the period. Scholarly estimates
of the Indigenous population of California before contact vary substantially —
the most cited reconstructions put it somewhere in the range of three hundred
thousand, with a wide margin — and the population within the mission districts
fell catastrophically over the six decades of the mission system, principally
through introduced disease compounded by concentrated living conditions,
malnutrition, and disrupted subsistence. The figures differ by source and by
method, and the disagreement is worth naming rather than resolving with a single
round number.

Death rates inside the missions exceeded birth rates for most of the period,
which meant the system required continuous recruitment simply to maintain its
workforce. That is not an incidental cruelty. It is a structural property of the
arrangement, and it is visible in the missions' own registers, because the
missions counted burials too.

## The archive against the archivist

Which produces the strangest feature of this chapter's evidence.

Almost everything known in detail about Indigenous experience in the mission
period comes from records kept by the institution responsible. The registers,
the annual reports, the correspondence with superiors, the responses to official
questionnaires: all of it produced by Franciscans, for Franciscan and royal
purposes.

And it documents the resistance anyway. It records flight, and the expeditions
sent to recover the people who fled. It records revolts — at San Diego in 1775,
at Santa Barbara and elsewhere in 1824 — with names, causes, and outcomes. It
records the burials. An institution that counts everything ends up counting the
evidence against itself, which is a recurring piece of good fortune for the
historian and a recurring problem for the institution.

The registers are still consulted. Descendant communities, including the Muwekma
Ohlone, use them to establish lineage, which means a set of documents created to
convert people into an administrable population is now used by their descendants
to demonstrate that they were there. The instrument has outlived its purpose and
been turned around.

## Before markets

California in 1821 had no significant commerce, no meaningful currency, almost
no settlers, and a governing apparatus of a few hundred soldiers and a few dozen
priests. By any economic measure it was among the least developed places claimed
by any European power.

It also had a complete land system, a controlled labour force, and fifty years
of records.

Power began here before markets did, and it began as paperwork. Everything that follows — the ranchos, the American land commission, the railroad's grants,
the university's endowment — is a transfer of assets that the mission system had
already identified, mapped, and staffed.

\newpage



## Paper State

1790s–1821. Monterey, the Presidio, correspondence to Mexico City.

The supply ship came when it came.

For most of the Spanish period the Presidio of San Francisco was garrisoned by a
few dozen soldiers, frequently in arrears of pay, wearing uniforms that had worn
out, and dependent for European goods on vessels that arrived at intervals
measured in seasons rather than weeks. The empire that claimed the finest
harbour on the Pacific coast was in practice unable to keep its garrison in
boots.

This is a chapter about how that arrangement nonetheless worked, and for forty-
five years it did.

## Governing by correspondence

The binding constraint on Spanish California was the speed of a letter.

Madrid to Mexico City was months. Mexico City to Monterey was months more, over
land or by a sea route that fought contrary winds most of the way up the coast.
A question referred to higher authority and answered would consume the better
part of a year, by which time the situation prompting it had resolved itself one
way or another.

The practical consequence is that authority devolved to the point of contact.
Decisions in Alta California were made in Alta California — by the governor, by
the presidio commanders, by the mission presidents — and reported afterwards.
Instructions from the centre arrived as general policy rather than as specific
direction, and the famous formula of Spanish colonial administration, *obedezco
pero no cumplo* — I obey but I do not comply — describes a functioning
arrangement rather than mere insubordination. An official acknowledged the
crown's authority while explaining that local conditions made the particular
order impracticable, and by the time the exchange concluded, years had passed.

An empire governed this way is held together by documents, because documents are
the only thing that travels. Maps, manifests, reports, and registers were not
descriptions of Spanish authority in California. They were the substance of it.

## Charting as possession

The charts deserve particular attention, because their audience was not local.

Spanish expeditions surveyed the coast, named its features, recorded soundings
and anchorages, and filed the results. None of this improved life for anyone in
California. All of it was directed at other European powers, for whom a charted
and named coastline with a documented history of occupation constituted a claim
that a rival would have to contest formally rather than simply ignore.

This is why the enterprise looks so disproportionate from inside. Spain invested
in mapping, in mission foundations, and in a garrison at a harbour it did
nothing to develop, because the asset being protected was not the settlement. It
was the claim, and the claim had value in a diplomatic system where such things
were adjudicated on paper.

Sovereignty preceded settlement, and it preceded any economy that could have
justified it.

## The passage nobody built

The Golden Gate is roughly a mile and a half wide. Behind it lies a bay system
covering several hundred square miles, fed by the rivers draining the Central
Valley, with anchorage sufficient for any fleet then afloat and shelter from the
Pacific in every weather.

A passage of this kind is unusual in this book because nobody constructed it.
The Central Pacific had to lay track; Standard Oil had to build refineries; a
platform has to be coded. The Bay was simply there, and its value was obvious to
anyone who sailed past it — which is why the Spanish, who had sailed past it for
two centuries without finding it, moved to occupy it within seven years of the
Portolá expedition stumbling onto it overland in 1769.

What the Bay controls is access to the interior of northern California, and in
due course it will control the export of everything the interior produces:
hides, then gold, then wheat, then fruit, then the output of a technology
industry forty miles down the peninsula. Every subsequent chapter in this act
happens within a day's ride of it.

## The road

The other piece of durable infrastructure from this period is El Camino Real,
which was not a road in any engineered sense but a route: the track connecting
the missions, presidios, and pueblos in a chain up the coast.

It matters because it fixed the geography. The missions were sited roughly a
day's travel apart, on good water and workable land, and those sites became the
towns. San Diego, San Juan Capistrano, San Gabriel, Santa Barbara, San Luis
Obispo, Monterey, Santa Clara, San José, San Francisco: the settlement pattern
of coastal California was determined by a Franciscan logistics decision in the
1770s, and it has not changed since.

The route itself is still the Peninsula's main street. Drive south from San
Francisco today and El Camino Real runs through Millbrae, Burlingame, San Mateo,
Redwood City, Menlo Park, Palo Alto, Mountain View, and Sunnyvale — which is to
say it runs directly through the towns that later episodes is about, past the Stanford
campus, within sight of the buildings where the semiconductor industry was
founded.

A Spanish supply road became the main street of Silicon Valley, and it kept the
name.

## The other paper state

There is one more institution operating here, and it is the one that will produce the next transfer: the land grant.

Spanish authorities issued very few private grants in Alta California — a few
dozen over the whole period — because the missions held most of the usable land
in trust, at least in theory, for the Indigenous communities attached to them.
The grants that were made went to soldiers and officials, and they were made on
the same basis as everything else in this chapter: a document describing
boundaries by reference to landmarks, filed with an authority that had no
capacity to survey or enforce.

The instrument was in place before the land was available. When secularisation
makes the mission holdings available in the 1830s, there is already a method
for distributing them and already a class positioned to receive them.

## The rivals who never came

It is worth asking whether the strategy was necessary, because the threat Spain
was defending against never fully materialised and the defence nonetheless
determined the shape of California.

The Russian-American Company established Fort Ross on the coast north of the Bay
in 1812, roughly sixty miles from the Presidio, and farmed and hunted there for
three decades. It was a commercial outpost rather than an invasion, and it was
sold in 1841 to John Sutter — the same Sutter on whose land the gold would be
found seven years later, which is the kind of connection the story will keep
encountering and should not overstate.

The British were a more serious prospect. The Hudson's Bay Company operated in
the Pacific Northwest and maintained an agent in San Francisco from the early
1840s, and British naval interest in California was real enough that American
officials worried about it continuously in the years before 1846.

Neither took the territory. But the anticipation of them is why the missions
were founded, why the Presidio was garrisoned, and why Spain held a harbour it
never developed. Fifty
years of occupation, twenty-one missions, and a demographic catastrophe were the
price of foreclosing something that did not happen.

When the United States finally took California it did so from Mexico, and what
it acquired was the Spanish claim with two changes of ownership recorded on it.

## Who this cost

The peoples of the Bay shoreline had their territory designated a strategic
asset of the Spanish crown in documents they never saw, in a language they did
not read, on the basis of a legal theory — that European sovereigns could
allocate non-Christian lands among themselves — that had been developed several
centuries earlier and several thousand miles away.

And the garrison was itself expendable. Soldiers at the Presidio were unpaid for
long periods, supplied irregularly, and unable to leave. They were the
instrument of the empire in California, not beneficiaries of it, and their
complaints survive in the same correspondence that documents everything else.

By 1821 Spain has held the best harbour on the Pacific coast for forty-five
years using a garrison, a chain of missions, and a filing system, and has
developed essentially none of it. That was not a failure of the strategy. It was
the strategy, and the claim it established is what Mexico inherits, and what the
United States eventually takes.

---

1830s. Thin sovereignty and grants.

A Mexican-era California land grant describes its boundaries by reference to a
creek, a stand of oaks, a hill, and the corner of somebody else's property. The
accompanying map — the *diseño* — is frequently a freehand sketch. It carries
the signature of a governor whose own authority is contested, in a territory his
government cannot reach.

That document is worth several hundred thousand dollars in gold within thirty
years, and the courts of another country will spend decades deciding what it
means. Property as an assertion nobody is in a position to verify is the
condition of California in the 1820s, and it is the founding condition of every
fortune in this book.

## Inheriting a territory without the means to hold it

Mexico became independent in 1821 and acquired Alta California along with
everything else, which meant it acquired the same distances that had defeated
Spanish administration with substantially fewer resources to apply to them.

The new republic was fighting for its existence through most of the following
three decades: coups, constitutional crises, foreign interventions, the loss of
Texas, and finally the war with the United States. Alta California was the
furthest, poorest, and least populated of its territories, and it received the
attention that ranking implies. Governors were appointed in Mexico City and
frequently resisted on arrival. On several occasions Californios expelled them.
In 1836 a local revolt produced a governor who governed with local consent and
minimal reference to the capital.

The formal picture is a territory of the Mexican republic. The practical picture
is a few thousand non-Indigenous people, some hundreds of miles apart, governing
themselves through family alliance and local force, with a capital at Monterey
whose writ extended about as far as its ability to send someone.

## What replaced administration

Land grants did. Where Spain had issued a few dozen over five decades, Mexican
governors issued hundreds, and the practice accelerated through the 1830s and
1840s.

A grant was not primarily a real-estate transaction. It was an instrument of
governance in a place with no other instruments. Granting land to a man created
an interest — he now had something to defend, and defending it meant maintaining
order in his district, keeping a body of retainers, and cooperating with the
governor who had made the grant and might make another. In the absence of a
salaried bureaucracy or a standing army, this was the available method of
producing loyal local authority, and it is roughly what medieval European
monarchs had done for the same reason, as an earlier chapter described.

The men who received these grants became the Californio elite: a small number of
families holding very large properties, intermarried, running cattle for the
hide and tallow trade, and exercising the practical functions of government in
their districts. Fifty thousand acres was not an unusual holding. Some were much
larger.

This is the frontier oligarchy, and the phrase should be understood as a
description rather than an accusation. Nothing else was on offer. A government
that cannot enforce will be replaced by whoever can, and the people who can are
the people with land, cattle, and armed retainers.

\newpage



## Rancho

1834–1839. Secularisation and Rancho San Francisquito.

The decrees said the land would go to the Indians.

Mexican secularisation legislation of the early 1830s provided that the mission
estates would be broken up and that a portion — in some formulations half —
would be distributed to the neophyte communities that had worked them, with the
remainder passing to the state. The Franciscans would be reduced to parish
clergy. The mission holdings, which comprised most of the productive coastal
land in California, would be returned to the people who had been compelled to
develop them.

Almost none of it happened. Within a decade the mission lands were in private
hands, the neophyte communities held essentially nothing, and the labour force
was working the same ground for new owners.

Putting the promise beside the outcome is the whole chapter, and the gap between
them establishes a pattern the story will document for the next two centuries:
inclusion announced, exclusion delivered, with a paper trail showing that
somebody meant it at the time.

The last private transaction in that sequence is still on the campus map. Peter
Coutts Road, Frenchman's Road, French Lake Drive, and Ayrshire Farm Lane record
Jean-Baptiste Paulin Caperon — "Peter Coutts," a French banker and self-described
socialist who assembled the Matadero Rancho stock farm in 1875 and sold it to
Leland Stanford in 1881 for $140,000. The ranchos became one estate. The estate
became a university that was forbidden to sell the land. The street names are
the receipt.

## Why it was done

The motives were real and the chapter gives them fairly.

The missions were an anomaly in a liberal republic: a clerical corporation
holding vast estates and controlling a subject labour force, exempt from
ordinary civil authority, loyal to a religious order many of whose members had
opposed independence and remained Spanish nationals. Mexican liberals objected
to this on principle and had been objecting for a decade.

There was also a fiscal argument and a settlement argument. The territory
generated almost no revenue. Land in private hands would be taxed, developed,
and defended by owners with an interest in defending it, and settlement was the
only real answer to the strategic problem of a coastline that Russia, Britain,
and increasingly the United States were eyeing.

None of this is pretextual. The mission system was indefensible on the terms the
Mexican republic professed, and dismantling it was a coherent policy.

## What actually happened

Administration was placed in the hands of commissioners appointed by the
governor, who were to inventory the estates, distribute the allotments, and
manage the remainder.

The commissioners were, very often, the same men who stood to receive the land.
The neophytes, who were not literate in Spanish, had no experience of individual
title, no capital, no legal representation, and no way to pay the taxes and
debts attached to the allotments they received. Those who did receive land
generally lost it quickly — sold under pressure, seized for debt, or simply
occupied by a neighbour with more retainers.

Mission livestock was slaughtered in enormous numbers for immediate hide and
tallow sales during the transition. Vineyards and orchards went untended.
Buildings were stripped. The physical capital that six decades of coerced labour
had accumulated was substantially destroyed within a few years, which suited
nobody's stated intentions and served the interests of whoever could convert an
asset to cash fastest.

And the land moved. The pace of private grants accelerated sharply through the
late 1830s and 1840s, and the recipients were the Californio families of the
previous chapter and the naturalised foreign merchants who had married into
them. By 1846 several hundred ranchos held most of the coastal land in
California.

## The rancho economy

What emerged was an economy of extraordinary simplicity. Cattle grazed on
enormous unfenced properties. Once a year they were slaughtered for hides and
tallow, which were traded to visiting ships for manufactured goods. There was
little agriculture beyond subsistence, almost no manufacturing, and no currency
worth the name.

It was also, for the families who held the land, a comfortable and in some
respects admirable society, and the chapter should not caricature it. Californio
ranch culture was hospitable, skilled, and genuinely attached to the place.

It ran on Indigenous labour. Former neophytes and other Indigenous Californians
worked the ranchos as vaqueros, field hands, and domestic servants, under
arrangements that ranged from waged employment to debt peonage to something
closely resembling the mission system with a private owner in place of a priest.
Mexican-era vagrancy provisions permitted the arrest of unemployed Indians and
their assignment to labour, which is a method the American state of
California will adopt and extend after 1850.

The legal fiction had changed. The arrangement had not.

## What the ranchos became

It is worth following the properties forward, because the boundaries drawn in
the 1830s are still visible on a map of California today.

Rancho boundaries became city boundaries. The grants were surveyed, contested,
confirmed, subdivided, and sold, but the original lines frequently survived the
process as property lines, and property lines become streets, and streets become
municipal limits. A great many towns in coastal California occupy the footprint
of a single Mexican-era grant and are named after it — the Ranchos San Antonio,
Las Pulgas, San Francisquito, and dozens of others persist in the names of
neighbourhoods, roads, parks, and shopping centres.

One of them matters more than the rest for this book. The land that became the
Stanford campus, and the towns of Palo Alto and Menlo Park around it, was
assembled in the 1870s from parcels that had been part of Mexican-era grants on
the San Francisco Peninsula, including Rancho San Francisquito. The eight
thousand acres a railroad baron will give to a university in 1885 were laid out
as cattle country by a Mexican governor's signature fifty years earlier.

This is not mysticism about land. It is an observation about how property
persists. A boundary, once recorded, tends to survive every subsequent change of
sovereignty, currency, language, and land use, because each new owner acquires
the previous owner's description and it is cheaper to keep it than to redraw it.
The mission road became the Peninsula's main street; the rancho lines became the
Peninsula's parcels; and the parcels became a research park.

## The second dispossession

The people the decrees named received nothing.

That sentence should be allowed to stand without elaboration, because the
documents are explicit. The legislation specified allotments for the neophytes.
Officials in Mexico City intended it. The provisions existed, were published,
and were not carried out, and the men who did not carry them out ended up with
the land.

It is the second dispossession of the same population within seventy years:
first of their territory by the mission system, then of the restitution the
dismantling of that system had promised. And it will be followed by a third,
after 1848, under a new flag and a different legal theory, which the next
chapters describe.

## Sutter

One arrival deserves his own section, because he built the closest thing Mexican
California had to a private state and because the gold will be found on his
property.

John Sutter was a Swiss émigré who reached California in 1839 having left debts
behind him in several countries. He obtained Mexican citizenship, and with it a
grant of some fifty thousand acres at the confluence of the Sacramento and
American rivers — well inland, away from the coastal ranchos, in territory the
Mexican government did not control in any sense.

What he built there he called New Helvetia. It had an adobe fort with walls and
cannon, workshops, a tannery, a distillery, agriculture, and a workforce drawn
from local Indigenous communities under arrangements that contemporaries
described in terms ranging from paternalist employment to something much closer
to bondage. He issued his own scrip. He maintained an armed force. He conducted
what amounted to a foreign policy, corresponding with Mexican officials,
American officials, and the Russians at Fort Ross, from whom he bought the
assets of the abandoned colony in 1841.

Sutter's Fort was the destination for American overland migrants coming down
from the Sierra, and by the mid-1840s that traffic was substantial and growing.
He sheltered them, employed them, and outfitted them, which made him
simultaneously a Mexican official and the principal facilitator of the American
settlement that would end Mexican California.

The instructive thing about Sutter is not his ambition but what his position
reveals. A single foreigner with a land grant, a fort, and a private workforce
was one of the significant powers in a Mexican territory, and the government
that had granted him the land had no means of restraining him. That is what thin
sovereignty means in practice.

He will be ruined within three years of the gold discovery, overrun by squatters
on land he could not defend and could not get the courts to protect. The man who
built the largest private establishment in Mexican California ended it as a
claimant, petitioning legislatures for relief.

## Who the arrangement failed

For Indigenous Californians, independence altered the paperwork and very little
else.

Mexican law after 1821 extended citizenship in principle, and the mission system
was formally understood as a temporary arrangement that would end with the
neophytes receiving the land. In practice the missions continued to operate as
labour systems for another decade, and when they were dismantled the labour
force was transferred rather than freed, as the next chapter describes. Outside
the mission districts, Indigenous communities that had maintained autonomy came
under increasing pressure from expanding ranchos.

The legal category changed. The obligation to work somebody else's land did not.

## What is now in place

By 1834 California has a landholding class with very large properties, local
power, and titles issued by a distant government that can neither survey them
nor revoke them. It has a resident foreign merchant class with capital and
connections. It has an Indigenous population still supplying most of the labour
and holding none of the title. And it has an administrative apparatus so thin
that the principal instrument of government is the gift of land.

That is a stacked deck waiting for a dealer, and the largest transfer of
property in California's history is about to run straight through it.

## The merchants

The other significant development of the period is the arrival of foreigners,
and they arrived as businessmen rather than as conquerors.

The hide and tallow trade drew New England and British ships to the California
coast from the 1820s. Cattle hides — "California banknotes," in the traders'
phrase — were the territory's only substantial export, and the trade required
agents resident on shore to accumulate cargo between voyages. Those agents
stayed. They learned Spanish, converted to Catholicism, became Mexican citizens,
married into Californio families, and received land grants of their own.

Richard Henry Dana, who sailed the coast as a common sailor in 1834 and
published his account six years later, described the arrangement with the
specificity of someone who had loaded the cargo himself. His book was widely
read in the United States, which meant that by the 1840s Americans had a
reasonably accurate picture of California as a place with an excellent harbour,
negligible defences, and a government unable to control it.

The merchants are not villains in this chapter, and several of them were loyal
Mexican citizens who behaved decently in 1846. But they constituted, by the
mid-1840s, a resident foreign commercial class with local knowledge, local
marriages, local property, and connections to a rapidly expanding country three
thousand miles away that had already annexed Texas.

\newpage



## Nine Days

January–February 1848. Coloma and Guadalupe Hidalgo.

The Treaty of Guadalupe Hidalgo was signed on 2 February 1848, transferring California to the United States for fifteen million dollars.

Gold had been found at Sutter's Mill nine days earlier.

Nobody in Mexico City knew. Nobody in Washington knew. Word reached the eastern United States months later and was not widely believed until December, when the president confirmed it in a message to Congress and the rush began in earnest. For those nine days, and for a good while afterwards, the most valuable territory on the continent changed hands at a price set in complete ignorance of what it contained.

The coincidence is the best-known fact in California history and it is usually told as irony. It is more useful as an argument about what the treaty actually accomplished, because the gold was in the ground either way. What changed on 2 February was not the value of California. It was the legal system that would determine who owned it.

## The war, briefly

The fighting in California was small and confused, and this chapter treats it as
such.

American settlers around Sonoma declared a republic in June 1846 and raised a
flag with a bear on it, an episode that lasted under a month and was overtaken
by the arrival of United States naval forces. There was resistance in the south,
notably at San Pasqual, where Californio lancers handled American dragoons
roughly. The territory was secured by January 1847 under a capitulation
negotiated at Cahuenga.

The war was decided in Mexico, not in California. What California contributed
was the reason for it: the harbour at San Francisco had been an explicit
American objective for years, and the administration in Washington had attempted
to buy it before deciding to take it.

## What the treaty did

The instrument that mattered ran to a few thousand words and did three things.

It transferred sovereignty over an enormous territory — California, Nevada,
Utah, and parts of several other future states — in exchange for fifteen million
dollars and the assumption of certain claims. It established the boundary. And
it addressed the property and citizenship of the Mexican nationals who lived
there.

That third element is the one this chapter turns on, because it is where a
military occupation becomes a system of ownership. Conquest by itself produces
control. It does not produce title, and without title there is nothing to buy,
sell, mortgage, or develop. Capital cannot operate in a territory where
ownership is a matter of who is standing on the land.

The fifteen million dollars purchased a legal framework. That is the actual
acquisition, and it is the reason California became investable in 1848 rather
than merely occupied.

## Article X

The treaty as negotiated contained an article protecting Mexican land grants in
the ceded territories. The United States Senate struck it before ratification.

What replaced it was a diplomatic statement of assurance — the Protocol of
Querétaro — indicating that grant-holders retained their rights and could assert
them in American courts. The distinction between a treaty article and an
assurance about access to litigation looks technical. It was the whole game.

Because in 1851 Congress passed the California Land Act, which established a
commission before which every holder of a Spanish or Mexican grant had to appear
and prove title. The burden of proof was on the claimant. The proceedings were
conducted in English under American rules of evidence. Decisions could be
appealed to the federal district court and then to the Supreme Court, and they
routinely were, by whichever party lost.

Consider what a Californio family had to produce. A grant document from a
government that no longer existed. A *diseño* sketching boundaries by reference
to an oak tree and a creek. Evidence of continuous occupation. Witnesses. And a
lawyer, for a process that took, on average, many years — in a number of cases
more than a decade.

## The method

The outcome was a transfer of land accomplished procedurally.

Most claims were eventually confirmed. This is important and is regularly left
out: the commission was not a rubber stamp for dispossession, and the majority
of the acreage claimed was upheld. But confirmation took years, and years of
litigation had to be paid for, and the way a cattle-rich, cash-poor rancho
family paid for it was by selling or mortgaging the land at whatever price was
available during the period of uncertainty. Attorneys were frequently paid in
acreage. Squatters occupied the disputed land while the case ran, and by the
time title was confirmed there were people living on it who had improvements, a
claim in equity, and votes in a state legislature increasingly hostile to large
grants.

Families that won were ruined by winning. That is a more durable method of
transfer than confiscation and a much harder one to protest, because at every
stage it is a legal process with an appeal available.

The land ended up with American purchasers, lawyers, banks, and squatters.
Within a generation, Californio landholding in the north had largely collapsed.

## What fifteen million dollars bought

The sum deserves a moment, because it is the price at which the largest transfer
of territory in North American history was settled.

Fifteen million dollars, plus the assumption of some three and a quarter million
in claims by American citizens against Mexico, for a cession of roughly half a
million square miles. It was not a trivial amount of money in 1848 — it was
comparable to the Louisiana Purchase four decades earlier, and rather more than
the federal government's ordinary annual revenue at the time.

It was also, within eighteen months, less than the value of the gold coming out
of a single year's production in the territory it had purchased.

What the
United States bought was not a quantity of gold it did not know about. It was
jurisdiction: the authority to determine, through its own courts and its own
statutes, who owned what. Everything extracted from California over the
following century — the gold, the land, the timber, the water rights, the
railroad grants — was allocated under a legal system that the treaty installed.

Jurisdiction is the most valuable thing a state can acquire, and it is almost
never priced correctly, because its value depends entirely on what is done with
it afterwards.

## Citizenship

The treaty also addressed the people. Mexican nationals in the ceded territory
could retain Mexican citizenship by declaring so within a year, or become United
States citizens by default. Most became Americans, and Californios participated
in the constitutional convention the following year and in the early state
legislature; several were substantial political figures.

Within a generation that participation had largely ended, and the method was
the one described above rather than any formal exclusion. A landholding class
whose land is consumed by litigation stops being a political class. The
Californio delegates who helped write California's first constitution watched
their families' holdings pass to the lawyers who had represented them.

The treaty promised protection of property and delivered access to courts. In a
contest between a written guarantee and a procedure, the procedure wins.

## Indigenous title

Neither sovereign recognised it, before or after.

Mexican law had extended citizenship in principle without recognising Indigenous
land title in practice. American law did not extend either. Eighteen treaties
negotiated with California tribes in 1851 and 1852, which would have reserved
substantial territory, were sent to the Senate and never ratified; they were
placed under an injunction of secrecy for half a century. The tribes had ceded
their claims in the belief that reservations would follow. They did not.

There is no procedural complexity to describe here and no litigation to trace.
The claims were simply not recognised.

The fighting in California lasted seven months and changed very little. The paperwork lasted forty years and changed everything: who owned the coast, who owned the interior, who could borrow against it, and who was left holding a sketch map and a lawyer's bill.

The legal framework is now in place. The gold has been in the river for two weeks. And the largest voluntary migration in the history of the hemisphere is about to arrive in a territory where the land is already owned, the title is already contested, and the courts are three thousand miles away.

## After the treaty

Sam Brannan bought the shovels first.

Brannan was a Mormon elder, newspaper publisher, and storekeeper who had a
general store at Sutter's Fort and another in San Francisco. When word reached
him in the spring of 1848 that gold had been found on the American River, he
went and looked, confirmed it, and then went quietly around the region buying
every pick, pan, shovel, and length of canvas he could find.

Then he walked through the streets of San Francisco holding up a bottle of gold
dust and shouting.

The order of operations is the pattern in one anecdote. Brannan did not find
gold, did not mine gold, and by most accounts never seriously tried. He
identified the bottleneck between the deposit and the people who wanted it,
bought the bottleneck, and then created the demand. He is said to have become
California's first millionaire, and he did it selling implements at prices that
would have been indefensible in any market where a competitor could have arrived
in time.

## What happened to the men at the centre of the story

James Marshall found the gold and got nothing. He tried mining, tried lecturing,
tried a vineyard, and died poor in 1885 in a cabin in Kelsey. The State of
California voted him a small pension for a few years and then discontinued it.

John Sutter, on whose land the discovery was made and whose private establishment at New Helvetia had been the valley's only real enterprise, was destroyed by it within three
years. His workforce left for the diggings. Squatters occupied his land, killed
his cattle, and could not be removed, because the courts were overwhelmed, the
title was Mexican-era and contested, and the squatters had votes. His claim was
ultimately rejected by the Supreme Court in 1858. He spent the rest of his life
petitioning legislatures for compensation and died in a Washington hotel still
petitioning.

The discoverer and the landowner both ended as claimants. The man who bought the
shovels ended as a millionaire. That is not a moral about luck. It is a
statement about position.

\newpage

## Notes to Episode 1

**The Tree**

- …a university town named for the stick itself: Harris 2023, intro.
- …paper that had never been occupied in force: Harris 2023, intro.


\newpage



# Episode 2: The Toll

## Done

10 May 1869. Promontory Summit, Utah Territory.

The air was thin and dry. Two locomotives sat on one track, pilots almost touching, boilers cooling, men climbing over wheels and couplings with the restless energy of a job that had been years in the doing. Someone had brought champagne. Someone had brought a silver mallet and a spike plated for the cameras.

The golden spike was not the completion of a public work in the sense the speeches would claim. It was the ceremony at which a public work became a private monopoly on the only rail connection between California and the rest of the country.

Leland Stanford, president of the Central Pacific, stepped up to drive the last spike. He swung and missed, striking only the rail. It made no difference. The telegraph operator closed the circuit and the wire went out, “done!”.



The nation heard bells. Church towers on the Atlantic coast rang before the sound of the hammer had finished travelling through the wire. Editors treated the moment as the closing of a continental wound: the Pacific and the Mississippi bound at last. What the bells did not carry was the accounting underneath the ceremony.

There is a famous photograph taken that afternoon. Two locomotives face each other, close enough to touch. Men crowd the pilots and the boilers, holding bottles. Two engineers lean out to shake hands.

The men who built the western half of the line are almost entirely absent from it.



Somewhere between ten and twelve thousand Chinese workers had been employed by the Central Pacific at the peak of construction, constituting the large majority of its workforce. They laid the track over the Sierra Nevada, drove the tunnels through the summit granite, and worked through winters in which snow buried the grade and men lived and worked in tunnels dug through the drifts. At Promontory they are essentially not in the picture.

That absence is not a photographic accident. It is a statement about who the ceremony was for. The golden spike was not the completion of a public work in the sense the speeches implied. It was the moment a line financed with federal land and federal bonds became a private toll road on the only rail connection between California and the rest of the United States.

Collis P. Huntington was not in the crowd. He was on the Atlantic side of the country, where he had spent the war years doing what he did best: purchasing, lobbying, and keeping the books of the enterprise at a distance from the men who swung hammers in the snow. Mark Hopkins stayed with the figures. Charles Crocker had run the construction. Stanford provided the face.

The photograph would travel farther than any payroll ledger. It would become the image Americans used when they wanted to believe the continent had been united by effort and vision. The payroll ledgers, where they survived at all, told a different story about whose effort had been bought cheapest.

Nobody on the platform that afternoon needed to say any of that aloud. The spike did not have to connect for the wire to report success.

The telegraph operator did not wait for a second swing. The circuit closed. Editors from Sacramento to New York received the same monosyllable and printed it as destiny. Men who had never seen a tunnel face or an isthmus mud hole cheered in streets that would, within a generation, argue about whether the railroad charged them fairly for the privilege of reaching those streets at all.

That delay—between the missed blow and the word “done”—is the episode in miniature. The ceremony could fail in fact and still succeed in wire. The toll would be collected anyway.

---

Twenty years earlier, the corridor still bled.

## Panama

1849. The Isthmus of Panama.

Collis P. Huntington was not yet a railroad man. He was a merchant trying to move goods and himself between the Atlantic economy and a California that had begun, in the previous year, to pull the world toward it like a magnet. The fastest route was not around Cape Horn. It was through the isthmus: by steamer to the Caribbean shore, then overland through mud and fever to a Pacific port, then by steamer again to San Francisco.

The isthmus was a toll booth nature had built in the wrong place, and men were already charging to pass through it.

“Death is carrying off the Americans most fearfully,” Huntington wrote to his brother. “There are eight to ten deaths a day.”.



The letter is dated to the year the gold rush turned California from a distant province into a destination. San Francisco had numbered its residents in hundreds at the start of 1848. Within two years it held tens of thousands, and the territory’s non-Indigenous population would climb toward two hundred thousand within four. Every one of those arrivals had to eat, sleep, and pay someone for the privilege of continuing the journey.

Huntington was stuck in that arithmetic.

The crossing was not a single ordeal. It was a chain of small sovereignties. Pack animals on the jungle road. Boatmen on the rivers. Agents who controlled space in a shed where a man could lie down if he paid. Hospitals that filled because drinking water and dysentery travelled together. The isthmus did not care whether a passenger was headed to dig gold or to sell shovels. It taxed bodies at the same rate.

Huntington’s letters home from this period are the earliest portrait in the record of a man who would later be accused of buying legislatures and hiding construction accounts. Here he is simply another American watching other Americans die in a hurry to reach a strike that was already famous before half the arrivals knew how to use a pan. The gold was the story. The passage was the business.

That distinction would outlive the isthmus.

On the Pacific side, ships piled up in the bay. Crews abandoned vessels to join the rush. The harbour became a throat: everything and everyone passed through it, and whoever owned the ground at the water’s edge collected. Huntington was learning the same arithmetic in Panama that Sam Brannan would act out in San Francisco the following season—buy the passage, not the metal.



Brannan would walk the streets holding a bottle of gold dust and shouting after he had bought the picks and pans first. Huntington, writing to his brother about eight to ten deaths a day, was measuring a different inventory: the cost of being in the right corridor when demand became a flood.

The arithmetic of mining, once the placers were crowded, was wage-like for most men and ruinous for many. The arithmetic of supply was not. Merchants, shippers, landlords, and bankers captured the surplus while correspondents wrote home about nuggets. The reliable money was not in the American River. It was in the harbor, the store, and the route between them.

Huntington survived the isthmus and went on to Sacramento, where he and his partners sold hardware and dry goods to miners who still believed they were the protagonists of the story. Twelve years later those same merchants would apply the corridor logic to rails, and the federal government would pay them in land and bonds to do it.

But in 1849 the railroad was not yet in view. There was only the mud, the waiting, the dead, and a merchant writing home that the Americans were being carried off fearfully while California filled with people who had not yet arrived.

The gold rush made California the destination. Panama made the journey the business. Huntington learned on the isthmus what Brannan would prove in San Francisco and what the Central Pacific would institutionalize at Promontory: whoever controls the passage sets the price, and the passage outlives the strike.

The bells at Promontory had not rung yet. The deaths in Panama were already counting toward who would own the line when they did.

## The harbor they were racing toward

While Huntington waited on the isthmus, San Francisco was turning the bay into a ledger.

Ships arriving from the Pacific side found a waterfront that could not keep pace with the people walking off the gangplanks. Crews deserted to the diggings. Vessels sat abandoned until their hulls became lumber, fill, and eventually lots sold to men who had never sailed them. Eggs, flour, and lumber reached prices correspondents described with exclamation marks. A city was being built by people who did not plan to stay, using materials that had to come around Cape Horn or across Panama.

The arithmetic of mining, once placers crowded, looked like wages for most men and ruin for many. The arithmetic of supply did not. Merchants, shippers, landlords, and bankers held the positions between the miner and the metal. Levi Strauss arrived in 1853 as a dry-goods wholesaler; the trousers came later. The brand was built on the throat of the trade, not the pan in the creek.

Huntington was not yet part of that Sacramento quartet. He was still a body in a line on the isthmus, watching Americans die at a rate he could put in a letter. But the letter itself was evidence of a habit that would define the Associates: keep the account, send it to someone who can act on it, and treat the corridor as the asset.

When he reached California he sold hardware with Mark Hopkins. Charles Crocker sold dry goods. Leland Stanford sold groceries. They were not engineers. They were men who understood that California’s second fortune—after the initial shock of gold—would belong to whoever owned the passage between the East and the diggings, and then whoever owned the passage east of Sacramento altogether.

The isthmus and the summit were the same problem at different scales. Mud and fever in 1849. Granite and snow in 1867. In both places the men who owned the corridor wrote the letters that survived.

## What the photograph would not show

Promontory would fix an image of completion: faces on locomotives, hands clasped, a spike driven for the wire. The photograph would not show Panama in 1849 or the Summit Tunnel in the winter of 1866. It would not show Chinese workers walking out of tunnel mouths in 1867 or Crocker’s commissary closing behind them.

It would not show Collis Huntington in Washington, years later, worrying that investigators would ask to see the Contract and Finance Company books.

The ceremony was the visible end. The toll began long before, in a corridor where eight to ten Americans died each day while a merchant wrote home to his brother.

The pattern was already visible in mud before it was laid in rail: the money is not in the strike. The money is in the passage.

## Huntington’s habit

Years after Panama, when the line was financed and the fights moved to Washington, Huntington would run the eastern end of the Associates’ enterprise the way he had survived the isthmus: by controlling the corridor between need and supply. He purchased. He lobbied. He wrote letters that kept the account straight while other men kept the grade straight through snow.

He was not at Promontory. He did not need to be. The photograph was for the public story. Huntington’s story was in invoices, bond issues, and the fear—recorded later—that a committee might ask to see the Contract and Finance Company books.

Between the 1849 letter about eight to ten deaths a day and the 1869 wire reporting “done!” lies a single continuity. California was reached by men who thought they were chasing gold. It was owned by men who owned the way in.

The isthmus taught Huntington the first half. The railroad would teach the country the second.

## Waiting for the Pacific side

The crossing in 1849 was not a single day. It was a queue.

Men waited for mules that were already overloaded. They waited for canoes when the trail turned to river. They waited in sheds for a steamer that would not leave until it had enough passengers to justify the fuel. They paid at each handoff. They watched the sick moved aside and the dead buried without ceremony in ground they would never see again.

Huntington’s letter about eight to ten deaths a day is a snapshot of that queue. It does not describe every hour, but it describes the rate at which hope converted into corpses among men who believed California was waiting with gold in easy reach.

The Pacific side was not mercy. San Francisco’s prices punished newcomers as thoroughly as the isthmus punished travellers. The Foreign Miners’ Tax and its successors would later target men who had survived both crossings only to be defined as outsiders at the diggings. The passage extracted from the body at the isthmus; the state would learn to extract from the wallet at the mines.

Huntington survived both gates. He would help build a third—the railroad gate—where the federal government paid per mile and the Associates decided who could afford to move grain, lumber, and people east.

When Stanford missed the spike in 1869, Huntington was already thinking like a man who had watched Americans die in line. The line on the map was finally continuous. The lines in the ledgers had been continuous for years.

## Brannan’s order of operations

Sam Brannan would confirm the corridor arithmetic in San Francisco the season after Huntington’s isthmus letters. Brannan bought picks, pans, shovels, and canvas, then walked the streets holding gold dust and shouting. He did not need to mine. He needed the crowd to arrive after he owned the implements.



Huntington and Brannan never partnered, but they shared a premise: the discoverer and the landowner could end as claimants while the man who owned the passage ended rich. James Marshall and John Sutter would prove that half of the premise in the years after Coloma. The Associates would prove the other half on rails.

Panama in 1849 was the first toll booth on the route east to west. Promontory in 1869 was the toll booth west to east. Between them, a generation learned to call it progress.

The cold open returns to where it began: bells, a missed swing, a wire that closed anyway. The reader already knows what the crowd on the platform did not—that the men who built the western half of the line are missing from the frame, that the books are already elsewhere, that the isthmus letter was the first entry in a ledger that would outlive every spike.

\newpage



## The Store

1852–1856. Sacramento.

The scale of the shock

San Francisco had somewhere under a thousand residents at the beginning of 1848.
Within two years it had more than twenty-five thousand, and California's non-
Indigenous population went from a few thousand to well over two hundred thousand
within four years. Ships arriving in the bay were abandoned by their crews, who
left for the diggings; at one point several hundred vessels sat derelict in the
harbour. Some were broken up for timber. Some were run aground deliberately,
filled in around, and built on — there are ships under the financial district,
found periodically during excavation.

Prices behaved accordingly. Eggs, flour, lumber, and labour reached levels that
correspondents wrote home about in disbelief. A city was being built by people
who had no intention of staying, using materials that had to come around Cape
Horn.

## Who captured the surplus

The arithmetic of mining is the part the mythology omits.

Placer gold — the loose material in streambeds that a man with a pan could
recover — was accessible for a few years and then substantially exhausted.
During that window the average return was, by most reconstructions, roughly
comparable to wages available elsewhere, with enormous variance: a small number
of men did extremely well, most made an ordinary living for hard work in bad
conditions, and many lost money on the cost of getting there. After the placers
played out, mining required capital — hydraulic operations, stamp mills, and
eventually deep shafts — and became an industry employing wage labour rather
than an opportunity for individuals.

The reliable money was in supply. Merchants selling equipment and provisions.
Shippers controlling the passage from the eastern United States. Landlords in
San Francisco, where a lot on the waterfront appreciated faster than any claim
in the Sierra. Bankers, who held gold dust, issued notes against it, and
financed the merchants. Levi Strauss arrived in 1853 and sold dry goods; the
trousers came later and the business was a wholesale supply house long before it
was a brand.

Every one of these positions is a passage. The harbour was the only realistic
way into northern California, so everything and everybody passed through San
Francisco, and whoever owned the ground they passed over collected.

The extractive story — the miner, the pan, the strike — obscures the
infrastructural reality, and the obscuring is part of the method. A myth of
luck explains the winners as fortunate rather than as positioned, which is a
considerably more comfortable account for the winners.

## The state joins in

The Foreign Miners' Tax of 1850 imposed a monthly licence fee on miners who were
not United States citizens. It was aimed principally at Mexican, Chilean, and
Peruvian miners, many of whom had arrived early and knew more about placer
mining than the Americans did. It was widely enforced by threat, produced
substantial revenue for the state, and was repealed after the foreign miners
largely left, which had been the object.

A second version in 1852 was aimed at the Chinese and was more durable,
eventually supplying a significant share of state revenue. It is the first
appearance in California of a manoeuvre that will recur on the sandlots of the 1870s and again in the twenty-first century: extract from a group
defined as outsiders, in a way that a majority of voters will support, while the
actual concentration of wealth continues undisturbed.

## The catastrophe

The Gold Rush was, for the Indigenous population of California, the most
destructive event in its history, and this is not a matter of interpretation.

Miners moved into the foothills and the interior valleys in enormous numbers,
displacing communities, destroying fisheries with hydraulic sediment, and taking
land. The violence was organised. State and local authorities paid bounties and
funded militia expeditions against Indigenous communities, and the California
legislature appropriated money for the purpose; the federal government later
reimbursed a substantial portion of it. An 1850 state statute, titled an act for
the government and protection of Indians, permitted the arrest of Indians deemed
to be loitering and their assignment to labour, and permitted the indenture of
Indigenous children — a system of forced labour operating in a free state.

The Indigenous population of California declined by a very large proportion
during the 1850s and 1860s. Scholarly estimates of the pre-contact population
and of the decline vary considerably, and the range should be stated rather than
collapsed, but no serious account puts the collapse at less than catastrophic,
and California's first governor said publicly in 1851 that a war of
extermination would continue until the Indian race became extinct.

The eighteen treaties negotiated with California tribes in 1851 and 1852, which
would have reserved substantial land, were never ratified by the Senate. The
tribes had already ceded their claims.

## What California took away

California took away something in these years that it has never unlearned, and
Brannan is its clearest exponent.

The money is not in the commodity. The money is in the position between the
commodity and the person who wants it — the harbour, the store, the bank, the
only road in. Positions of that kind do not require luck, do not require skill
at the underlying activity, and do not exhaust when the placers do.

Twelve years later, four Sacramento merchants who had made ordinary money
selling supplies to miners applied the same insight to a railroad, and the
federal government paid for it.

## Four merchants

Leland Stanford sold groceries. Collis Huntington and Mark Hopkins ran a
hardware business together. Charles Crocker sold dry goods. All four were in
Sacramento, all four had made ordinary merchant money supplying the mining
economy, and none of them was an engineer.

They were, in other words, Brannan's shovel-selling operating at a
different scale, and they understood the same thing Brannan had understood: the
money is in the position, not the commodity.

\newpage



## Vigilance

1856. San Francisco.

The artifact is a single paragraph of small type on the front page of the
*Daily Evening Bulletin* for the fourteenth of May, 1856.

The paper was seven months old and already the most-read sheet in the West. Its
editor, James King of William — the suffix was his own, adopted to separate
himself from the other James Kings of his generation — had built its circulation
on one subject: the men who ran San Francisco, and how they had come to run it.
That afternoon the *Bulletin* printed a few lines observing that James P. Casey,
lately seated on the city's Board of Supervisors, had once been an inmate of
Sing Sing.

That is the whole artifact. A back-page fact about a New York prison, set in a
California paper, in a column about municipal fraud. Everything in this chapter
follows from those two sentences and from what the city decided to do with them.

## What the paragraph said

Stand where a reader stood and ask the question the reader asked: what was Casey
actually accused of?

Two things, and the *Bulletin* was careful to keep them apart. The first was the
prison term — a matter of record in New York, no crime in California, and the
paper said as much. A man who had served his sentence had served it. The second
was the ballot box. Casey held a seat on the Board of Supervisors from a
district in which, the paper charged, he had not even stood on the ticket; he
had counted himself in. The prison term was not the accusation. It was the
character reference attached to the accusation, which was that a convicted felon
had let himself into the government of San Francisco through a false return and
nobody had stopped him.

Picture the record itself, the thing the sentence only gestured at: a New York
commitment, a name entered in the register at Sing Sing, a term served and
closed. It would have sat in an Ossining ledger two thousand miles away,
unreachable and therefore unanswerable — which is exactly why printing that it
existed was an act of such precision. King was not producing the document. He
was announcing that he knew what it said.

## Five o'clock

Casey read the paper in the *Bulletin* office. He went to the Board of
Supervisors, then to the offices of a rival paper, and then he waited on the
street.

At about five o'clock King left the *Bulletin* and walked up Montgomery Street
toward Washington, wrapped against the evening in a short cloak. Casey stepped
out near the Pacific Express building, called to him to draw and defend himself,
and fired once with a Navy revolver. The ball went in above the collarbone. King
turned, took a few steps, and was carried into the Express office, where they
laid him on a counter and sent for surgeons.

He did not die there. He lingered six days, in a room above Montgomery Street,
while the city outside organised — and the lingering mattered, because it gave
the reaction the time it needed to grow to the size of a funeral.

## The street

Reconstruct the next hour from what the crowd did, because here the crowd is a
primary source as much as the paper is.

Word moved the way word moved in a compact city of tens of thousands: by voice,
outward from the Express office, along Montgomery, up to Portsmouth Square. Men
left counting-houses and stood in the street. By the time Casey had been walked
to the county jail there was a crowd around it calling for him, and the sheriff
moved him to the stronger jail on Broadway ahead of it. The *Bulletin*'s own
circulation — several thousand copies a day, read aloud in bar-rooms and passed
hand to hand — meant the paragraph and the shooting reached the same reader
within hours of each other, the accusation and its answer arriving as a single
event.

What the city did with it was not a riot. Within a day the men who had led the
short-lived Committee of Vigilance of 1851 had reconstituted it, and this time
they built something meant to last the summer.

## Fort Gunnybags

They took a commercial building on Sacramento Street, stacked gunny sacks filled
with sand against its front until the wall stood two storeys of packed earth,
mounted a cannon above the door, and enrolled members by the thousand — somewhere
between six and eight thousand men, organised into companies and drilled like an
army. The city called the building Fort Gunnybags. From it the Committee ran
arrests, held trials, and kept a membership roll and an executive, in open
defiance of a state government that declared the county in insurrection and could
not raise a force against it. William Tecumseh Sherman, then a San Francisco
banker, took a commission to muster the militia, could not obtain arms, and
resigned.

The Committee took Casey from the sheriff. It also took Charles Cora, a gambler
whose trial for killing a United States marshal the previous winter had ended in
a hung jury. It tried them both inside the fort.

On the twenty-second of May — the day of James King of William's funeral — the
two men were brought onto a platform built out over the Sacramento Street wall.
The editor's cortège was moving through the city at the same hour, thousands in
the procession, the largest San Francisco had assembled for anyone. As the
funeral passed, the drops fell. Two bodies hung from beams projecting over the
street, in daylight, before a crowd, hanged by a private association of
merchants that had arrested, charged, tried, and executed them in the space of
eight days.

The Committee was not a mob, and that is the whole point of the chapter. It was
the city's commercial elite, acting as a parallel state and keeping minutes. When
it dissolved itself three months and two more hangings later, its members did not
scatter. They went into ordinary politics as the party of order, and San
Francisco remembered them for a century as the men who had saved it.

## The formal apparatus

The formal institutions had arrived first, and quickly.

California's constitutional convention met at Colton Hall in Monterey in
September and October of 1849 — forty-eight delegates, meeting on the upper
floor of a two-storey stone building that is still standing at 570 Pacific
Street. They had no authorisation from Congress. Military government had been in
place since the occupation, the treaty had transferred sovereignty, and Congress
had failed to organise a territorial government because it could not agree about
slavery. So the delegates organised one themselves.

They produced a constitution in six weeks. It prohibited slavery, which was the
central decision and was reached with less argument than expected — partly on
principle, partly because free miners did not want to compete against enslaved
labour gangs. It established the eastern boundary. It recognised existing
property rights, which mattered enormously to the Californio delegates present.

Statehood followed on 9 September 1850, as part of the Compromise of 1850 —
California admitted as a free state, in exchange for a stronger fugitive slave
act and territorial organisation elsewhere on terms the South could accept. The
territory had gone from foreign soil to statehood in under three years, without
passing through the territorial stage that every other part of the Mexican
Cession endured for decades.

That speed is a fact about gold and about congressional arithmetic, and it puts
California on the sectional chessboard from its first day in the Union. Two
senators from a free state, in a chamber balanced on exactly that count.

## Why the institutions did not work

A state government existed on paper and had almost no capacity.

San Francisco in the early 1850s was a city of tens of thousands, most of them
transient, most of them men, most of them armed, in a place with no established
policing, an overwhelmed and corruptible court system, and a municipal
government that was both incompetent and demonstrably dishonest. The city burned
down repeatedly. Municipal debt and land scandals were continuous. Elections
were contested by organised groups who understood that whoever controlled the
count controlled the contracts.

Into that gap stepped whoever was organised enough to fill it, which is the
finding of this chapter and the reason it sits where it does in the act.

## The precedent, and the target

This was the second time the city's merchants had done it. The first Committee of
Vigilance had formed in June 1851, after a run of fires and robberies blamed on a
group of Australian arrivals known as the Sydney Ducks; it hanged four men,
deported others, and dissolved within months. So by 1856 the move was not an
improvisation. It was a remembered instrument, taken down off the shelf when it
was wanted.

And what it was wanted for is the part the civic-virtue version leaves out. The
1856 Committee's energy was aimed, with considerable precision, at the Irish and
Catholic immigrants of the city and at the Democratic organisation that turned
their votes into offices. Both men hanged during the King affair were Irish
Catholics. The Committee's own membership was overwhelmingly Protestant and
mercantile, much of it transplanted from New England, and the party that grew out
of that summer — running under the banner of reform — took the city's government
at the next election and held it. The ballot box that Casey was accused of
stuffing did change hands. It changed hands to the men who had built the fort.

It was, in other words, a factional instrument wearing the costume of civic
order, and it worked.

## The important detail

The important thing is not the violence. It is the respectability.

These men were not outlaws and were never treated as such. They were the city's
leading businessmen, they operated openly with published membership, they kept
records, and they emerged from the episode with enhanced standing rather than
criminal liability. Nobody was prosecuted. The Committee's leaders went on to
prominent careers, and the episode entered San Francisco's account of itself as
a moment when decent men restored order.

Private organisation substituted for public authority, and was honoured for it.

That is the premise the story will need repeatedly. It is the premise underlying
the Hoover Commission for Relief in Belgium in a later episode, which fed a country
without being a state. It is the premise underlying the university-contractor
arrangement, in which public functions are performed by private institutions
because the private institutions are better at them. It is the premise
underlying every platform that now performs a governmental function under terms
of service, and it is the reason those arrangements attract so little objection:
California decided in 1856 that competent private governance was preferable to
incompetent public governance, and it has never seriously revisited the
question.

## The water and the land

Two other decisions of these years determined more about California's future
than the hangings did, and both followed the same logic.

The first was water. California adopted, largely from mining practice, the
doctrine of prior appropriation: the first party to divert water to a beneficial
use acquires a right to continue doing so, superior to later claimants, whether
or not they own land adjoining the stream. This is not how water law worked in
the eastern states, where riparian rights attached to the land itself, and
California ended up with an awkward hybrid of both.

What prior appropriation does is convert a common resource into a form of
property allocated by speed. It rewarded whoever arrived first with capital
enough to build a diversion, which in practice meant hydraulic mining companies
and, later, large agricultural operators. The rule that would determine who
could farm the Central Valley was made by miners in the 1850s for reasons that
had nothing to do with farming.

The second was land, in the form of the state's disposal of the federal acreage
it received on admission. Substantial holdings passed in large blocks to a small
