number of buyers through methods that were loosely administered and
frequently abused, including the swampland grants, which allowed a purchaser to
acquire land at a nominal price by declaring it wet. Vast tracts of the Central
Valley were acquired this way.

By the 1870s a handful of individuals and companies held agricultural properties
of a scale unknown anywhere in the eastern United States. Henry George, writing
in California in 1871, made the concentration of land the centre of his argument
about poverty amid progress, and he was describing what he could see out of the
window.

Concentration preceded the market here as it had under the ranchos, and the
instruments were public.

## Who had no standing

The men hanged had no appeal, and two of them had colourable defences that a
court would have had to consider.

Beyond that, entire populations stood outside both the formal order and the
informal one. Chinese residents were subject to a statute, upheld by the state
supreme court in 1854, holding that Chinese witnesses could not testify against
a white defendant. Indigenous Californians were subject to the labour and
vagrancy provisions described in the previous chapter. Californio landholders
were in court defending titles the treaty had promised to protect.

The Committee of Vigilance is remembered as a story about order. It was a story
about who counted, and the answer was decided by the people who had the
sandbags.

\newpage



## The Surveyor

1860–1863.

In November 1860 Theodore Judah told the *Sacramento Union* he had "struck a lucky streak".



Theodore Judah brought them the project, incorporated the Central Pacific with
them in 1861, secured the legislation described in the previous chapter, and
then fell out with them over how the company would be financed and controlled.
He was bought out and travelled east to raise money to buy them out in turn. He
crossed Panama, contracted yellow fever, and died in New York in November 1863
at thirty-seven.

The four divided the work by temperament. Huntington handled Washington and the
purchasing. Crocker ran construction. Hopkins kept the books. Stanford handled
California politics, which he did by becoming the governor.

## Both chairs

Stanford was elected Governor of California in 1861 and served from January 1862
to December 1863 — precisely the period in which the Pacific Railway Act passed,
the Central Pacific was organised, and California committed public support to
the enterprise.

He was simultaneously president of the Central Pacific Railroad.

As governor he advocated the railroad as a public necessity, which it arguably
was. He signed legislation providing state and county assistance to it. He
presided over the groundbreaking in Sacramento in January 1863 in both
capacities, and nobody at the ceremony appears to have regarded the arrangement
as remarkable.

 This is worth dwelling on, because it is the cleanest illustration in the
book of a point the Voice Guide insists on. Nothing here is concealed. The
method does not depend on secrecy. It depends on a structural arrangement
being visible and unremarkable.

## The subsidy, and where it went

The public contribution was enormous. Land grants of alternate sections along
the right of way, doubled in 1864 to twenty sections per mile — which is to say
twenty square miles of land for every mile of track. Government bonds at six per
cent, issued per mile, graduated by terrain: sixteen thousand dollars a mile
across the flat, thirty-two thousand across the high plains, and forty-eight
thousand a mile in the mountains.

That last figure produced an immediate and characteristic manoeuvre. Where the
mountains began was a question of geology, and geology was a matter of expert
opinion, and the Central Pacific obtained an opinion placing the base of the
Sierra Nevada considerably further west than any geologist unconnected to the
company would have put it. Lincoln accepted it.

 The railroad — a corporation with public
obligations, public credit, and eventually public shareholders — paid the
construction firm, which was private and theirs, at prices they set on both
sides of the transaction.

 The railroad could show modest
returns while the men controlling it became extraordinarily rich, and the
accounts of the Contract and Finance Company were destroyed before a
congressional investigation could examine them.

Keep this manoeuvre in mind. Later, a university under pressure over its
military research will divest the research institute to itself, and the
structure of the transaction will be recognisable.

## The workforce

Crocker's construction superintendent, James Strobridge, opposed hiring Chinese
workers. From 1865 the
company recruited heavily, first in California and then directly from Guangdong.

They were paid less than white workers doing the same work, and unlike white
workers they were not provided with board, which they organised themselves. The
work was lethal. The Summit Tunnel was driven through granite by hand and with
black powder and nitroglycerine, at a rate measured in inches a day, through two
of the worst winters on record. Men were killed by explosions, rockfalls,
avalanches, and cold.

In June 1867 several thousand Chinese workers in the Sierra struck. Their
demands were an increase in pay to match white workers, a reduction in the
working day in the tunnels, and an end to the practice of whipping and of
restraining workers from leaving. It was, by any measure, one of the largest
labour actions in the United States to that date.

Crocker broke it in about a week by cutting off the food supply.

They went back to work. Two years later they finished the line, and on 28 April
1869 a Central Pacific crew — Chinese, with a handful of Irish rail handlers —
laid ten miles and fifty-six feet of track in a single day to win a bet with the
Union Pacific. It has never been beaten.

 The next chapters describe how.

\newpage



## Vacant Chairs

1 July 1862. Washington.

The Senate chamber in midsummer heat held a different geography than it had held three years earlier. The desks that had belonged to members from the seceded states stood empty—not removed, not reassigned, simply vacant in a room where every vote now landed on one side of a war. The opposition that had blocked a central transcontinental route for nearly a decade was not present to block it anymore.

The Pacific Railway Act would be signed that day. Four Sacramento merchants who sold groceries and hardware would receive a federal charter, a checkerboard of public land along the right of way, and thirty-year government bonds paid per mile of track. The engineer who had surveyed the Sierra route would not be in the photograph when the line was finished. He was already dead of yellow fever in New York. The men who bought him out would be in every picture that mattered.

## Davis speaks

On 21 January 1861, days before leaving the Senate to help lead a rebellion, Jefferson Davis spoke in favour of a transcontinental railroad.

“I have thought it an achievement worthy of our age and of our people,” he said, “to couple with bonds of iron the people of the Pacific with the valley of the Mississippi” — and to show that even snow-capped mountains intervening could not divide them.

He was about to divide the country. And his departure is the reason the railroad was finally built, on a route he had spent eight years opposing.



The irony is too neat for fiction, which is why the record is useful. Davis had spent the 1850s arguing that the southern route was the practicable one. He would spend the early 1860s trying to destroy the Union that would eventually fund a line nowhere near his preferred terminus. His January speech is the last public embrace of a railroad as national glue from a man who was preparing to break the nation in two.

## The surveys

On 3 March 1853 Congress appropriated a hundred and fifty thousand dollars for the War Department to determine the most practicable and economical route for a railroad from the Mississippi to the Pacific. The work was given to the Corps of Topographical Engineers, under the direction of the Secretary of War.

The Secretary of War was Jefferson Davis.

Four principal corridors were surveyed: along the 47th and 49th parallels in the north, the 37th to 39th through the central Rockies, the 35th across the southwest, and the 32nd along the southern border. Parties went into the field within two months. The results were published as a twelve-volume report, one of the great documents of nineteenth-century American science, full of geology, botany, ethnography, and superb lithographs.

As a decision procedure it failed completely. Every field commander reported having found the most practicable and economical route to the Pacific. Stevens made the case for the north, Whipple for the 35th parallel, Beckwith for the central line, Parke and Pope for the south. Congress, having spent two years and a considerable sum, was exactly where it had started.

The surveys mapped more than grades. They mapped peoples whose territories lay across every corridor, reduced in the reports to obstacles, resources, and climate tables. A distributive fight wore the costume of a technical one.

## Davis's conclusion

Davis was not in doubt, and his case deserves its full strength because the case was substantially sound.

He reported for the 32nd parallel, and the engineering arguments were real. It was the shortest of the routes, at under seventeen hundred miles. It was estimated as the cheapest, at something in the region of seventy-two million dollars. It ran at low elevations through country with mild winters, avoiding the snow that would eventually require the Central Pacific to build miles of timber snowsheds over its track. It passed through territory that was already organised.

On the merits available in 1855, a competent engineer with no sectional interest could have reached the same conclusion. The southern route was not obviously wrong. It was obviously southern, and everyone in Washington understood that a railroad determines where population, commerce, and political weight accumulate for the next century.

Davis was from Mississippi. He had served in both houses for that state. He would become president of the Confederacy, and he continued to advocate a southern railroad after secession.

## Gadsden

The clearest evidence that this was never merely an engineering question is James Gadsden.

Gadsden was a South Carolinian, a former army officer, a plantation owner, and president of the South Carolina Railroad. He had advocated secession in 1850. He wanted San Diego as the Pacific terminus of a southern line, and he had earlier supported a proposal to divide California into two states, with the southern half open to slavery.

In 1853 he was appointed minister to Mexico, and he negotiated the purchase of a strip of territory south of the Gila River — the Gadsden Purchase — which resolved the one significant obstacle to the 32nd-parallel route, a section that would otherwise have run through Mexican territory.

The United States bought land from a foreign country in order to make a particular railroad route feasible, and the man who negotiated it wanted that route because it would carry a slave economy to the Pacific.

## Eight years of nothing

And then nothing happened, for eight years.

Both major parties put a Pacific railroad in their platforms in 1856 and again in 1860. Bills were introduced. None passed. Northern members would not fund a southern route; southern members would not fund a northern one; and neither side would accept a central compromise that gave the other an advantage in the territories.

The deadlock was not technical and could not be resolved technically. A survey can establish grades and rainfall. It cannot resolve a distributive question, because the parties to a distributive question are not disagreeing about facts.

## The chairs empty

Then, in the winter of 1860 and 1861, the southern members left.

The arithmetic of the chamber changed completely and at once. With no prospect of a southern route being adopted, and nobody present to oppose a central one, Congress found that it had very little difficulty agreeing terms. As the National Park Service's own history of the line puts it, senators and representatives had little trouble settling on an acceptable bill once the opposition was gone.

Theodore Judah spent the winter of 1861 and 1862 in Washington lobbying for it.

Judah was the engineer who had surveyed a practicable line over the Sierra Nevada, and who had persuaded four Sacramento merchants to incorporate the Central Pacific Railroad of California in June 1861 to build it. He was relentless, well prepared, and personally responsible for a great deal of the bill's technical content.

Lincoln supported it, on two grounds that were both about the war: a transcontinental line was a military asset, and binding the Pacific coast to the Union was a strategic necessity at a moment when the Union's continued existence was not assured.

The bill moved through Congress in May and June of 1862. Lincoln signed it on 1 July.

## What the Act did

The Pacific Railway Act authorised two companies — the Union Pacific building west from the Missouri River at Council Bluffs, and the Central Pacific building east from the navigable waters of the Sacramento River — to construct a railroad and telegraph line, and it paid them in two currencies.

The first was land: alternate sections along the right of way, in a checkerboard pattern, subsequently doubled by amendment. The second was credit: thirty-year government bonds at six per cent, issued per mile of track completed, at rates that varied with terrain and were highest in the mountains.

The Union Pacific was chartered by the federal government directly — the first corporation so chartered since the Second Bank of the United States.

One correction is worth making because a reader checking the most obvious source will hit it. The National Archives' summary page for the Act states that it designated the 32nd parallel as the initial transcontinental route. That is contradicted by the statute printed on the same page, which specifies Council Bluffs to Sacramento, and by the Senate's own historical account.

## What the vacancy purchased

Four merchants in Sacramento — a grocer, a hardware dealer, a dry-goods merchant, and another hardware dealer — now held a federal charter, a land grant, and a bond subsidy for the western half of the most valuable infrastructure project in American history.

They had not invented anything. They had not surveyed the route; Judah had done that, and they bought him out. What they had was position, at the moment the chairs emptied.

The route was chosen by subtraction. The war fought to preserve the Union built the machine that would govern California, and handed it to private hands on the way through.

Nobody in the empty desks voted on that July afternoon. Their absence was the vote that counted.

## Judah in the snowless city

Washington in the winter of 1861–62 was a city of mud streets and military trains, preparing for a war that had already begun. Theodore Judah was there without the polish of a lobbyist born to the capital. He was an engineer with maps, profiles, and the kind of patience that came from having surveyed a route over the Sierra and then having to persuade merchants—and now congressmen—that the line was possible if someone would pay for it.

He had incorporated the Central Pacific with four Sacramento businessmen in June 1861. He believed in the central route because he had walked it on paper and in the field. He was also, by the winter of his lobbying, already entangled with men who saw the railroad as a vehicle for personal fortune rather than a public work. That fight would kill his leverage before yellow fever killed him. For the moment, he needed statutes.

Lincoln’s support mattered for reasons that had little to do with California commerce. A transcontinental line was a military asset: troops, materiel, and messages could cross the interior faster than a voyage around the Horn. Binding the Pacific coast to the Union mattered at a moment when the Union’s continued existence was not assured. California had entered the Union as a free state in 1850, part of a sectional bargain. Keeping it tied to Washington was not an abstraction in 1862. It was strategy.

Judah supplied the technical scaffolding—the grades, the passes, the practicable alignment. The empty southern desks supplied the votes.

## Land and bonds, made human

The Act’s two currencies sound abstract until they are measured against a body of work.

Land grants in alternate sections created a checkerboard of public and railroad acreage along the right of way—later doubled to twenty sections per mile, which is to say twenty square miles of land for every mile of track laid. Government bonds at six per cent paid sixteen thousand dollars per mile on the flat, thirty-two thousand across the high plains, and forty-eight thousand in the mountains.

Those mountain miles would become a battlefield of geology reports. The Central Pacific would obtain an opinion placing the base of the Sierra Nevada farther west than independent geologists would have drawn it, collecting the higher rate across valley floor that any traveller would call valley. Lincoln accepted the opinion. The men laying track in snow would not be consulted.

The Union Pacific became the first corporation chartered directly by the federal government since the Second Bank of the United States. The Central Pacific was a California corporation that would behave, for the next decade, like a state within a state—setting rates, acquiring ferries, deciding which towns lived and which withered when the line passed them by.

## The road not taken

James Gadsden’s southern corridor did not disappear because it was absurd. It disappeared because the men who wanted it left the chamber.

The Gadsden Purchase bought the strip that made the 32nd-parallel survey legible on paper. The proposal to split California into two states—opening the southern half to slavery—shows how closely railroad politics tracked the slavery politics that emptied the desks. A reader who treats the Pacific Railway Act as a triumph of engineering alone will miss that the engineering had been available for years. What changed in 1862 was the seating chart.

Indigenous nations across the surveyed corridors appear in the expedition reports as ethnography and climate tables, not as parties to the decision. Their land was mapped as terrain to be crossed. The Act would cross it with iron whether or not anyone in those nations had consented.

## Stevens, Whipple, Beckwith, Parke, Pope

Name the commanders because the failure of the survey was personal as well as political. Stevens argued the north. Whipple argued the 35th parallel. Beckwith argued the central line. Parke and Pope argued the south. Each man had walked ground, measured grades, and returned to Washington with the certainty only fieldwork breeds—that his route was the one.

Congress received twelve volumes of science and zero consensus. The lithographs were beautiful. The botany was meticulous. The ethnography treated peoples as data. None of it answered the question Congress actually needed to settle: which section of the union would benefit from the next century of commerce.

Davis answered for the South. Secession removed his answer from the chamber. Judah answered for the Sierra. The Associates answered for themselves.

When Lincoln signed the Act on 1 July 1862, the empty desks were still empty. The route was no longer in doubt. The only doubt left was how much public money would flow into private hands before the spike was driven—and whether anyone at Promontory would miss.

## July heat

The signing itself was quiet compared with the war news that summer. Lincoln’s hand moved across a page authorizing two companies to bind the continent with iron and wire. Judah’s maps were in drawers and briefcases. The Associates’ incorporations were already filed in California. The Chinese workers who would die in the snow were not yet recruited under that name. The isthmus was already a memory for men like Huntington, who had learned to charge for passage long before they charged for freight.

The Act did not mention Promontory. It did not mention a golden spike. It mentioned land, bonds, and a right of way. Everything else—the bets, the strikes, the burned books, the photograph—would be built on top of those two currencies like track on ties.

The vacant chairs made that possible. The toll would follow.

\newpage



## The Summit

Late May–June 1867. Donner Summit, Sierra Nevada.

Snow still lay in the cuts above the tunnel faces. The Central Pacific had been driving granite for two winters, measuring progress in inches per day, blasting with black powder and nitroglycerine while men worked in cold that turned breath to ice on beards and jacket collars. The Summit Tunnel was the line’s throat: until it opened, the Sierra remained a wall between Sacramento and the interior West.

The men doing that work were, by this stage, overwhelmingly Chinese.

Crocker's construction superintendent, James Strobridge, had opposed hiring Chinese workers. Crocker overruled him — the labour supply was inadequate, white workers left for the mines constantly, and a trial group performed well. From 1865 the company recruited heavily, first in California and then directly from Guangdong.



They were paid less than white workers doing the same work, and unlike white workers they were not provided with board, which they organised themselves. The company did not systematically record their deaths, which is why every figure offered is an estimate and why the estimates vary so widely. Avalanches, explosions, rockfalls, and cold killed men whose names rarely entered the official story the Promontory photograph would later tell.

In late May, unbelievably and unacceptably, the Chinese went on strike. “This is the hardest blow we have had,” E. B. Crocker reported.



One reconstruction fixes a date: 25 June 1867, several thousand men on the Sierra grade demanding pay parity and shorter tunnel hours. Ambrose places the walkout in late May; the company’s own later accounts blur the calendar. The strike’s length in days matters less than its length in leverage: long enough to frighten Crocker, short enough to break before bond deadlines slipped. Whether the walkout began in late May or consolidated in June, the fact on the ground was the same: labour that had been treated as replaceable stopped moving rock and rail at the moment the company needed it most.

Chang’s account of the walkout is quieter than a Hollywood strike. “The men who were working at that hour walked out of the tunnels and away from the tracks. The ones who were sleeping slept on…”.



No speeches survive in the company record like Crocker’s complaint. The action was spatial: bodies leaving the tunnel mouth, tools set down, the grade going still while foremen counted who was missing.

The demands, as historians have reconstructed them, were concrete: wages equal to white workers doing the same work, shorter hours in the tunnels, and an end to whipping and to physical restraint when men tried to leave camp. By any measure it was one of the largest labour actions in the United States to that date.

The timeline notes Stanford relayed warning about Chinese unhappiness to Huntington before the walkout. If the governor in Sacramento knew trouble was coming and the men in the snow knew they had been underpaid for years, the strike was not a surprise. It was a bill presented after a long tab.

Charles Crocker broke it in about a week by cutting off the food supply.

That sentence is the whole politics of the summit in seven words. The company did not argue the merits on a square. It controlled rice and pork and flour in camps miles from any town. Men who refused to work could be starved back to the face faster than any court could hear them. They went back to work.

## After the strike

The tunnels did not stop because men had made a moral point. They stopped because granite was still there and bonds were still being issued per mile. Snowsheds would eventually cover miles of track on the western approach—timber tunnels above the rails so avalanches would pass over the line instead of burying it. Chinese crews continued to do the lethal share of that work, paid less, credited less, and absent from the photographs when the work was done.

Thirteen years later the United States passed a law barring Chinese labourers from entering the country. The men who had blasted through the Sierra would be told, by federal statute, that their presence had been a temporary convenience rather than a contribution.

The strike did not change the pay structure in any lasting way the record celebrates. It did reveal what the company would do when labour briefly held the only leverage available in a mountain camp: withhold food until the line moved again.

The hardest blow Crocker reported was not to the schedule alone. It was to the story the Associates needed—that the continent was being united by voluntary American effort. The walkout in the snow proved the summit was built by men the ceremony at Promontory would not name, and that when those men asked for parity, the answer came from the commissary, not the courts.

The tunnel faces waited. The snow did not negotiate. Neither did Crocker.

## The work before the walkout

To understand the strike, start with the work it interrupted.

The Summit Tunnel was driven through granite by hand, black powder, and nitroglycerine, at rates measured in inches per day through two of the worst winters on record. Men lived and labored in cuts where snow buried the grade so completely that crews dug tunnels through drifts simply to reach the face. Avalanches killed without warning. Explosions killed with warning men could not heed fast enough.

Strobridge ran construction on the ground. Crocker ran the decision to hire Chinese workers when white labour walked off to mines or simply would not stay at the wages offered. From 1865 recruitment extended to Guangdong. At peak construction the Central Pacific employed somewhere between ten and twelve thousand Chinese workers—constituting the large majority of its workforce.

They organized their own board because the company would not provide it. They were paid less than white workers doing the same work. When they died, the company did not systematically record their names. Historians still argue about totals. The absence of a ledger is itself a policy.

## The week Crocker won

Crocker’s response to the strike was not negotiation. It was logistics.

Food in a mountain camp is a weapon if one side controls the storehouse. Cutting off rice and pork and flour did not require a court order. It required foremen willing to enforce starvation until men returned to the tunnels. The company won in about a week.

Ambrose’s framing of the strike as “unbelievable and unacceptable” records the shock of men who believed the workforce was a commodity that would not organize. Chang’s framing records the silence of the walkout itself—the men who were sleeping slept on—without the brass band of American labour history. Read together, they describe the same event: a revolt large enough to terrify Crocker, and disciplined enough to leave the company’s narrative scrambling.

E. B. Crocker’s line—“the hardest blow we have had”—is the sound of that scramble. It is also an admission that the line’s hardest blows were not granite. They were human.

## What continued after

The strike ended. The tunnels did not.

Men went back to faces that still had to be blasted. Snowsheds would cover miles of track on the western approaches—wooden tunnels above the rails so avalanches could pass over the line instead of wiping it out each winter. Chinese crews continued to do the lethal share. Irish rail handlers worked beside them in smaller numbers. Pay inequality persisted.

On 28 April 1869 a Central Pacific crew—Chinese, with a handful of Irish rail handlers—would lay ten miles and fifty-six feet of track in a single day to win a bet with the Union Pacific. That record still stands. It is remembered as a feat of organization and speed. It is rarely remembered as the culmination of labour that had been starved back to work less than two years earlier.

The Promontory photograph would come eleven days later. The men who laid ten miles in a day would not fill the frame. The men who broke the strike with a commissary would.

Nobody in the snow in 1867 knew the date of the photograph. They knew the face of the tunnel, the weight of a drill, and the price of asking to be paid like the men beside them.

Crocker knew the price of saying no. He set it at a week without food.

## A photograph they would not take

There would be no lithograph of the strike. No champagne on the tunnel face. No telegraph message to tell the nation that Chinese workers had shut down one of the largest construction projects in North America until they were treated like men rather than replaceable parts.

There would only be Crocker’s letter calling it the hardest blow, and Chang’s image of men walking out while others slept on, and the company’s commissary closing like a door.

When the line was finished, promoters would prefer the ten-mile day—a spectacle of speed. They would prefer Promontory—a spectacle of union. They would not prefer the week in 1867 when the summit belonged to labour, briefly, until hunger returned it to capital.

That omission is as deliberate as the absence of Chinese faces in the Promontory photograph. The toll road required a story about voluntary American progress. The strike was evidence that progress had been extracted, inch by inch, from men who had signed up for wages and received a war instead.

The tunnel faces waited. The snow did not negotiate. Neither did Crocker—and when he won, the line moved again toward the bet Crocker would make with Durant, toward the spike Stanford would miss, toward the books Huntington would hide.

The summit was not a backdrop. It was the price of the photograph.

## Numbers without names

Between ten and twelve thousand Chinese workers at peak. Several thousand on strike. A week to break them. Two years later, ten miles and fifty-six feet of track in a day. These figures come from company papers, congressional testimony summarized in secondary accounts, and the project ledger that lists 25 June 1867 as a strike date.

What the numbers do not carry is names in proportion. The Central Pacific did not systematically record Chinese deaths. Estimates of fatalities on the Sierra range widely in later scholarship. The company preferred records that could be shown to bondholders: miles completed, subsidies claimed, photographs taken.

When historians reconstruct the strike, they reconstruct it from Crocker’s alarm, Chang’s image of men walking away, and the silence where payroll detail should be. That silence is not an archival accident. It is how a firm communicates which lives are countable.

Strobridge and Crocker would be named in every history. E. B. Crocker’s quote would survive because it served the company’s narrative of victimhood at the hands of ungrateful labour. The men who walked out would survive as a crowd.

The strike did not stop the tunnel. It marked the moral economy of the line: work extracted under unequal pay, discipline enforced by hunger, credit claimed by men who would later miss spikes on purpose for the cameras and still hear bells.

## Strobridge on the ground

Strobridge had opposed hiring Chinese workers until Crocker overruled him with evidence from a trial crew and the simple fact that white labour would not stay. After that, Strobridge ran camps where nitroglycerine and black powder turned granite into rubble inch by inch. He was the face foremen saw when shifts changed. Crocker was the voice that decided whether rice arrived.

When the strike came, Strobridge did not negotiate as an equal with thousands of men. He enforced Crocker’s commissary policy because that was the job: keep the tunnel moving, keep the bond miles counting, keep the mountain rate collecting on the western slope.

The company’s public story would later praise organization—the ten-mile day, the snowsheds, the completion photograph. The private story in 1867 was a week without food until men picked up tools again.

That is the summit’s legacy as surely as the tunnel opening: proof that the line’s owners would treat hunger as a management tool, and that Congress, having paid in land and bonds, would not intervene on the side of the men who made the line possible.

The tunnel faces waited. The snow did not negotiate. Neither did Crocker—and when the strike ended, the same faces returned to the same granite, for the same differential pay, until the wire at Promontory said the job was done.

## Snowsheds and credit

After the strike, the company did not reform pay. It engineered around the mountain.

Miles of timber snowsheds would cover the western approaches—wooden tunnels above the track so avalanches could pass overhead while trains ran underneath. Chinese crews cut and lifted the timber. Irish handlers spiked rail. Bond miles accumulated at the higher mountain rate where geology reports placed the Sierra’s base.

Credit flowed to the railroad. Wages flowed to men at different tables. Death flowed to the camps without ledgers.

When E. B. Crocker called the strike the hardest blow, he meant hardest to the schedule and to the subsidy arithmetic. He did not mean hardest to conscience. The company’s response proved that.

The summit was where the public story of the transcontinental line—heroic engineering, national union—met the private story of who would be paid, fed, and named. The strike made the meeting visible for one week. Hunger made it invisible again.

The tunnel opened. The sheds went up. The ten-mile day approached. Promontory waited with its cameras and its missing spike.

The men who had walked out in June would not be invited to the platform in May. They had already been taught what the commissary could do.

The strike remains documented at the edges: Crocker’s alarm, Chang’s walkout, the ledger’s date, Stanford’s warning relayed east, and the commissary’s answer. That is enough to know who held power when granite was still between California and the rest of the country.

\newpage



## Ten Miles

28 April 1869. Utah desert, Central Pacific grade.

Charles Crocker offered a bet of ten thousand dollars to Thomas Durant of the Union Pacific, saying the Central Pacific would lay ten miles of track in one day.



Durant ran the eastern company the way the Associates ran the western one: as a race for subsidies, land, and credit. The bet was not a sporting gesture. It was a public-relations weapon in a contest over which line could claim the continent’s attention while both hauled iron toward Promontory. Ten miles in a day had never been done. Crocker intended to do it anyway.

Ten thousand dollars was a fortune to a rail hand and a line item to a man who would invoice the work through a construction company he co-owned. The wager made the newspapers. The invoices made the fortune. Both depended on Chinese crews who had already struck once and been starved back to the grade.

Organizing such a day required the same brutality the Sierra had taught. Rails, ties, and spikes had to be staged for miles. Teams had to be fed before dawn and moved in relays. Chinese crews who had been paid less than white workers, who had struck in 1867 and been starved back to work, would shoulder the heaviest share again. The record—ten miles and fifty-six feet—still stands. It is remembered as triumph. It is also the arithmetic of a company that had learned to treat men as movable parts in a wager between millionaires.

Think of what ten miles means for a body.

A mile of track is ties, plates, spikes, and rail lifted, aligned, and driven home by crews working in relays. Ten miles in a single day meant staging material far ahead of the railhead, feeding men before light, and keeping the line of wagons and handlers moving without the usual stop at sundown. It meant Chinese crews who had built the Sierra grade doing again, in desert heat instead of snow, what the company had trained them to do: move iron faster than anyone believed possible.

Durant watched from the Union Pacific side of the contest. Crocker collected on the bet. The record still stands—not because the day was humane, but because it was organized with military precision for a single purpose: to prove the Central Pacific could outwork its rival in public.

They finished the line two weeks later. The photograph at Promontory would not show them.

## The subsidy, and where it went

The public contribution that made the wager possible was enormous.

Land grants of alternate sections along the right of way, doubled in 1864 to twenty sections per mile—twenty square miles of land for every mile of track. Government bonds at six per cent, issued per mile, graduated by terrain: sixteen thousand dollars a mile across the flat, thirty-two thousand across the high plains, and forty-eight thousand a mile in the mountains.



Where the mountains began was a question of geology, and geology was a matter of expert opinion. The Central Pacific obtained an opinion placing the base of the Sierra Nevada considerably further west than any geologist unconnected to the company would have put it. Lincoln accepted it. The company collected the mountain rate across some twenty-odd miles of what was, by any ordinary description, valley floor.

The larger method was the construction company. The Central Pacific contracted the building of its line to the Contract and Finance Company, which was owned by the same four men. The railroad—a corporation with public obligations, public credit, and eventually public shareholders—paid the construction firm, which was private and theirs, at prices they set on both sides of the transaction.

The profit was captured at the point of transfer. The railroad could show modest returns while the men controlling it became extraordinarily rich, and the accounts of the Contract and Finance Company were destroyed before a congressional investigation could examine them.

Collis Huntington feared that investigators would demand to see those books.



The fear is instructive. The line was not complete when Huntington began worrying about ledgers in Washington. The ceremony at Promontory was still days away. The Associates already knew which numbers they did not want read in a committee room.

## Both chairs, briefly

Leland Stanford had been Governor of California from January 1862 to December 1863 while also serving as president of the Central Pacific. He advocated the railroad as a public necessity, signed state assistance, and presided over the Sacramento groundbreaking in both capacities. There was no conflict-of-interest framework to violate. Everyone knew. The arrangement was visible and unremarkable—which is its own kind of power.

Stanford would drive the ceremonial spike at Promontory and miss. The public would cheer anyway. The telegraph would report “done!”. The public subsidy would remain in private hands.

## What the monopoly then did

With the line complete, the Central Pacific and its successor the Southern Pacific held the only rail connection between California and the east, and proceeded to price it.

Rates were set not by cost but by what a shipper could bear—a practice the company defended openly as charging what the traffic will bear. A grower in the Central Valley had one way to reach a market. A town that cooperated got a station; a town that did not got a line routed around it and ceased to exist. The company acquired control of shipping and ferries as well, so that alternatives to its rails led to its docks.

Four men now held a fortune built on federal land, federal credit, and a workforce paid a differential that was never made up. One of them was about to convert his share into something that could not be regulated, taxed, or broken up—a university on rancho land that could not be sold.

That conversion belongs to a later episode. The toll road belongs to this one.

## The bells and the books

The nation heard bells on 10 May 1869. Editors called it binding. Engineers shook hands for the camera. Stanford’s mallet struck rail and the wire closed.

The Contract and Finance Company books were already being kept where no passenger would look. Huntington’s Washington work ensured that the margin stayed on the private side of the transfer while the risk stayed on the public side of the charter.

The Chinese workers who laid ten miles in a day were not in the photograph. The men who starved a strike in the Sierra were not on the platform. The Americans who died on the isthmus in 1849 were not remembered at all.

The episode ends on that split: a public story told in spikes and bells, and a private story told in ledgers that would burn before investigators could read them.

The border had moved nine days after gold in the previous generation. In this one, the continent was “joined” while the toll was installed. The next episode would ask what a monopoly does when it owns the only way through—and whom it chooses to blame when people notice.

## Epilogue at the wire

On 10 May 1869 the telegraph operator closed the circuit. Bells rang in cities that would never see the Summit Tunnel or the isthmus mud. Listeners heard “done!” and understood completion.

Completion was the wrong word. Transfer was the right one.

Public land had been granted. Public bonds had been issued. Private construction companies had captured the margin. Labour that had struck in the snow had been broken at the commissary. Labour that had laid ten miles in a day had been excluded from the frame. Labour that had died on the isthmus had been folded into a merchant’s letter home.

The Associates would price the line. They would buy legislatures. They would acquire ferries and wharves until shippers could not escape their rates. One of them would build a university that could not sell its land, turning a railroad fortune into an institution that would outlast any antitrust suit.

But that is forward. At the wire, only the sound travelled instantly. The books moved more slowly, and some of them did not move at all—they were destroyed before anyone outside the four merchants could read the margin.

The nation celebrated. The toll was open. The ledgers were already elsewhere.

## What “done” left out

Read the Pacific Railway Act as a price list and Promontory as a receipt.

The Act paid in land checkerboarded along the route—acres that would become towns, farms, and eventually suburban real estate without the railroad ever needing to haul another tie. It paid in bonds graded by terrain, with mountain miles worth more than valley miles, and with the definition of “mountain” negotiable by experts on the company payroll.

The Contract and Finance Company sat between the public railroad and the private fortunes. It invoiced the work. It set the prices. It destroyed its books when investigators drew near.

The Chinese workforce that made the ten-mile day possible had been imported when white labour would not stay, paid less when it did, struck when the terms became unbearable, and starved back to the grade when the company refused to bargain.

The isthmus deaths Huntington recorded in 1849 were not on the platform either. They were part of the same supply chain: bodies moving toward California, money moving toward whoever owned the bottleneck.

“Done!” was a telegraph word. It was not an accounting word. The accounting continued in rooms Huntington controlled, on paper Hopkins kept, and in courts the Associates would learn to treat as another line item.

When the Wilson Committee and other investigators later pressed for the construction company’s records, they would find fire and absence where ledgers should have been. By then the monopoly would already own the only way out of the Central Valley. By then the bells would already be memory.

The episode ends where it began: a ceremony visible from coast to coast, and a set of books kept where passengers never went. The toll was not the spike. The toll was everything that happened after the wire said done.

## Stanford’s two hats

Stanford had been elected governor in 1861 and served through 1863 while serving as president of the Central Pacific. He signed state assistance. He spoke of the railroad as necessity. He stood at the Sacramento groundbreaking in January 1863 wearing both offices without embarrassment, because no law forbade it and no constituency powerful enough to stop the line wanted to try.

At Promontory he wore the face the East wanted: benefactor, statesman, man of the spike. The mallet missed. The wire closed. The public heard completion.

The private side was Hopkins’s ledgers, Huntington’s fear of committees, Crocker’s bets, and a construction company that existed to invoice the railroad at prices the same four men set. That side did not miss. It collected.

## The grower at the station

Picture a wheat grower in the Central Valley after 1869. One railroad. One rate set by what the traffic will bear. One ferry connection if the company owns the dock. Cooperation meant a siding; resistance meant rerouting until a town dried up.

That is the toll in daily life—not a spike in Utah, but a invoice in Sacramento for moving crop to market. The public had paid to build the line. The Associates charged to use it.

The bells rang for binding. The grower would pay for passage.

## Durant’s view

Thomas Durant had his own subsidies to protect on the Union Pacific side of the bet. The contest over ten miles in a day was not friendship. It was rivalry between companies that both lived on federal land and federal bonds, each trying to prove it deserved the next tranche of credit.

When Crocker’s crews set the record, Durant paid the wager and lost face. The Central Pacific gained prestige it would convert into political cover when rate-setting angered shippers. Prestige is cheaper than rebates.

The bet mattered because it was public. The books mattered because they were private. Huntington understood both currencies. Hopkins kept both ledgers. Stanford provided the photograph.

## One sentence on fire

The accounts of the Contract and Finance Company were destroyed before a congressional investigation could examine them. Rayner’s account of Huntington’s fear that the Wilson Committee would demand those books is the shadow of that fire in Washington correspondence.

Fire is convenient when margin lives on the construction side and modesty lives on the railroad side. The public saw ties and spikes. The investigators saw smoke.

The episode closes on that split: bells for the public, ashes for the committee, and a single rail line charging what the traffic will bear for decades to come.

Collis Huntington would spend the post-Promontory years making sure the committee never got a clean read of the margin. Leland Stanford would spend his turning part of the fortune into stone and campus on rancho land. Charles Crocker would spend his defending rates. Mark Hopkins would spend his in ledgers that looked modest next to the construction invoices.

The Chinese workers who laid ten miles in April and vanished from the photograph in May would spend theirs in exclusion law and erasure—a cost the episode records here so the ceremony cannot pretend it was the whole story.

The toll was open. The books were already elsewhere. The wire had said done.

## Both chairs

Stanford was elected Governor of California in 1861 and served from January 1862 to December 1863 — precisely the period in which the Pacific Railway Act passed, the Central Pacific was organised, and California committed public support to the enterprise. He was simultaneously president of the Central Pacific Railroad.



As governor he advocated the railroad as a public necessity. He signed legislation providing state and county assistance. He presided over the Sacramento groundbreaking in January 1863 in both capacities, and nobody at the ceremony appears to have regarded the arrangement as remarkable. The roles were simply held at the same time by the same man, and the disclosure was total: everyone knew.

Nothing here depended on secrecy. It depended on a structural arrangement being visible and unremarkable. That is how public subsidy becomes private fortune without a scandal—openly, while bells still ring for the spike.

\newpage
