# Episode 7: The Road

## Sand Hill

**Menlo Park, California. 1972.**

The fog was still on the foothills when Eugene Kleiner and Tom Perkins signed the lease. The address was 3000 Sand Hill Road — low buildings, parking lots, oak trees, a view of the coastal range, and nothing that announced what the place would become. 

Sand Hill Road runs west from the freeway at Menlo Park, past the Stanford campus edge, and up into the hills. There is nothing to look at.

A very large share of the world's consequential technology funding over the last fifty years has passed through those buildings. 

Nobody in the office that morning knew they were founding a toll gate. They knew they were opening a partnership. Kleiner had been one of the eight who walked out on William Shockley in 1957; Perkins had run Hewlett-Packard's computer division. They were operators from the semiconductor industry who had moved into finance, which meant they could evaluate technical claims and knew personally most of the people worth funding. 

The capital and the industry were the same social network. They had been colleagues.

## Why the money had to change form

Consider financing a semiconductor company in 1957.

It has no revenue. It has no collateral, because its assets are a lease, some equipment, and eight men. It has enormous variance: it will either be worth a great deal or nothing, with little in between.

A bank cannot lend against that. Arthur Rock's insight in the Fairchild financing was to take equity instead. If the enterprise fails, the money is gone. If it succeeds, the return is unbounded. 

Rock said he backed people rather than businesses, on the argument that the plan would change and the founders would not.

## 1972

Two firms were founded in 1972, both on Sand Hill Road.

Kleiner Perkins was formed by Kleiner and Perkins. Sequoia Capital was founded by Don Valentine, who had been head of marketing at Fairchild and then at National Semiconductor.

Note the provenance. These were not financiers who had moved into technology. They were the industry, rearranged.

The physical clustering followed immediately, and it followed for a reason that is not obvious until stated: the asset being traded is judgement about people, and judgement about people does not travel. A partner needs to sit in the room, know the founder's former manager, and hear what the market is saying this week.

Proximity to the deal flow — which meant proximity to Stanford — was the whole business.

Four minutes from the campus that supplies the founders. That is the geography of the chokepoint, and it has not moved.

## The public money leg

The industry's scale changed because of a regulatory clarification, and this is the part that almost every account leaves out.

The Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974 imposed a prudent man standard on pension fund investment, and fund managers read it as prohibiting anything speculative. Venture capital, which was speculative by construction, was effectively closed off from the largest pool of investable capital in the country.

In 1979 the Department of Labor issued a clarification. The prudent man standard, it said, applied to the portfolio as a whole rather than to each investment in isolation, which meant a pension fund could allocate a small percentage to high-variance assets without breaching its duty.

The effect was immediate and enormous. Capital committed to venture funds rose by an order of magnitude over the following few years, and the dominant source of it became pension money — the retirement savings of American workers, institutional and public alike. 

So the capital chokepoint runs on public and quasi-public money in exactly the way the railroad did, and the beneficiaries of those pension funds have no say in how it is deployed and mostly do not know it is being deployed this way.

## Gatekeeping, not investing

Money is the smallest part of what a term sheet allocates. It sets the valuation, which determines what the founders keep. It assigns board seats, which determines who can remove them. It carries liquidation preferences, which determine who is paid first if the company sells. It includes protective provisions that give the investor a veto over the decisions that matter most: raising more money, selling the company, changing the business. 

Beyond the document, the firm supplies the things that actually determine the outcome. Introductions to customers. Recruitment of executives. Credibility with the next round of investors, which is nearly always the deciding factor.

A company that cannot get through Sand Hill Road does not merely lack money. It lacks access to customers, executives, and subsequent capital, all of which are routed through the same small number of partnerships.

The Southern Pacific decided which towns in the Central Valley would exist by deciding where the line ran. Sand Hill Road decides which companies exist by deciding which ones can scale. The mechanism is identical and the second version attracts no regulatory attention whatsoever, because nobody has ever thought of a venture fund as a common carrier. 

## Genentech

Bob Swanson was twenty-nine years old when Kleiner Perkins let him go.

He had been a partner at the firm for less than a year. He was intense, impatient, and convinced that recombinant DNA — the technique Stanley Cohen at Stanford and Herbert Boyer at UCSF had published — could be turned into products if someone would finance the attempt. 

Swanson called Boyer. In 1976 they incorporated Genentech in South San Francisco, on the other side of the hills from the road where Swanson had just lost his job.

The company would become the commercial proof that biology could be financed like software: long timelines, binary outcomes, enormous returns on the ones that worked. It would go public in 1980, on patents filed while Swanson was still trying to get Kleiner Perkins to return his calls.

A fired kid at the gate had become a biotech empire. The road had turned him away. The science had not.

He would not live to see the full shape of what he started. Swanson died in 1999, at fifty-two. Genentech outlived him by decades.

## Who does not get through

Studies of venture funding over the past several decades have consistently found that the overwhelming majority of American venture capital goes to companies in a handful of metropolitan areas, that the share going to companies founded solely by women has run in low single-digit percentages, and that the share going to Black founders has been lower still. The partnerships making the decisions have had a comparable composition. 

The industry's explanation is pattern matching: partners fund founders who resemble founders who have succeeded before. That explanation is honest and it describes a mechanism for reproducing an existing distribution indefinitely.

Rock said he backed people rather than businesses. The question the corridor leaves is which people, and the answer has been consistent for fifty years.

The April Third Movement had occupied a laboratory on campus three years earlier. The university would cut SRI loose in 1970. The military work moved off-campus without slowing down.

The rebellion had not stopped the machine. It had made the machine look less like a weapons lab.

On Sand Hill Road, the gate was already open.

## The other side of the corridor

Two further pieces completed the arrangement in the 1970s, and both were within a few miles of the road.

The first is the law. Wilson Sonsini, founded in Palo Alto in 1961, developed a practice of representing startups on terms adapted to companies with no money, sometimes taking equity in place of fees. They standardised the documents. The terms on which American technology companies are financed were substantially drafted by a handful of firms within a few miles of Stanford, and the resulting templates are now used everywhere. 

The second is the bank. Silicon Valley Bank, founded in 1983, built a business around lending to venture-backed companies and to the funds themselves, on underwriting logic that no conventional bank used: it assessed the quality of the investors behind a company rather than the company's own assets. That is a bank extending credit on the strength of a gate's endorsement. It worked for forty years before failing in 2023 for reasons that were about interest rates rather than about technology. 

Between the partnerships, the law firms, and the bank, a startup's entire institutional environment was contained within a few square miles and was constituted by people who knew each other. An outsider was not merely unfunded, but unrepresented, unbanked, and unintroduced.

The tightest gate in the system was four minutes from the campus that supplied its deal flow.

\newpage



## Pong

**Sunnyvale, California. 1972.**

Al Alcorn had built a cabinet with one game on the screen: two paddles, a dot, a score counter. He called it Pong. He installed a prototype in a bar in Burlingame to see if anyone would put quarters in it.

They did.

Nolan Bushnell had hired Al Alcorn at Atari when the company was still small enough that the engineer who built the game was also the man who fixed it when the coin mechanism jammed. 

What Pong proved was not that Americans wanted video games. Shopkeepers had known that since pinball. What it proved was that a hit could be manufactured in a garage, installed in a bar, and scaled — if someone could raise the money to build more cabinets than one engineer could solder.

"'We didn't need a business plan for Pong or the VCS,' Alcorn replied. He needed one now." 

The moment a garage becomes a corporation is the moment the corporation needs Sand Hill Road.

## The road answers

Venture capital in the 1970s was still learning what it was for. Semiconductors had been the template: brilliant engineers, impossible physics, a market that did not exist until the product existed.

Consumer electronics looked different. Margins were thinner. Fashion mattered. A game could be a phenomenon in 1973 and obsolete in 1974.

The firms on Sand Hill Road funded Atari anyway, because the pattern from Fairchild still applied: back the people, accept that the product would change, and own a piece of the upside if the upside arrived.

Atari's story would end in corporate wreckage — sold, split, buried under layers of debt and litigation that belong to a different book. What mattered for this corridor was simpler: a new category had passed through the gate, and the gate had learned to recognize categories.

## Reimers

**Stanford, California. 1974.**

Niels Reimers ran Stanford's Office of Technology Licensing out of a small suite of rooms that most faculty walked past without noticing. His job, in the years before Bayh–Dole, was to persuade professors that the university had an interest in what they invented — and to persuade companies that Stanford would not sue them if they licensed honestly.

Recombinant DNA was not an invention in the usual sense. It was a technique for cutting and splicing genetic material, published by Cohen and Boyer in 1973, funded principally by the National Institutes of Health. The public had paid for the science. The question of who would own the applications was still open.

Reimers filed patent applications on the Cohen–Boyer work on behalf of Stanford and UCSF. The filing was criticized inside the university for its potential to undermine Stanford's fundamental mission — the phrase appears in the record of the debate, and the criticism was not wrong. 

A research university was about to patent life itself.

Reimers's answer, reduced to its structure, was that if Stanford did not patent and license, someone else would — and the public would get neither revenue nor control. Whether that answer justified the filing is a question this book does not need to settle. The filing happened. The patents issued. An industry followed.

## Bayh–Dole

**Washington, D.C. December 1980.**

The least famous consequential statute in this book is the Bayh–Dole Act of 1980.

It provided that universities, small businesses, and non-profits could take title to inventions made with federal research funding. Before it, the default in much of the government was that publicly funded inventions belonged to the public. After it, the university patents the result of research the taxpayer paid for, licenses it to a company, and collects a royalty. 

Every research university in the United States built a technology licensing office. Stanford already had one — established in 1970 — which meant it was positioned when the statute arrived.

The Cohen–Boyer patents were already in hand. Stanford licensed the technology broadly and non-exclusively at modest rates rather than granting an exclusive licence, and it earned the universities a very large sum over the life of the patents — a few hundred million dollars, split between the two institutions. 

The non-exclusive approach is worth marking, because it is the best version of the arrangement. The technology was available to anyone who wanted it, an entire industry was built on it, and the universities were paid.

The case against is equally structural. The public funded the research through NIH grants, and then bought the resulting products at prices set by patent holders. Royalties flowed to institutions rather than to the agency that paid for the work.

## Genentech's IPO

Genentech went public in October 1980. The stock rose sharply on the first day of trading on a company with essentially no product revenue.

What that offering established is that biology could be financed like software. The venture model from Sand Hill Road had found a second industry.

Life became an asset class in 1980, on a Stanford and UCSF patent, funded from the road that Kleiner and Perkins had opened eight years earlier.

Reimers had filed the papers. Congress had changed the law. Swanson had been fired and then proved the firers wrong.

The gate was no longer only for chips.

## Atari's lesson

By the time Bayh-Dole passed, Atari had already taught the corridor a second lesson: consumer hits could be financed like chips if the people were right and the quarters kept coming.

Venture firms learned to tolerate fashion risk. The term sheet still controlled the board. The Southern Pacific had decided which towns lived. Sand Hill Road was deciding which categories of company would be allowed to become industries.

Al Alcorn would later run an engineering division far from the bar in Burlingame. Pong would be a footnote in a museum case. The road would remember the pattern longer than it remembered the game.

\newpage



## The assignment

**Stanford, California. 1962.**

Frank Shallenberger's entrepreneurship course required each student to write a business plan for a small company. Most of them wrote about electronics, because it was 1962 on the San Francisco Peninsula and electronics was the subject.

Phil Knight, a middle-distance runner from Oregon with an accounting degree, knew nothing about electronics, so he wrote about shoes.

---

company that came out of that paper controls no factories. It controls the design,

contracts out the parts that can. That is chokepoint logic in a product with no
transistors in it.

and electronics is the subject. Phil Knight, a middle-distance runner from Oregon

pattern recognition, and that the pattern is the book's. Knight had picked it up
from photographers at the *Oregon Journal* grumbling that their expensive German
cameras were being undercut by cheap Japanese ones. He heard a complaint and
extracted a mechanism: when manufacturing capability moves and brand loyalty
lags, there is a window, and the person who owns the distribution rights during
the window collects.

He acts on it with a directness that is almost comic. Travelling in Japan in
November 1962, he cold-calls Onitsuka in Kobe, is asked which company he
represents, invents "Blue Ribbon Sports" on the spot, and secures Tiger
distribution for the western United States. In January 1964 he and his old
Oregon coach Bill Bowerman put in $500 each and order three hundred pairs. The
partnership is sealed with a handshake. Nike's first full-time employee, Jeff
Johnson, is a runner Knight met through Stanford intramural track.

The second argument is structural. Nike, as it develops, does not build
factories. It keeps design, brand, and distribution, and contracts out
production — first Japan, then South Korea and Taiwan as costs rise, then
Indonesia, China and Vietnam. This is the fabless model, arrived at in footwear
before the semiconductor industry named it, and it works for the same reason: the
chokepoint is not the making, it is the specification and the shelf.

The third argument is the cost, and it gets equal weight. The contract-factory
system that made Nike's margins is the same structure, in a different century,
as the labour arrangements that built the Central Pacific: the work is real, the
workers are legally someone else's, and the fortune accrues where the contract
was signed rather than where the work was done.

Closes on the return leg. In 2011 Knight dedicates the Knight Management Center
at the Stanford Graduate School of Business — the school where the paper was
written. Later, the Knight-Hennessy Scholars programme. Money that began as a
class assignment returns to endow the classroom. Rail did this with a university
in 1885. Shoes did it with a business school in 2011.

**Note.** Do not put a page count on the Shallenberger paper. Sources conflict;
see the research log.

Does the mechanism work outside technology?

In 1962 a graduate student at the Stanford Graduate School of Business wrote a
paper for an entrepreneurship class. The assignment was to produce a business
plan for a small company. Almost everyone in the room wrote about electronics,
because it was 1962 on the San Francisco Peninsula and electronics was the
subject.

Phil Knight knew nothing about electronics. He was a middle-distance runner from
Oregon with an accounting degree, and he wrote about shoes.

The paper's title was its argument: could Japanese sports shoes do to German
sports shoes what Japanese cameras had done to German cameras? Frank
Shallenberger gave it an A. Sixty years later the company it became was among
the most valuable consumer brands on earth, and its founder's name is on a
building at the school where he wrote it.

Knight has told the origin repeatedly and consistently, and the detail that
matters is that he overheard it.

He had worked at the *Oregon Journal*, and the photographers there complained
that their expensive German cameras — Leica, Zeiss — were being undercut by
Japanese equipment that was nearly as good and dramatically cheaper. The
complaint was about their own profession. Knight extracted a general proposition
from it: when manufacturing capability relocates and brand loyalty lags behind,
there is a window, and whoever holds distribution rights during the window
collects.

That is not an insight about shoes or cameras. It is an insight about the timing
of a chokepoint, and it is the same insight Sam Brannan had about shovels.

He chose athletic shoes because he knew the sport, because German firms —
principally Adidas — dominated it, and because he knew nothing about
electronics. The most consequential business decision of his life was made by
process of elimination.

He acted on it with a directness that is close to comic.

Travelling in Japan in November 1962, he telephoned Onitsuka in Kobe, makers of
the Tiger running shoe, and asked for a meeting. He was received. Partway
through the discussion he was asked which company he represented.

He had no company. He invented one on the spot and called it Blue Ribbon Sports,
after the ribbons on the wall of his childhood bedroom. He left with
distribution rights for the western United States.

In January 1964 he and Bill Bowerman — his coach at Oregon, who had been cutting
up shoes and rebuilding them for years in search of something lighter — each put
in five hundred dollars and ordered three hundred pairs. The partnership was
sealed with a handshake. Knight sold the first shoes out of the boot of a car at
track meets, and kept his accounting job for years while doing it.

Nike's first full-time employee, hired in 1965, was Jeff Johnson, a runner
Knight had met through Stanford intramural track. The network again, doing
what networks had always done on the Peninsula: lowering the cost of finding someone you
can trust.

Here is why this chapter is in this book rather than in a business history.

As Nike developed, it did not build factories. It kept design, brand, and
distribution, and contracted out production entirely. That decision — made
initially because Blue Ribbon was a small importer with no capital, and then
retained deliberately as the company grew — turned out to be the durable source
of its margins.

The parts Nike kept cannot be copied. A design language, an athlete endorsement
portfolio, and shelf position in every sports retailer on earth are assets that
compound and that a competitor cannot buy. The parts Nike contracted out can be
copied by anyone with capital and a workforce, which is precisely why they are
low-margin and why they should belong to somebody else.

That is the fabless model, and Nike reached it in canvas and rubber before the
semiconductor industry named it in silicon. When chip designers in the 1980s and
1990s began separating design from fabrication for the same reasons, they were
rediscovering a structure that a shoe company had been running for a decade.

Own the specification and the shelf. Contract the making.

The model requires the factory to move, and this is the part the chapter must
not soften.

Onitsuka's Japanese production became expensive as the yen strengthened and
Japanese wages rose. Production went to South Korea and Taiwan in the 1970s and
early 1980s. As those economies developed, it went to Indonesia, then to China,
then to Vietnam, which is now the largest single source of Nike footwear.

Each move followed the same arithmetic and each was executed through contractors
rather than owned subsidiaries, which meant the conditions inside the factories
were legally somebody else's responsibility.

Through the 1990s that arrangement produced sustained reporting on wages,
overtime, and safety in contract plants in Indonesia and Vietnam, a consumer
boycott campaign, and a period in which the brand Nike had built so carefully
was the principal liability. Knight said publicly in 1998 that the Nike product
had become synonymous with slave wages, forced overtime, and arbitrary abuse,
and the company subsequently built a monitoring and disclosure apparatus,
published its supplier list, and became by some measures a leader in supply
chain transparency.

The arm's-length arrangement is not a
lapse in the model. It is the model. A company that owns no factories has no
employees in them, and every improvement in conditions is therefore a voluntary
undertaking rather than an obligation, revocable and dependent on continued
attention.

This is precisely the Contract and Finance Company. Put a
legal boundary between the activity and the party benefiting from it, and the
benefit flows across the boundary while the liability does not.

It is worth being precise about what Nike actually owns, because the answer
explains the margins.

A running shoe is not technically difficult. The materials are available, the
construction is well understood, and the factories that make Nike's shoes make
other companies' shoes on adjacent lines. There is genuine engineering in the
product and it is not the source of the pricing power.

What Nike owns is a position in the culture, and it acquired it by buying
attention. The endorsement model — signing athletes, associating the mark with
achievement, and advertising heavily enough that the association becomes
automatic — was not invented by Nike but was executed by Nike at a scale nobody
had attempted. The Michael Jordan contract in 1984 is the canonical instance: a
signature line that became a business larger than most footwear companies, built
on a person.

The mark itself was designed in 1971 by a graphic design student at Portland
State for a reported thirty-five dollars.

That is a chokepoint on attention, and it is the same asset that the earlier platforms will monetise directly. Nike got there first by paying for it rather
than by owning the channel, and the arrangement has held for forty years because
attention, unlike manufacturing capacity, cannot be relocated to a cheaper
jurisdiction.

In 2011 the Knight Management Center opened at the Stanford Graduate School of
Business — a complex of buildings funded principally by a hundred-million-dollar
gift from the man who had written a class paper in that school in 1962.

Later came Knight-Hennessy Scholars, endowed with a further very large gift and
run with the university's former president, funding graduate students across the
university on an explicitly leadership-focused model.

Trace the circuit. A credential produces an idea. The idea produces a company.
The company's margins depend on contract labour eight thousand miles away. The
proceeds return to endow the institution that issued the credential, which then
produces the next cohort, in buildings carrying the donor's name.

Leland Stanford did this with a railroad in 1885. Phil Knight did it with shoes
in 2011.

The mechanism is not about technology. It never was. It is about who holds the
part that cannot be copied, and where the proceeds go afterwards.

## Washington

Ronald Reagan was elected in November 1980, and a substantial part of the
intellectual apparatus of his administration came from a building on the
Stanford campus.

The Hoover Institution had spent the decades since Hoover's defeat in 1932 assembling
what a defeated president had begun: a permanent body of scholars developing a
particular set of economic and foreign policy arguments, sustained through a
period when those arguments were entirely out of fashion.

Milton Friedman joined Hoover in 1977. George Shultz was there before becoming
Secretary of State in 1982 and returned afterwards. The Institution supplied
personnel, position papers, and intellectual standing to an administration
committed to deregulation, tax reduction, and a harder line in the Cold War.

That is what Herbert Hoover built the archive for. He founded it in 1919 to
collect the documentary record of how power moves; it became, over sixty years,
an instrument for moving it. Hoover had understood in 1919 that whoever gathers the documents acquires interpretive power. This is the interest payment.

The policy changes of the early 1980s reached the Valley directly.

Capital gains tax rates had been cut in 1978 and were cut again in 1981, which
raised the after-tax return on exactly the kind of long-horizon equity
investment that venture capital makes. Combined with the 1979 ERISA clarification, the effect on the amount of capital available to
venture funds was transformative, and the industry grew by an order of magnitude
across a few years.

Deregulation in telecommunications, finance, and transportation opened markets
that technology firms would later occupy. Defence spending rose sharply, which
flowed to the defence corridor of Sunnyvale and Mountain View.

The administration brought a second Californian export to Washington, and it had
been tested in the state a decade earlier.

Reagan had been governor of California from 1967 to 1975, and the politics that
carried him to Sacramento were substantially about the universities: the
Berkeley Free Speech Movement, the campus unrest of the late 1960s, and a promise to restore order. In office he presided over the beginning of
the end of free tuition in the California public system, which had been a
founding principle of the 1960 Master Plan.

That is a durable shift and it inverted the arrangement Terman had built. The G.I. Bill and the Master Plan had made higher
education a public good financed collectively. From the 1970s the cost migrated
to the student, through fees and then through debt, on the argument that the
private returns to a degree were substantial and the beneficiary should pay.

The argument has force. It also converts a credential from something a society
provides into something an individual purchases, which changes who can obtain
one and what they must do afterwards to service the purchase.

Proposition 13, passed by California voters in 1978, capped property taxes and
required supermajorities for new taxes, permanently constraining the revenue
base of the state's schools and local government. It was the most influential
state ballot measure in modern American history and it was widely imitated.

So the same decade that vastly increased the flow of public research money into
a private university's patent portfolio also began withdrawing public support
from the system that had made California's technical workforce possible. Both
movements originated in California and both went national.

What assembled in California in these years is a coherent position, and the
chapter's obligation is to describe it without sneering, because most of the
people holding it hold it sincerely.

It runs roughly as follows. Markets allocate better than governments. Regulation
protects incumbents and suppresses innovation. Individual technical talent is
the scarce resource and should be rewarded accordingly. The people who build
valuable things deserve what they earn, and the mechanism that identifies them
is approximately fair.

Set against that the material in this act. The semiconductor industry's early
volume came from federal procurement. The network came from a defence agency.
The research came from federal grants routed through a university, on land
granted to a railroad. The capital came substantially from pension funds
admitted by a regulatory reinterpretation. The patents on which the biotech
industry was built came from federally funded work, assigned to universities by
statute.

The contradiction is not hypocrisy, and calling it hypocrisy would be the lazy
version. It is something more interesting: a genuine belief in market
allocation, held by people whose own position was produced by an unusually large
and sustained programme of public investment, who did not experience that
investment as such because it arrived through institutions — a university, a
research contract, a pension allocation — rather than as a cheque.

Public subsidy that arrives through an institution is invisible to the person it
benefits. That is the most durable finding in this book.

## Bechtel

Caspar Weinberger became Secretary of Defense in 1981. George Shultz became Secretary of State in 1982. Both men had been executives at Bechtel, the San Francisco engineering firm that had grown on public-works contracts the way the railroad had grown on land grants. Shultz — "the outsider and former academic" — had become president of Bechtel a year after joining the company. 

Shultz told his 1982 nomination hearing that "Private interests have no business with nuclear weapons." 

Bechtel had business with nuclear weapons. The line is worth holding in mind.

The firm's social world included the Bohemian Grove, the private campground in Monte Rio where California's corporate and political leadership met each summer. Nixon's Watergate tapes record him calling the Grove "the most faggy goddamned thing you could ever imagine," in a passage that also describes Bechtel courting Shultz there. 

One detail, sourced once, is enough. The point is not the campground. The point is that the cabinet and the contractor drank from the same cup.

Shultz would later chair the board of the Hoover Institution on the Stanford campus. Weinberger would help steer defence spending back toward the firms that had trained them. The road, the university, the archive, and the cabinet were the same circuit, opened wider in 1981.

\newpage



## BackRub

**Stanford, California. January 1997.**

The Gates Computer Science building was quiet after hours, but the network was not. Larry Page had been crawling the web from Stanford's machines for months. He called the project BackRub. 

The name was literal. A search engine that ranked pages by who linked to them was measuring the web's back links — the citations of the open internet, the way footnotes work in a journal. Sergey Brin had joined the effort; together they were graduate students on federally supported research, turning a library problem into an engineering one.

On the public project page, still hosted under the Stanford domain that winter, BackRub described itself as a "web crawler" built for the Digital Library Project in the computer science department. It promised a demo searching titles across more than sixteen million URLs. A note apologized for outages: a local network failure beyond the team's control had taken the service down on December 4, 1997. The crawl was not a secret hobby. It was visible, documented, and hungry for bandwidth the department could measure. (Stanford BackRub site, Wayback Machine, 10 Dec 1997)

## The insight

Search engines in 1997 ranked pages primarily by the text on them, which meant anyone could rank highly by repeating a word. PageRank used a different signal: the structure of links between pages. A page linked to by many pages is probably useful, and a link from a page that is itself heavily linked counts for more than one from a page nobody reads. It is a recursive measure of authority, and it was much harder to fake because it depended on the behaviour of other people. 

The results were obviously better, and anyone who used the web in 1998 remembers the difference.

Page had started from academic citations. Brin brought the statistical machinery. Their adviser, Terry Winograd, had seen search as a serious research direction when many faculty still treated the web as a toy. The NSF-backed Digital Libraries work gave them cover, computers, and time. It also gave Stanford a claim.

## Excite

Vinod Khosla was on the board of Excite, the portal that had grown out of the same Stanford orbit a few years earlier. Khosla had co-founded Sun Microsystems and was now a partner at Kleiner Perkins on Sand Hill Road — the same corridor Eugene Kleiner had opened in 1972, a few minutes from the quad. 

Page wanted to finish his doctorate. He also believed BackRub could make money immediately if someone with traffic would license it. In January 1997 he emailed Khosla a plan for Excite to buy the technology. "Excite's total outlay would be $1.6 million, including $300,000 to Stanford for the license." 

Stanford's cut sat inside the pitch like a line item on a grocery receipt. The university was not an afterthought. It was part of the price.

Khosla arranged the meeting that mattered. Excite had taken venture money and, like many Valley firms of the period, had been told to hire adult supervision. George Bell, a magazine executive, ran the company now. In Bell's office the Stanford team opened two windows on the same monitor: Excite's search and BackRub's. They typed the query "Internet."

Levy's account of what happened next is a parable about the wrong metric. Excite's results, according to the contemporaries Levy interviewed, surfaced pages where the English word appeared among unrelated text. BackRub returned pages that actually explained how to use browsers — the answer a person typing "Internet" in 1997 likely wanted. Bell worried that a search engine which solved the query too well would send users away. Portals in that era sold advertising against time on site; relevance was secondary to stickiness.

Excite passed.

Khosla reportedly floated a lower cash figure — seven hundred fifty thousand dollars in some accounts — but no deal closed. The road had seen the algorithm. The portal chose its business model instead.

## Reimers

Niels Reimers was not in the room at Excite. He did not need to be. His office at Stanford's technology licensing shop had already learned the Cohen–Boyer lesson: federally funded science, filed early, licensed broadly, could return hundreds of millions to the university. 

When Reimers filed on recombinant DNA, critics inside Stanford said the application risked undermining the university's fundamental mission. The same tension applied to PageRank. A public institution was about to patent the map of the public web, developed on its machines with public grants, and lease it back to its own students.

Reimers's structural answer, reduced to its bones, was that if Stanford did not capture the title someone else would. Whether that justified the capture is a moral question. The institutional question was simpler: Bayh–Dole had made capture legal, and capture was now routine.

## Stanford's position

The university owned the patent.

PageRank was developed on federally funded research at Stanford — the Digital Libraries project, supported by the National Science Foundation among others — and under the Bayh–Dole arrangement the university took title. It would license the patent to the company exclusively, in exchange for fees and shares. Patent 6,285,999, "Method for node ranking in a linked database," lists Lawrence Page as inventor and Stanford as assignee.

The equity leg mattered as much as the license fee. Chronologies of the deal report roughly 1.8 million shares to Stanford in exchange for exclusive rights — the university becoming a shareholder in the chokepoint its own students built. After the 2004 initial public offering, Stanford would sell most of those shares for on the order of three hundred thirty-six million dollars. 

That transaction is the entire book in a single line item. Federal research funding produces an invention; a statute assigns the invention to the university; the university licenses it to a company founded by its own doctoral students; the company becomes one of the most valuable enterprises on earth; and the university collects a nine-figure return.

Public money in, private monopoly out, institution enriched, elite reproduced. There is no step in that sequence that is hidden, illegal, or even unusual.

## The paper

The paper that described Google contains an appendix explaining why Google should not exist.

Sergey Brin and Larry Page published "The Anatomy of a Large-Scale Hypertextual Web Search Engine" in 1998. Its final appendix argues that advertising-funded search engines are inherently biased toward the advertisers and away from the needs of consumers, that the bias is insidious because it is difficult for users to detect, and that these incentives mean a search engine of this kind will be crafted to serve the advertiser rather than the searcher. 

They concluded that it is crucial to have a competitive search engine that is transparent and in the academic realm.

Then they built the largest advertising-funded search engine in history.

They would need a garage, venture capital on Sand Hill Road, and a business model before the appendix caught up with them. Excite had already shown what the university's three-hundred-thousand-dollar line item looked like when a buyer walked away. The next buyer would not walk away. The shares would matter more than the license fee.

Nobody in Bell's office knew they had just declined the passage everyone would soon need to pay for.

## The department notices

Graduate students had run heavy jobs on shared clusters before. Page and Brin's crawl was different because it did not end. The web in 1997 was smaller than it would become, but it was already larger than any single machine could hold in memory. Every pass across the graph added disks, threads, and outbound requests that showed up in departmental logs.

Administrators asked polite questions first. How long would the experiment run? Could the crawl be throttled? Could the index live on machines paid for by the Digital Library grant rather than by the general pool? The questions were reasonable. They were also previews of the fights the company would later have with universities, publishers, and governments: who pays for the copy, and who owns the index?

Page and Brin were not the first Stanford students to treat the campus as a laboratory for a product. Phil Knight had written a shoe paper in a classroom a generation earlier. Bob Swanson had walked out of Kleiner Perkins with recombinant DNA in his pocket. The difference in 1997 was scale on the network. A crawl was not a pair of Tigers in a trunk. It was a continuous drain on a commons everyone else needed for email.

## What Excite bought instead

Excite was not stupid. It was a child of the same soil: Stanford engineers, venture money, a portal strategy built for an era when nobody knew which page a user would open first. Portals wanted search as a feature, not as a destination. They wanted users to linger among banner ads, chat rooms, and headlines assembled by editors.

Bell's stickiness argument, as Levy reconstructs it, was honest about the business. A perfect search engine is a exit door. Excite's shareholders had paid for a hotel lobby.

Khosla's role in the episode is a reminder that Sand Hill Road is not only a funder of new companies. It is a board member of old ones, a broker between graduate students and incumbents, a person who can schedule the meeting where a crawl becomes a acquisition target. When Excite said no, Khosla did not stop taking calls from Page. The road keeps the relationship even when the deal dies.

## Patent pending

By the time the 1998 paper appeared at a conference, the commercial path was already drawn. Stanford filed for patent protection on PageRank; the inventors listed Page; the assignee was the Board of Trustees. The exclusive license to Google would follow incorporation, but the direction of travel was set: the university would not release the method into the public domain the way early internet protocols had been released.

Defenders of Bayh–Dole would later point to Genentech and to the non-exclusive Cohen–Boyer licenses as proof that the system could spread wealth without creating monopolies. Search would be a harder case. One winner could index the whole web; network effects would concentrate queries on a single engine; the patent was a moat around the moat.

Brin and Page's appendix warned that advertising would distort rankings. They were writing as academics. Reimers was filing as a landlord. Kleiner Perkins was listening as an investor. Each was telling the truth about a different part of the same machine.

## The name on the door

Google was still months away. BackRub still lived on a Stanford server, with a banner announcing the Digital Library Project. The Excite number — $1.6 million, $300,000 to Stanford — had failed to clear Bell's stickiness test.

The algorithm worked. The portal metrics said that was the problem.

In a few months the project would leave the `.stanford.edu` address. It would not leave the institution. The shares were already implicit in the license Reimers was preparing. Sand Hill Road had seen the demo. The department had seen the bandwidth bill.

The next chapter would be a garage with a rent receipt, and venture partners meeting in glass-walled offices four minutes from the quad where the crawl began.

## AltaVista and the old gods

Before Google, the web had other priests. AltaVista, born in a Palo Alto lab, had taught users that search could be fast. Yahoo had taught them that search could be a feature inside a directory. Excite had taught them that search could be bundled with news and weather and stock quotes.

Each incumbent optimized for the business it already had. None wanted an engine so honest that users left immediately. Page and Brin optimized for relevance because they were not yet responsible for banner-ad revenue on a portal homepage. They were responsible for a dissertation committee, a licensing office, and a venture capitalist who had watched Excite say no.

The bake-off in Bell's office was the hinge. If Excite had bought BackRub for $1.6 million — if the three hundred thousand to Stanford had cleared — the algorithm might have been smothered inside a stickiness metric before the public ever learned to type a standalone URL. The failure of the sale preserved the engine for a company that would later make leaving the site the whole point.

That was luck only from Excite's perspective. From Stanford's perspective it was the second chance to price the same patent. From Sand Hill's perspective it was proof that the best deals are the ones the incumbents reject.

Winograd had framed the work as library science while the crawl still fit inside the Digital Library mandate. The market would not cooperate. When the crawl hit sixteen million URLs on the public demo, the project was already larger than most startups' products. The department's network failure notice on December 4 was a reminder that infrastructure built for teaching was carrying freight meant for commerce.

Page kept a copy of the Excite email with the $300,000 line item. Brin kept improving the ranking. Khosla kept returning calls. Reimers kept filing. The road kept collecting tolls whether or not the portal understood it was standing on a bridge.

## Brin

Sergey Brin arrived in the story as the partner who could make Page's idea run at scale — rewriting code, arguing about mathematics, testing the ranking on live queries at odd hours. Their dynamic was legendarily abrasive in the way graduate partnerships often are: two people who disagree in private so the product can agree in public.

Brin had come to Stanford from Maryland by way of an undergraduate degree and a family that had fled the Soviet Union — a biography that would later be packaged for IPO roadshows as evidence of American meritocracy. In 1997 he was simply another student on the cluster, watching the crawl eat disks.

When Excite passed, both men still had the option of finishing doctorates and publishing more papers. The failure of the sale did not force incorporation immediately. It forced a question: if portals would not buy relevance, who would pay for it? The answer would take another year and a garage. The question itself was already worth more than Bell had offered.

## The Digital Library grant

The NSF line on the grant paperwork was bland: improve access to information. The implementation was a bot that copied the web. Every federal dollar that paid for graduate student time on the cluster was a dollar the public had already spent before any venture capitalist wrote a check. Bayh–Dole then assigned the output to the university. Sand Hill Road would later assign the valuation.

That sequence — public grant, private title, venture price, public markets — was not a betrayal of Stanford's mission in the eyes of the people running the licensing office. It was the mission after 1980. Cohen–Boyer had proved the model in biology. BackRub would prove it in links. The only surprise was how quickly the second proof scaled.

In January 1997 the Excite email put a number on the proof: $1.6 million total, $300,000 to Stanford. The number would look quaint before the decade ended. In the moment it was enormous — enough to buy a portal's attention, enough to make the university's share visible before incorporation, enough to show anyone reading the thread that the institution intended to be paid.

The crawl would keep running after Excite said no. The department would keep noticing. Kleiner Perkins would keep its office on Sand Hill Road, four minutes from the machine room where the web was being copied one link at a time.

\newpage



## The garage

**Menlo Park, California. September 1998.**

Susan Wojcicki rented rooms in her house on Santa Margarita Avenue to make mortgage payments. She charged Google $1,700 a month to rent the garage and several rooms. 

The garage myth had a rent receipt.

Larry Page and Sergey Brin incorporated Google on September 4, 1998. The index already worked better than anything else on the web. They were still Stanford graduate students, still using the story of academic search, still holding the warning in their own published appendix that advertising would distort results. The company name was a joke about scale — a googol of pages — misspelled into a trademark.

Inside the house on Santa Margarita, servers stacked where a car should have been, the company looked like every Valley origin story: young engineers, cheap rent, an algorithm that worked. Outside, the institution that owned the algorithm was preparing the license that would make the garage a branch office of the university's portfolio.

## Sand Hill again

The venture meetings happened in the glass-walled buildings in Menlo Park's Sand Hill Road. 

Kleiner Perkins and Sequoia Capital both invested in June 1999 — rival firms sharing a round, which Valley gossip treated as a sign that the deal was obvious. The money was not only money. It was board discipline, hiring introductions, and credibility with the next fund on the road. Tom Perkins's firm sat four minutes from the quad where the crawl had started. Don Valentine's firm had funded Apple and Cisco from the same oak-shaded parking lots. The gate recognized its own.

John Doerr, Kleiner's best-known partner, would join Google's board and become one of the company's outward faces in the press. The term sheets those firms used had been drafted a few miles away, at Wilson Sonsini and at the banks that lent against venture signatures rather than against revenue. A startup's institutional world still fit inside a short drive.

Stanford's technology licensing office finalized the PageRank deal in parallel: exclusive license, cash, and equity. The university was now both landlord of the research and shareholder of the product. When the company later raised larger rounds, the same road that had financed Genentech from a fired associate's desperation now financed search from a graduate project that had already failed to sell itself to Excite.

## Leaving the campus

Page and Brin did not drop out immediately. The transition from dissertation to company was negotiated the way Reimers negotiated patents — slowly, with lawyers, with the institution's cut explicit. They moved off the departmental machines that had triggered bandwidth complaints and onto hardware paid for by investors. The BackRub demo page on Stanford's server would become a historical artifact; the crawl would continue on private capacity.

The culture they brought with them was academic in one way that would matter for decades: they believed measurement could settle arguments. They also brought the portal era's blind spot Bell had articulated — that keeping users inside a property might conflict with answering questions honestly. They would resolve the tension in favor of answers, then sell the answers.

## The revenue problem

Google's initial business plan was to license its search technology to portals, and it did — to Yahoo, among others. The revenue was insufficient to support the infrastructure the index required, and the infrastructure requirement grew with the web. They tried other approaches. The venture funding brought expectations of a real business model. 

Advertising was what worked, and it worked so well that within a few years no alternative was conceivable.

The turn was not cynical if you credit the constraints. Serving the whole web required clusters of machines that doubled in size faster than licensing fees grew. Portal partners wanted white-label search, not a rival brand. The investors on Sand Hill Road wanted a margin that could scale with queries, not a fee per partner. The appendix in the 1998 paper had described the hazard of mixing search with ads. The balance sheet described the hazard of mixing search with insolvency.

## AdWords

Advertisers bid on search terms. But the auction did not simply sell the top position to the highest bidder. Google introduced a quality score — a measure of how likely users were to click a given advertisement — and ranked ads by the combination of bid and quality. An advertiser with a more relevant advertisement could outrank one paying more. 

Advertisers paid per click rather than per impression, which meant they paid only when the system had actually delivered someone.

The result aligned three interests that ordinarily conflict. Users got advertisements that were more relevant. Advertisers paid for outcomes rather than exposure. And Google captured a share of the value of every commercial intention expressed anywhere in its index.

Refined between 2000 and 2002, the auction was elegant in the way railroad tariffs were elegant: a small rule change at the bottleneck reshaped everything downstream. Excite had wanted stickiness. Google sold departure — then taxed the departure at the moment of intent.

## Intent as an asset

A magazine advertisement reaches people who happen to be reading a magazine. A television advertisement reaches people who happen to be watching. Both are sold on demographics, which is a guess about who is present. 

A search query is a person stating, in their own words, at a specific moment, what they are trying to find. It is a declared intention, timestamped and immediately actionable, and its commercial value is correspondingly enormous.

Google did not invent human intention. It occupied the position between a stated desire and the market that could satisfy it, and priced the passage — which is what the Southern Pacific did to freight, what the studios did to films, and what Microsoft did to applications.

Each of these is the same toll in a different medium. This one turned out to be the largest, because everyone wants something.

## The infrastructure underneath

Serving search at web scale on commodity hardware required a different kind of computing, and Google built it: enormous numbers of inexpensive machines, software that assumed individual machines would fail constantly, and distributed systems for storing and processing data across all of them. The published papers describing these systems in the early 2000s were read very widely and were reimplemented in the open, which is where a substantial part of the modern data infrastructure industry comes from. 

The company then built its own data centres, sited for electricity and cooling along rivers and in counties that wanted the tax base, and eventually designed its own chips. A company that begins by owning an index ends by owning the physical capacity to compute at scale. The garage on Santa Margarita was already a fiction by the time the first custom silicon arrived.

## The excluded

Users, whose behavioural data constitutes the asset and who receive the service in exchange, on terms nobody negotiated and few have read.

Publishers, whose content is the raw material of the index and whose relationship with their own audiences was intermediated by a company that eventually competed with them for the same advertising revenue.

And advertisers, bidding in an auction whose rules and reserve prices they cannot inspect — a fact that has been the subject of litigation on both sides of the Atlantic.

The toll had moved again. It was no longer on the rail. It was on the question.

Susan Wojcicki would later run the company's advertising business and then YouTube. The woman who cashed the seventeen-hundred-dollar garage check became one of the people who sold the intention the garage had been built to index. Stanford kept the shares. Sand Hill kept the board seats. The road kept the gate.

## Yahoo and the licensing winter

Before AdWords, there was Yahoo. Google's first real revenue came from powering other people's search boxes — a subcontractor to portals that still believed they owned the customer relationship. Yahoo drove traffic; Google drove quality; the checks were too small to fund the index doubling every few months.

Levy's account of the period is a sequence of near-miss business models: custom search appliances, enterprise contracts, sponsorships that did not scale. Each attempt treated search as a component. The breakthrough treated search as the marketplace itself — every query an auction lot.

The portal era ended slowly, then all at once. Users learned to type `google.com` instead of waiting for a portal to curate the morning. Defaults on browsers and toolbars became the new stickiness. Google paid for placement where Excite had refused to buy relevance. The company that Bell thought was too good at answering questions became too good at owning the moment the question was asked.

## June 1999

When Kleiner Perkins and Sequoia closed the June 1999 round, the company was less than a year old and already famous among technologists. The valuation was hot by the standards of the time; the terms were standard by the standards of Sand Hill Road: preferred shares, board seats, protective provisions, the right to block a sale that did not return the fund's model.

Doerr's presence on the board linked Google to the lineage of Netscape, Amazon, and Sun — firms that had taught the Valley how to scale on venture gasoline. Valentine's Sequoia linked it to Apple and Cisco — firms that had taught the Valley how to survive the transition from product to platform.

Stanford sat on the cap table through the licensing office, not through the venture funds. That separation mattered publicly — the university could claim it had enabled research, not picked winners — and it mattered privately, because the shares were worth more than any royalty the OTL had ever collected from a single license.

## Quality score

The AdWords auction is easier to describe than to invent. Bidding alone produces garbage: the highest bidder might be the least relevant page. Google's quality score reweighted bids by predicted click-through rate, rewarding ads that users actually wanted to click. Advertisers paid per click, not per impression, which aligned Google's revenue with demonstrated intent rather than with accidental eyeballs.

The mechanism turned the search box into a commodities exchange where the commodity was a moment of desire. Farmers' markets price apples by weight. AdWords priced curiosity by the word.

## Stanford's sale

After the August 2004 initial public offering, Stanford began selling its Google shares into the market. Press accounts and university chronologies place the proceeds in the hundreds of millions — a nine-figure return on OTL paperwork and on NSF grants that had paid graduate students to improve libraries.

The transaction closed the loop Reimers had opened with Cohen–Boyer: public science, private title, public markets, institutional endowment. The difference was concentration. Biotech could host many firms on one patent family. Search, at scale, wanted one index.

## Bell's echo

George Bell had wanted a search engine that was eighty percent as good as the competition — good enough to keep users wandering the lobby. Google built an engine that was better than good enough to leave, then built an advertising machine that profited from the leaving.

Excite would merge, stumble, and disappear from the daily life of the internet. The portal Bell protected would lose to the search box. The road that had funded Excite would fund Google's competitors and then fund Google's acquirers and then fund the next generation of founders who met in the same glass-walled buildings Levy described on Sand Hill Road.

## Night on Santa Margarita

In the first months on Wojcicki's property, the company was still small enough that the founders cooked meals, argued about ranking tweaks, and slept near the humming drives. The mythmakers would later photograph the garage as if it were a birthplace independent of Stanford. The rent receipt said otherwise: a landlord, a mortgage, a suburban street minutes from the investors who would price the round.

The street signs on the way to Sand Hill Road already named railroad men, university presidents, and donors. Page and Brin drove past them without reading. They were building the index that would make reading optional — the answer before the question finished typing.

The appendix still said advertising would distort search. The auction made distortion profitable. The shares made distortion permanent for the institution that had hosted BackRub when the network failed on December 4, 1997, and apologized on a Stanford web page that the failure was beyond the students' control.

When the company moved out of the garage, it did not move out of the corridor. Every later round, every default payment, every data-centre acre would still route through the same geography: graduate students from the Gates building, term sheets from Sand Hill, patents from the OTL, pension money from the ERISA clarification that had enlarged the funds waiting in those glass-walled offices. The garage was a photograph. The gate was the business. PayPal's parking lot was next, and the young men there would learn the same lesson in money before they learned it in politics. The road would fund both exits.

## The IPO line

When Google went public in 2004, the registration statement listed Stanford among the largest beneficiaries of the licensing deal — not as a footnote, but as a shareholder whose sales would be watched by analysts. The endowment's gain was not charity. It was rent on the algorithm, collected after the public had paid for the research that trained the inventors.

Knight would later return to campus to dedicate a business school building named for his shoe fortune. Page and Brin would return as trustees, donors, and symbols. The garage on Santa Margarita would be photographed for annual reports. The rent receipt would disappear into myth.

The road would remember all of it as entrepreneurship. The ledger would remember it as succession.

\newpage



## PayPal

**Palo Alto, California. 1999.**

Elon Musk had enrolled at Stanford for a PhD in applied physics in 1995. "As the story goes," Ashlee Vance writes, Musk dropped out after two days to start Zip2.  By 1999 he was running X.com on University Avenue, two blocks from where Hoover had once fed Belgium from an archive crate.

Musk's joy when the X.com debit card successfully spit out money at an ATM close to the company's Palo Alto office is preserved in a photograph in Jimmy Soni's account of the period. 

The product was money by email. The fight was over who would own the network.

PayPal was not, by the standards of what followed, a large success. Confinity
and Elon Musk's X.com merged in 2000 after a difficult period, the combined
company went public in 2002, and eBay acquired it later that year for roughly
$1.5 billion. The business itself was frequently in trouble — fraud losses,
dependence on a single marketplace, and a management fight that removed Musk as
chief executive while he was on a plane.

 The proceeds and the relationships from
that one exit capitalised Tesla, SpaceX, LinkedIn, Palantir, YouTube, Yelp, and

A ruling class assembled from one company's payroll. This book's thesis, running

How does a failed startup become a ruling class?

In 2007 *Fortune* photographed a group of former PayPal employees dressed as a

label the PayPal Mafia.

It was a joke about a real thing, and by naming it the magazine made it more so.
A network that has been told it is a network behaves like one.

PayPal was not, by the standards of what its alumni went on to build, a large
success.

Confinity was founded in 1998 by Max Levchin and Peter Thiel, initially to do
cryptography and payments on handheld devices, and it developed a way to send
money by email. X.com was founded by Elon Musk in 1999 as an online financial
services company. Both were in Palo Alto, both were chasing the same emerging
market in payments for online auctions, and in 2000 they merged.

The merged company then had a difficult year. There was a management fight; Musk
was removed as chief executive while he was on a plane, and Thiel took over.
Fraud losses were severe enough to threaten the business, and Levchin's team
built the detection systems that eventually contained them. The company depended
almost entirely on eBay for transaction volume, and eBay was building a
competitor.

PayPal went public in February 2002 and was acquired by eBay in October of that
year for roughly one and a half billion dollars in stock.

That is a good outcome and an unremarkable one. Several dozen companies of the
period did comparably well, and most of their alumni went on to ordinary
careers.

What made this one different is what the participants did with the proceeds and
with each other.

Musk put most of his share into SpaceX, founded in 2002, and into Tesla, where
he led the Series A in 2004 and became chairman. Thiel founded Palantir in 2003
and the hedge fund Clarium, and in 2004 made the first outside investment in
Facebook, half a million dollars for a stake that eventually returned around a
billion. Reid Hoffman, who had been chief operating officer, founded LinkedIn in
2002. Chad Hurley, Steve Chen, and Jawed Karim, who had worked at the company,
founded YouTube in 2005. Jeremy Stoppelman and Russel Simmons founded Yelp in
2004, with Levchin backing it. Levchin later founded Affirm. Roelof Botha became
a partner at Sequoia and then its senior steward. Keith Rabois invested and
operated across a dozen of the others. Thiel and others founded Founders Fund in
2005.

They invested in each other's companies, sat on each other's boards, recruited
from each other, and introduced each other to capital. Facebook's first outside
money came from this group. So did a substantial share of the early rounds of a
generation of companies.

Consider what the group actually held after 2002. They had capital, which is
useful and replaceable. They had shared operating experience of scaling a
business through fraud, regulation, and a near-death funding environment, which
is rarer. And they had a calibrated view of each other: they knew who was good
at what, who could be relied on, and who could be backed without diligence.

That last item is the valuable one. The fundamental problem in early-stage
investing is that there is no information — no revenue, no product, sometimes no
company. Everything turns on an assessment of people, and assessment is
expensive. A group that has already assessed each other under pressure can move
faster and with more conviction than anyone outside it, and speed and conviction
are what win competitive deals.

This is a credentialling system with no examination and no institution. Stanford had been built to issue exactly that kind of certificate. This is the same function performed by a shared employment
history and a photograph.

The group also holds, in substantial part, a coherent set of political
commitments.

Thiel has been explicit for two decades: a scepticism about the compatibility of
democracy and freedom as he defines them, a conviction that competition is
overrated and monopoly is the correct objective for a business, and a belief
that technological progress has stalled outside computing. His 2014 book states
the monopoly argument directly, and it is a coherent position rather than a
slip.

He funded a Senate campaign in 2022 that reached the vice presidency two years
later. Others in the group have funded candidates, founded political
organisations, and acquired media. Musk bought a communications platform in 2022
and subsequently took a role in the federal government.

What it records is that a
group of colleagues from one company's payroll in 2002 held, twenty years later,
positions in defence software, space launch, electric vehicles, social
networking, payments, venture capital, and national politics.

There is a reading of PayPal's founding that is easy to miss and that explains a
great deal about what its alumni did next.

Thiel has said repeatedly that the original ambition was not to build a
convenient way to pay for auction items. It was to create a currency outside the
control of governments — a means of moving value that monetary authorities could
not devalue or block. The payments business was what that ambition became when
it met regulation, the card networks, and the need for revenue.

The company that resulted was thoroughly conventional: it settled in dollars,
partnered with banks, complied with money transmission rules in fifty states,
and was eventually bought by an auction site. The founding intention survived as
disposition rather than as product.

That disposition — a conviction that existing institutions are obstacles rather
than frameworks, and that a sufficiently well-designed private system can route
around them — is visible in almost everything the group built afterwards. A
private launch company competing with a national space programme. A software
firm supplying capabilities that governments could not build themselves. A
currency movement several of them backed early. A social platform acquired and
reoriented by one of them on explicitly political grounds.

view about the inadequacy of public institutions, held by people with the
capital and the network to act on it, produces a systematic pattern of private
substitution.

The efficiency of the network is precisely the problem for everyone outside it.

A founder inside it raises on a phone call. A founder outside it raises through
a process, if at all, and the previous chapters have described what the
composition of that process looks like. The network lowers transaction costs for
its members by raising them, relatively, for everybody else, which is what a
network always does and is the reason concentration compounds.

Admission to Stanford had long been formally open to all and practically available to families who could support years of preparation. This
is the same structure with the examination removed. A ruling class assembled
from one company's staff list, reproducing by introduction.

## October 2002

That is a good outcome and an unremarkable one. Several dozen companies of the period did comparably well.

 Musk put most of his share into SpaceX and Tesla. Thiel founded Palantir in 2003 and made the first outside investment in Facebook in 2004. Hoffman would build LinkedIn. Levchin would start Affirm. The address book became a governing class.

In 2007 *Fortune* photographed a group of former PayPal employees dressed as a crime family and ran the picture under the label the PayPal Mafia.

The young men walked out with cash and a network. The road had done its work again.

Nobody in the parking lot that afternoon knew which of them would sit in a rotunda twenty-three years later, behind a president and ahead of a cabinet.
