# Episode 8: The Open Era

## The rotunda

**Washington, D.C. January 20, 2025.**

The cold was not the story. The seating was.

In the Capitol rotunda, Elon Musk, Mark Zuckerberg, Jeff Bezos, Sundar Pichai, and Tim Cook were placed in the first rows behind Donald Trump's family — ahead of much of the incoming cabinet in the photographs the wire services published that afternoon. The *Wall Street Journal* reported that the chief executives of the world's largest technology companies sat "directly behind Trump" while he took the oath, a prominence the paper contrasted with cabinet secretaries still finding their places.

Musk had spent more than a quarter of a billion dollars to help elect him. Zuckerberg and Bezos had ended years of public friction with Trump and appeared at St. John's Church that morning alongside Cook and Pichai.

Nobody in the rotunda needed a historian to explain what the seating meant. They needed only to look.

Then cut back twenty-one years.

## Palo Alto

**Palo Alto, California. 2004.**

When does subliminal become explicit?

On 20 January 2025, at the presidential inauguration in the Capitol rotunda, the
founders and chief executives of several of the largest technology companies in
the world were seated in front of much of the incoming cabinet.

Everything load-bearing in this chapter comes from disclosure filings, which is
the only responsible way to write about living people in a book making this
argument.

Peter Thiel contributed roughly fifteen million dollars to J. D. Vance's 2022
Senate campaign in Ohio, principally through a supporting committee — one of the
largest sums ever directed by a single donor to a single Senate candidate. Thiel
had employed Vance at his investment firm, had mentored him, and had introduced
him to the network.

Vance won the seat in November 2022. He was elected vice president in November
2024.

That is the network from a single company's payroll in 2002 reaching the second
office of the United States within twenty-two years, and it can be documented
entirely from Federal Election Commission records. No claim about coordination
is required, and none is made.

The broader pattern is visible in the same filings.

Technology executives and investors contributed at scale to committees
supporting the successful campaign, and several who had previously been publicly
associated with the other party or with no party endorsed publicly for the first
time. Elon Musk's contributions through a supporting committee were among the
largest individual political expenditures on record, and he campaigned
personally.

After the inauguration Musk was given a formal advisory position in the federal
government concerned with spending reduction and the reorganisation of federal
agencies, described in official announcements and reported extensively.

Where the position's boundaries were
disputed — and they were, in litigation and in congressional correspondence — it
says so.

What is structurally novel is not that a wealthy person had influence over
government. Collis Huntington had done that in the 1870s, and was more candid about it. What is novel is a serving executive of firms holding major federal
contracts occupying a formal advisory role over the agencies that let them, with
the conflict identified and not resolved.

The argument of this chapter is that January 2025 changed the visibility rather
than the substance, and that this distinction is the whole point of the book.

The relationships were not new. They had been building for two decades in
disclosure filings that almost nobody read, in board seats, in a network that
funded itself, and in an institution that supplied the founders. Every mechanism
this book describes was operating in 2015 exactly as it was operating in 2025.

What ended was the discretion. An elite that once exercised influence through
donations, foundations, and quiet access began exercising it in front of
cameras, on a platform one of them owned, from a seat in the rotunda.

The book's thesis stopped requiring inference and started being televised. It
was equally true the morning before.

There is one further novelty. In 2022 one of the men in the rotunda had bought a
communications platform outright and reoriented its moderation and ranking
policies according to his own stated views. A previous generation of proprietors
bought newspapers, and the comparison is fair as far as it goes. It goes less
far than it looks, because a newspaper's readers knew they were reading a
proprietor's newspaper, and a ranking algorithm's users generally do not
experience the feed as an editorial product at all.

At an event at Capital One Arena on the day of the inauguration, Musk made a
gesture that was immediately and widely discussed, with interpretations
diverging sharply and along predictable lines.

This book does not adjudicate it.

The gesture occurred and was recorded, the dispute about what it meant was
extensive and remains unresolved, and intent is not knowable from a video. The
project's rule for contested material is to state what is documented, name the
disagreement, and build nothing on it, and that rule is applied here.

filing is a fact. A seating chart is a fact. An advisory role established by
executive action is a fact. The gesture is a controversy, and controversies age
badly while filings do not.
Those
have been removed, and the reasoning is recorded in the project's cut-material
log.

The realignment that produced the rotunda seating deserves a section, because it
was not inevitable and happened quickly.

For most of the period since the 1970s, the corridor's politics were broadly
liberal on social questions and libertarian on economic ones, and its money went
predominantly to Democrats. The industry's self-image was progressive, and its
relationship with Democratic administrations was close enough that the traffic
in personnel between them was routine.

Several things changed that. Antitrust enforcement became bipartisan, and the
appointments made after 2021 were more hostile to the industry's structural
position than anything in the preceding forty years. Content moderation became a
partisan battlefield in which the platforms were attacked from both directions
and satisfied neither. Cryptocurrency regulation and the tax treatment of
unrealised gains became salient. And a generation of founders who had
experienced the internal politics of their own companies during the late 2010s
concluded that their workforces were an adversary.

The result was that a portion of the industry's most prominent figures moved,
publicly and rapidly, and the 2024 cycle was the first in which technology money
was a major factor on the Republican side.

It was the same set of positions — light regulation, favourable capital
taxation, resistance to antitrust, and freedom to operate — pursued through a
different party because the other one had stopped supplying them. That is what
an interest does, and describing it as an interest rather than as a conversion
is the analytically accurate move.

A citizen's participation in an American election consists of a vote. In 2022 a
single investor directed fifteen million dollars to a single Senate campaign,
and the candidate became vice president.

Both facts are legal. Both are documented. They are not the same kind of
participation, and the difference is not a matter of opinion.

And the reader should notice what this chapter did not have to do. It made no
claim about a meeting, a plan, or an agreement. It cited filings, a seating
arrangement, and an executive action. The argument of this book does not require
anybody to have conspired, and at the point where the mechanism becomes fully
visible it turns out that nothing needed to be hidden in the first place.

In 1877 there had been a crowd on a San Francisco sandlot being directed
toward the Chinese while the railroad's owners watched from Nob Hill. The
mechanism has not changed. What has changed is that the men on the hill no
longer find it necessary to stay up there.

for what it does?

Facebook grew from a dormitory to global communications infrastructure between
2004 and 2012 through network effects, a real-name policy that made the graph
unusually valuable, and the acquisitions of Instagram and WhatsApp, which
neutralised the two credible challengers. Its advertising model extended
Google's: not stated intent, as in a search query, but inferred disposition
assembled from behaviour.

It is revised continuously. It is published only in part. It is enforced at a
scale that requires automation, and the automation is imperfect in ways that
fall unevenly. And for most of the period this chapter covers, the company that
wrote it insisted that it was not making editorial decisions at all.

Facebook was founded in a Harvard dormitory in 2004 and moved to Palo Alto
within months, into the corridor described in the previous act. Its first
outside investment came from Peter Thiel, from the group.

Its growth mechanics were straightforward and unusually effective. A real-name
policy made the social graph accurate, which made it far more valuable to
advertisers than the pseudonymous networks that preceded it. Expansion school by
school created density before breadth, so that the product was useful on arrival
rather than empty. And network effects did the rest: a social network is worth
what its membership is worth, which means a better competitor with fewer users
is not a better product.

Two acquisitions closed the position. Instagram in 2012, for about a billion
dollars, when it had a small team and no revenue. WhatsApp in 2014, for roughly
nineteen billion. Both prices were widely mocked and both were rational: each
target was the most plausible route by which a competitor could reach comparable
scale.

A search query is a stated intention. A social
feed has no stated intention; the user is not looking for anything. What the
platform sells instead is inferred disposition — an assessment, built from
behaviour, of what a person is like and what they might be susceptible to,
combined with the ability to reach precisely defined slices of the population.

That is a more invasive product than search advertising and a more powerful one
for certain purposes, because it can reach people who were not looking.

Throughout its expansion the company described itself as a technology company
and a neutral platform rather than a media company, and this was not merely
rhetoric. It was load-bearing.

Facebook commissioned an independent human rights impact assessment, published
the same year, which concluded that the platform had been used to incite offline
violence and that the company had not done enough to prevent it. The company
accepted the findings and described measures it was taking.

This is what it means for a platform to be infrastructure. A telephone company
is not responsible for what people say on the phone; it is also not amplifying
selected calls to millions of people because they generate engagement.

The line from the Chinese railroad crews of 1867 is direct. The system's stability depends on
people it does not employ, held at arm's length through a contracting
arrangement, doing the work that makes the product usable and appearing nowhere
in the description of it.

The company disputed the characterisation of the documents and argued that the
selections were unrepresentative, which is a fair objection to make about any
leaked archive and does not dispose of the contents.

## The seeing stone

**Palo Alto, California. 2003.**

Peter Thiel poured his grievances into the *Stanford Review*, the conservative paper he founded that spring at Stanford. 

By 2003 those grievances had a company attached. Thiel named it Palantir after the mythical Elvish "seeing stone." 

---

meaningful. Palantir was founded in Palo Alto in 2003 to build analytical
software for intelligence and security agencies, and it did not pretend
otherwise. When a private firm supplies the system through which an agency
perceives its own problem, the firm is not selling a tool. It is supplying a
faculty.

return of the earlier university-contractor-state triangle in software. Also elite
network reproduction: the founder is from the earlier staff photograph, and
the early funding included the CIA's venture arm.

that would sell it software. Verify the amount and date; the relationship is

the security state. One person occupying three of the book's mechanisms at once.

and then tested against the documents.

intelligence agency and selling analytical capacity back to the government.

In its early years Palantir received investment from In-Q-Tel, the Central
Intelligence Agency's venture capital arm. An intelligence service helped
capitalise a company that would go on to sell it software.

Palantir was founded in Palo Alto in 2003 by Peter Thiel and Alex Karp, and
unlike the platforms in the surrounding chapters it never claimed to be neutral
infrastructure. Its founding argument was that the alternative to wholesale
surveillance was better analysis of data already lawfully held, and the chapter
gives that argument in its strongest form, because it is a serious position and
the chapter is weaker against a straw man.

What the product does is integrate and analyse data across systems that were
never designed to talk to one another. Described plainly and without the
marketing vocabulary, it is a way of making an institution's own information
legible to itself — which places it in a line running back to the mission register and the Hoover archive.

Its customers are intelligence agencies, defence departments, and immigration
enforcement, and the chapter works from contract records with values and dates.
The immigration deployments get the documents first and the company's response
second, in that order.
The structural claim is that when the state's perception of a problem is
mediated by a vendor's model, the vendor is not selling a tool but supplying a
faculty. the earlier university-contractor-state triangle has reassembled in
software, with the same three parties and the same flow of public money.

Peter Thiel appears in this book as a PayPal founder, the principal funder of a
Senate campaign that reached the vice presidency, and the founder of a company
embedded in the security state — one person occupying three of its mechanisms at
once.

The excluded are the people the software is used to find.

What happens when surveillance software sits inside government capacity?

In its early years Palantir took investment from In-Q-Tel, the venture capital
arm of the Central Intelligence Agency.

An intelligence service helped capitalise a company that would sell it software.

Palantir was founded in Palo Alto in 2003 by Peter Thiel, from the group, with Alex Karp, Joe Lonsdale, Stephen Cohen, and Nathan Gettings.

Its founding argument deserves to be stated at full strength, because a chapter
that assumes bad faith would be both unfair and analytically weaker.

The premise was that the post-2001 security environment presented governments
with two bad options. They could refrain from analysing data and accept the
resulting failures, or they could conduct mass surveillance and accept the
resulting damage to civil liberties. Palantir's proposition was a third path:
better analysis of data already lawfully held, with fine-grained access controls
and audit logs, so that investigators could find what they needed without
trawling everything and without the trawling being invisible.

That is a serious argument. Karp has made a version of it publicly for two
decades, and the company's software genuinely does include access control and
auditing features of the kind described.

Stripped of the vocabulary, the product integrates data across systems that were
never designed to speak to each other, and makes the result searchable and
navigable by an analyst.

A government agency typically holds its information in dozens of incompatible
databases, built at different times by different contractors for different
purposes. The practical consequence is that a question spanning more than one of
them cannot be asked. Palantir's business is making the question askable.

Described that way, the lineage in this book is obvious. A mission register in
1776 made a population countable. A Roman census made a province taxable. The
Hoover collection made a period of European history citable. Each converted a
disorganised reality into something an institution could act on, and in each
case the institution's capacity was the capacity to see.

The customer list is a matter of public record through federal contract
databases, and it is intelligence agencies, defence departments, and law
enforcement, alongside a commercial business that the company built later and
has emphasised increasingly.

The most contested contracts are with United States Immigration and Customs
Enforcement, which has used Palantir systems for case management and
investigative work. Documents obtained through freedom of information litigation
and reported over several years established the scope of that work, including
its use in operations against undocumented people and their families.

The company's response has been consistent: it distinguishes between divisions
of the agency, argues that its systems are used for criminal investigations
including human trafficking, and maintains that decisions about enforcement
policy belong to elected governments rather than to vendors. Employees inside
the company have objected internally and some have resigned, and the company has
declined to withdraw.

Here is the structural claim, and it is why this chapter is in this book rather
than in a survey of defence contractors.

When a state's perception of a problem is mediated by a vendor's model, the
vendor is not selling a tool. It is supplying a faculty. What the agency can
see, which patterns it recognises as significant, and which questions occur to
its analysts are shaped by the system through which it looks.

That is a governmental function, and it is being performed under a commercial
contract by an entity that is not subject to freedom of information law, not
directly accountable to any legislature, and answerable to shareholders.

Terman's university-contractor-state triangle had rested on the idea that federal money routed through universities because universities supplied
legitimacy along with talent. This is the same triangle with the legitimacy leg
removed and nothing pretending otherwise. Palantir never claimed to be a neutral
platform. It said from the beginning what it was for.

In that respect it is the most honest company in this book, and the chapter
should say so.

The company remained private for seventeen years, which is unusual, and listed
directly in September 2020 rather than through a conventional offering.

Its revenue figures are in its filings and should be cited from them, by fiscal
year, with the government and commercial segments separated.
What is durable and worth stating is the shape of the business: a long period of
losses funded by private capital while building relationships with agencies
whose procurement cycles are measured in years, followed by profitability at
scale once those relationships matured. That is a model only available to a
company with patient capital and a network that supplies it, which is what the PayPal network supplied.

The obvious question is why a government with the resources of the United States
buys this capability rather than building it, and the answer is the same one Terman's generation gave about universities.

Federal agencies procure software through processes designed to prevent
corruption, which means they are slow, specification-driven, and biased toward
contractors who are good at winning contracts. The resulting projects are
frequently delivered years late and occasionally not at all; the literature on
large federal IT failures is extensive and bipartisan.

Salaries are the other half. A government agency cannot pay what the corridor
pays, cannot grant equity, and cannot hire on the timescale a competitive market
requires. The people capable of building these systems are, with rare
exceptions, not available to it.

So the state rents the capability, and renting a capability is different from
buying a product in one respect that matters: the capability does not accumulate
inside the institution. When the contract ends the knowledge leaves, which makes
the next contract more likely and the dependency deeper.

This is the mechanism that produced the university-contractor arrangement in
1945, the research park in 1951, and the platform provision of public
infrastructure in 2000. Public institutions that cannot compete for talent end
up buying it, and buying it means the capability lives somewhere else
permanently.

One person now appears in three separate mechanisms in this book.

He co-founded a payments company whose alumni network became a governing class.
He made the first outside investment in the social platform of the previous
chapter. He founded a company embedded in the security state. He funded a Senate
campaign in 2022 that reached the vice presidency, which is the next chapter but
three.

It is a description of what a network
looks like when its members are individually very capable and collectively
positioned, and it is the most concentrated single illustration of this book's
argument about elite reproduction.

The people the software is used to find.

They have no relationship with the vendor, no standing to challenge the model,
and in most cases no knowledge that a system exists which has assembled a
picture of them from records held by agencies that never intended those records
to be combined.

The mission register created a population that could be administered. So does
this. The difference is scale, and the fact that the register is now owned by a
company.

\newpage



## Cambridge Analytica

**London / Menlo Park. 2014–2018.**

In March 2018, reporting in the *Observer* and the *New York Times* described how a personality-quiz app had harvested Facebook data and passed it to the political consultancy Cambridge Analytica. On April 4, 2018, Facebook's chief technology officer, Mike Schroepfer, wrote that the company believed the information of up to **87 million** people — mostly in the United States — may have been improperly shared with Cambridge Analytica, a higher figure than the fifty-million estimate in earlier press accounts.

The Federal Trade Commission's July 2019 administrative complaint against Cambridge Analytica alleged that the firm's app harvested profile data from roughly **250,000–270,000** users who installed it and from an additional **50–65 million** "friends" in those users' networks, including at least thirty million identifiable U.S. consumers.

When a platform becomes infrastructure, who is responsible for what it does?

A moderation guideline is a sentence, written by an employee, that determines

The advertising system extended Google's and differed from it in a way that

Section 230, provides that a service is not treated as
the publisher of content supplied by its users. That protection is what made the
business possible at scale. But the same platform was continuously making
editorial decisions — ranking the feed, promoting some material and demoting

description. A system that decides what a billion people see when they open an
application is exercising editorial judgment, whatever the statute calls it. The
company's own engineers understood this; the ranking algorithm was the product.

The position collapsed in public over roughly two years.

The 2016 American election produced sustained scrutiny of political
misinformation and of foreign influence operations conducted through the
platform. In March 2018 reporting in the *Observer* and the *New York Times*
revealed that Cambridge Analytica had obtained data on tens of millions of
Facebook users, harvested through a personality quiz application that had also
collected information about users' friends under permissions the platform had
granted developers.

The company's founder testified before Congress. In July 2019 the Federal Trade
Commission announced a settlement including a five-billion-dollar penalty — at
the time by far the largest privacy-related penalty the agency had imposed —
along with structural requirements on privacy oversight.

The most serious material in this chapter is not about privacy.

In 2018 the United Nations Independent International Fact-Finding Mission on
Myanmar reported on the campaign of violence against the Rohingya and addressed
the role of social media, finding that Facebook had been a significant
instrument for the spread of hate speech in a country where, for many users, the
platform was effectively the internet. The mission's report was direct about the
company's slow and inadequate response.

The structural facts are these. The platform had expanded into a country with
little local-language moderation capacity, in a context of documented ethnic
tension, and the ranking systems that maximised engagement did what such systems
do with inflammatory material. The company was warned by civil society
organisations inside Myanmar years before the crisis and did not act at
sufficient scale.

The people who enforce the rules are largely not employed by the company that
writes them.

Content moderation is performed substantially by contracting firms, in Manila,
in Nairobi, in Hyderabad, in Warsaw, and in Phoenix and Austin, by workers paid
a small fraction of engineering salaries to view the material that is removed.
The psychological consequences are documented in reporting, in litigation, and
in a 2020 settlement with American moderators over trauma-related claims.
Workers in Kenya have brought their own cases.

The internal evidence about how the ranking system behaved became public in
2021, and it is the most useful documentation available anywhere of how a
chokepoint on attention actually operates.

A former employee provided a large volume of internal research to regulators and
to the *Wall Street Journal*, and testified before Congress. The documents
included the company's own studies of the effects of its products, and the
finding that received most attention concerned Instagram's effects on teenage
girls' body image.

The more structurally important material concerned ranking. Internal research
examined how changes to the feed algorithm affected what was distributed, and
documented that content provoking strong reactions travelled further than
content that did not, with predictable consequences for the tone of political
discussion on the platform. Researchers proposed changes; some were adopted and
some were not, and the tradeoff against engagement was explicit in the
deliberations.

What the episode establishes for this book's purposes is narrower than the
headlines and more damaging. The firm knew, in detail, from its own research,
how its ranking systems shaped what people saw and how they behaved, and it made
decisions about that with commercial considerations in the room. That is
editorial judgment by any ordinary meaning of the phrase, exercised at a scale
no publisher in history has approached, by a company whose legal position was
that it was not a publisher.

Not the law, and not the business model.

What changed after 2018 was believability. The company's account of itself — a
neutral technology platform connecting people, with no editorial role and no
responsibility for outcomes — stopped being accepted by regulators, by
journalists, and eventually by a substantial part of its own workforce.

That is the same failure Herbert Hoover suffered in 1932. The machinery was intact and the explanation had stopped working.

The company changed its name in 2021 and continued.

A device is designed in California, fabricated in Taiwan, assembled in China,

The book has already documented the offshore turn twice. Fairchild opened
assembly in Hong Kong in 1963; Nike moved to contract factories through the
1970s. Apple's manufacturing arrangements are the mature form of a decision made
sixty years earlier, and the chapter's first argument is that the dependency was
self-created.

Its second argument is that the relationship is mutual and is narrated as
rivalry. China built manufacturing capability into design capability, partly
with engineers trained in American universities and often in this corridor.
Capital moved in both directions until restrictions closed it. And the genuine
chokepoint — advanced semiconductor fabrication, concentrated in a very small
number of facilities — is one that California does not own, which is the first
time in this book that the state is on the wrong end of a bottleneck.

The strategic response reopened the channel Terman built. Export controls, and
then industrial policy: public money returning to semiconductors at scale,
routed through the same triangle of state demand, corporate production, and
university research, under a new name.

TikTok is the inversion and the chapter treats it as the diagnostic case. It was
the first time a foreign-owned platform occupied a Californian chokepoint, and
the speed and character of the reaction reveal how that position is normally
understood by the people who hold it.

Three groups pay. The supply chain workforce across the Pacific. The American
manufacturing workers whose jobs the offshore turn moved. And Chinese and
Chinese-American researchers placed under security suspicion — the earlier scapegoat mechanism running again, with a different nationality and the same
structure.

What happens when the world's two largest economies share a supply chain and distrust each other?

A phone is designed in California, its processor fabricated in Taiwan, its
components sourced across a dozen countries, its final assembly done in China,
and it is sold everywhere as an American technology product.

Every part of that arrangement was chosen deliberately, for margin, by the firms
that now describe it as a strategic vulnerability.

This book has already documented the offshore turn twice, in two different
industries, in the same years.

Fairchild opened semiconductor assembly in Hong Kong in 1963, because packaging
and testing were labour-intensive and Hong Kong wages were a fraction of
Californian ones. Phil Knight was arranging contract manufacture of shoes in
Japan in the same period, and the production moved to Korea, then Taiwan, then
Indonesia, then China as each became more expensive.

Apple's manufacturing arrangements are the mature form of a decision made sixty
years earlier. The logic never changed: keep the parts that cannot be copied —
design, brand, the operating system, the store — and contract out the parts that
can.

The consequence, compounded over decades, is that the physical capacity to make
things migrated to Asia while the profits stayed in California, and that the
firms holding the profits no longer possess the capacity.

Contract manufacturing at that scale is not a passive activity. It builds
supplier networks, tooling expertise, logistics, and engineering capability, and
after enough iterations the contractor can design as well as build.

China's technology sector by the 2010s was not an assembly economy. It had
platform companies of global scale, a domestic payments and commerce
infrastructure more advanced than the American equivalent in several respects,
and research output in artificial intelligence that by most bibliometric
measures had become one of the two largest in the world.

A substantial number of the people who built it were trained in American
universities, many of them in this corridor. Stanford, Berkeley, and their peers
educated a generation of Chinese engineers and researchers, some of whom stayed
and some of whom returned. That is the elite reproduction mechanism of this book
operating internationally, and it operated in both directions.

There is one bottleneck in the modern technology economy that the Peninsula does
not control, and it is the most important one.

Advanced semiconductor fabrication — the manufacture of the most sophisticated
logic chips — is concentrated in a very small number of facilities,
overwhelmingly in Taiwan and secondarily in South Korea. The equipment required
to make them, particularly extreme ultraviolet lithography systems, is produced
by a single company in the Netherlands.

This is the first time in this book that California is on the wrong end of a
chokepoint, and the reaction to that discovery is the substance of the chapter's
second half.

The concentration happened for the reasons the fabless model made inevitable.
Moore's law required exponentially rising capital investment in fabrication, the
number of firms that could afford a leading-edge plant fell steadily, and
American firms increasingly chose to design rather than manufacture. The fabless
model that made Nike's margins and then the chip designers' margins produced, at
the end, a dependency on a small island that a rival power claims.

The response was industrial policy, and it is the earlier triangle reassembled
under a new name.

Export controls restricted the sale of advanced chips and chipmaking equipment
to China, tightened repeatedly from 2022. The CHIPS and Science Act, signed in
August 2022, committed tens of billions of dollars in federal money to
semiconductor manufacturing and research in the United States.

Public money, allocated by the state, to private firms, to build capacity deemed
strategically necessary, with universities attached for the research component.
That is the Pacific Railway Act's structure with silicon instead of track, and
the argument for it is the same argument: the private market will not build this
on the timescale the country requires.

The clearest diagnostic of how the position is understood came when somebody
else occupied it.

TikTok, owned by a Chinese company, became one of the most used applications in
the United States, particularly among younger users. The response was sustained
political pressure culminating in federal legislation requiring divestiture, on
national security grounds relating to data access and the possibility of content
manipulation.

The concerns are not frivolous. What the episode demonstrates is what the
American political system believes about platform power when the platform
belongs to someone else: that a foreign-controlled algorithm shaping what a
hundred million citizens see is a matter of national security requiring
emergency legislation.

Applied consistently, that belief has implications for domestically owned
platforms shaping what citizens see in dozens of other countries.

There is a second flow the chapter should record, because it complicates the
rivalry framing usefully.

Californian technology companies spent two decades trying to enter the Chinese
market, and the terms on offer were the mirror image of the arrangement they
enjoyed elsewhere.

Google operated a censored Chinese search service from 2006 and withdrew in 2010
after a series of intrusions and a decision it framed publicly as a matter of
principle. Facebook was blocked and courted the market for years without
success. Apple stayed, and the price of staying was compliance: hosting Chinese
users' iCloud data in China under local partnership, removing applications from
the Chinese store at official request, and manufacturing at a scale that made it
deeply exposed to the same jurisdiction.

The pattern reveals what platform power actually rests on. In most of the world
these firms set terms because no state was willing or able to impose its own. In
China a state was both, and the firms either left or complied. Nothing about the
technology changed; the difference was entirely a matter of who held sovereign
authority and was prepared to use it.

That is worth holding onto, because it is the clearest available evidence
against the proposition that platform dominance is a force of nature. It is a
relationship with public authority, and where public authority asserted itself,
the relationship changed.

The supply chain workforce, across a Pacific manufacturing system whose
conditions have been documented repeatedly — long hours, restrictive contracts,
and in the electronics assembly sector a series of well-publicised crises that
produced audits, commitments, and incremental improvement without changing the
underlying arrangement.

American manufacturing workers, whose jobs the offshore turn moved, and whose
communities absorbed the adjustment. The economic literature on the scale and
persistence of that dislocation is substantial and the political consequences
are not in dispute.

And researchers of Chinese origin working in American institutions, subjected
from 2018 to a federal programme of investigations into undisclosed foreign
affiliations that produced a small number of convictions, a larger number of
abandoned prosecutions and ruined careers, and was wound down in 2022 after
sustained criticism that it had operated on ethnicity.

That last item is 1882 running again with a different nationality:
recruit a population, depend on its work, and when the strategic weather
changes, treat it as the vulnerability. The pattern has now repeated with
Chinese, Japanese, Filipino, Mexican, and Chinese labour again across a hundred
and fifty years in the same state.

## The Daily

**Stanford, California. November 2022.**

"I called Sam, the Daily editor. 'Holy shit,' he said. 'We need to get going.'" 

Theo Baker was a freshman. The president of the university was Marc Tessier-Lavigne.

---

litigation reached the Supreme Court. None of it changed the machine. All of it
changed whether the machine's outputs are taken on faith.

the institution's response is restructuring rather than reform.

newspaper as the effective mechanism of accountability.

applicants on the wrong side of admissions decisions in either direction.

a period of sustained reputational damage.

The most consequential piece of accountability journalism about Stanford in a
decade was written by undergraduates at the *Stanford Daily*, and it ended a
presidency. Marc Tessier-Lavigne resigned on 19 July 2023 following a
university-commissioned review of papers he had co-authored.

Its structural interest is not the scandal. The credential's entire value rests
on the institution's claim to verify — to check work, to certify people, to be
the thing a stranger can rely on. A verification failure at the top of the
institution is a failure of the product, which is why this episode belongs in a
book about chokepoints rather than in a book about misconduct.

Two other pressures run alongside. The Hoover Institution's political role
became a campus fight over whether a university claiming disinterest can house
an explicitly committed research institute, and the chapter states both
positions fairly. And admissions came under sustained scrutiny, through the 2019
admissions bribery scandal and the 2023 Supreme Court decision, raising the
question of what the credential screens for and who still believes the answer.

Junior researchers dependent on principal investigators bear
most of the risk.

Then it reports what did not change. Applications, endowment, licensing revenue,
and placement all held. The pipeline is the same; the trust in the pipeline is
not. When trust erodes, the elite stops bothering to be subtle.

What happens when the legitimacy engine loses credibility?

The most consequential piece of accountability journalism about Stanford in a
generation was written by undergraduates.

In November 2022 the *Stanford Daily* published an investigation into research
papers co-authored by the university's president, Marc Tessier-Lavigne, raising
questions about manipulated images in work from laboratories he had led. The
reporting was led by a freshman.

Eight months later he resigned.

Precision matters here more than anywhere else in this act, because this is
where the coverage was loosest and where a careless account would be both unfair
and analytically useless.

The university's board appointed a special committee, which retained outside
counsel and independent scientific advisers. Its report, released in July 2023,
found that multiple papers on which Tessier-Lavigne was a principal author
contained manipulated data or serious deficiencies, and that these had occurred
in his laboratories.

It also found that the manipulation had been done by others, and that the
evidence did not establish that he personally knew of or participated in it. Its
criticism of him was that he had failed to correct the scientific record with
sufficient decisiveness once problems became apparent, over a period of years.

He resigned as president on 19 July 2023, retained his faculty position, and
requested retractions of three papers and corrections to others.

The distinction between misconduct by a principal investigator and inadequate
correction of the record by one is real, and a book that collapses it forfeits
the right to complain about anyone else's precision.

The scandal is not the point. Universities have scandals.

The point is what the credential rests on. A credential is an instrument for lowering the cost of trusting a stranger, and its value derives entirely from the issuer's capacity to
verify. Stanford's product is verification: this person has been assessed, this
work has been checked, this finding can be relied upon.

A failure of verification at the very top of the institution is a failure of the
product. It is not a moral embarrassment; it is a defect in the thing being
sold.

The Hoover Institution's position on campus became a sustained argument in the
same period, the tension Hoover planted when he founded it.

A university claims institutional disinterest — it does not have positions, its
scholars do. The Hoover Institution has a stated mission with substantive
political content and has supplied personnel to Republican administrations for
half a century. Whether an institution of that kind belongs inside a university
claiming neutrality is a genuine question, and both positions have serious
advocates. Faculty petitions and public arguments over particular fellows' roles
recurred through this period.

Admissions came under simultaneous pressure from two directions. The 2019
admissions bribery prosecutions revealed a market in fraudulent athletic
recruitment and test administration at elite institutions including Stanford, in
which wealthy families purchased places through a consultant. And in June 2023
the Supreme Court held in the Students for Fair Admissions cases that race-
conscious admissions programmes at Harvard and the University of North Carolina
were unlawful, ending an arrangement that had operated for decades.

Between them these episodes put the same question in two forms: what is the
credential actually screening for, and does anyone still believe the answer?

One pressure on the institution is older than any of these and is the same pressure the founders faced.

Stanford remains among the largest landowners on the San Francisco Peninsula, on
the acreage the Founding Grant forbade it to sell. Its expansion is governed by
agreements with Santa Clara County, and the negotiations over those agreements
have for years turned on housing: how much the university must build, for whom,
and at what price, in a region where the cost of housing has become the dominant
political fact.

The university's own workforce cannot generally afford to live near it. Neither
can the teachers, nurses, and municipal employees of the surrounding towns. East
Palo Alto, the community across the freeway, has
been substantially reshaped by displacement pressure from the same market.

The endowment that made the institution permanent is a land portfolio whose
appreciation is inseparable from a housing crisis, and the institution is a
principal beneficiary of that appreciation and a participant in the local
politics that determines whether more housing gets built.

This is the least glamorous of the pressures on Stanford and probably the most
consequential locally. A university endowed with railroad land in 1885 is, a
hundred and forty years later, a large landholder in a region where land is the
binding constraint on ordinary life.

There is a structural account of the research problems that is more damning than
the personal one, and the chapter should give it.

A principal investigator at a major research university runs a laboratory that
may have dozens of people in it, funded by grants that must be continuously
renewed, in a system where publication in a small number of high-prestige
journals determines careers. The investigator is an administrator and a
fundraiser as much as a scientist, and cannot personally verify most of the work
published under their name. The junior researchers doing the work are on
temporary contracts, dependent on the investigator for their futures, and
rewarded for positive results.

Those incentives reliably produce a certain rate of image manipulation,
selective reporting, and unreplicable findings, and the replication crisis
across several fields over the last fifteen years is the aggregate evidence.

That is a defect in the design of the research system rather than in the
character of individuals, and the individuals who bear the cost are
overwhelmingly the junior researchers, whose careers depend on outcomes they do
not control and who are the ones most likely to be blamed when something is
found.

Applications continued to rise. The endowment continued to grow. Licensing
revenue continued. Graduates continued to be placed, and the corridor continued to draw its founders from the same source.

One number makes the point. Applications to selective American universities rose
through precisely the period in which public confidence in higher education, as
measured by the standard survey series, fell sharply. Both things are true at
once, and they are not in tension. People can believe an institution is self-
serving and still want the credential it issues, because the credential's value
does not depend on the institution being admired. It depends on employers
continuing to read it.

The machine is intact. What has eroded is the belief that its outputs can be
taken on faith, and this book has now shown that erosion three times: the
Reformation, the Depression, and here.

In each case the mechanism survived and the story did not, and in each case the
institution responded by restructuring rather than reforming.

The consequence, which the next chapter is about, is that an elite which no
longer enjoys automatic deference stops bothering to be subtle. Legitimacy that
must be argued for is expensive. Power exercised openly is cheaper, and by 2025
it was being exercised openly.

## Rosewood

**Menlo Park, California.**

The Rosewood Sand Hill bills itself as "the modern clubhouse for Silicon Valley… directly on Sand Hill Road… on land owned by Stanford." 

Seventy years after Kleiner and Perkins signed the first lease, the university was still collecting rent on the view. Deal makers ate lunch where venture partners had learned to say no. The land bank outlasted every company on the cap table.

The freshman reporter and the hotel on the hill were the same story told at different scales: who verifies, and who owns the ground under the verification.

\newpage



## The Oval Office

**Washington, D.C. First Trump administration.**

More than a year before the second-term rotunda seating, Musk had sat in the Oval Office opposite President Trump while the administration pressed him to locate Tesla's next gigafactory in the United States — a meeting Siddiqui reconstructs as part of Musk's long courtship of presidential power. 

It is about what happened when a founder who had spent the 2024 cycle campaigning personally was given a formal advisory role after the inauguration described in the previous chapter — a position concerned with spending reduction and the reorganisation of federal agencies, announced publicly and litigated almost immediately over its boundaries and conflicts.

What is structurally familiar is not wealth influencing government. Collis Huntington was more candid about buying Congress in the 1870s. What is novel is a serving executive of firms holding major federal contracts occupying a formal advisory role over the agencies that regulate them, with the conflict identified in public and not resolved in public.

Musk's path to that desk ran through PayPal's alumni network, through Tesla and SpaceX's federal contracts, and through contributions documented in Federal Election Commission filings — the same disclosure trail the rotunda chapter relied on. Vance's vice presidency and Thiel's Senate spending are part of the same circuit; this chapter follows the man who bought a communications platform outright and then sat where cameras could see him.

Official announcements described the advisory remit. Congressional correspondence and court filings disputed how far it reached.

The comparison that matters for this book is visibility. An elite that once exercised influence through donations, foundations, and quiet access began exercising it in front of cameras. The relationships were not new. They had been building for two decades in filings almost nobody read. What changed was that the men on the hill no longer found it necessary to stay up there.

Episode 8's next chapter turns from people to compute — from seating charts to data centres drawing the load of a small city.

\newpage



## Compute

**The Dalles, Oregon. 2006.**

Google broke ground on a data centre campus near the Columbia River in The Dalles, Oregon — a town that wanted the jobs and accepted the water and power draw. Press accounts at the time described the facility as part of a new generation of internet infrastructure sited for cheap electricity and cooling, not for proximity to users.

A building sized for the price of electricity, drawing the load of a small city, is the physical form of the current chokepoint.

---

The bottleneck in artificial intelligence is not the idea, the model, or the
data. It is the hardware.

Models are copyable, and are copied within months; capable open-weight models
follow the frontier closely. Data is abundant and its marginal value falls.
Talent is mobile and extremely well paid precisely because it can leave.

What is scarce is compute — the specialised processors required to train large
models, and behind them the small number of facilities capable of fabricating
the chips at all. Training a frontier model requires capital expenditure at a
scale that only a handful of organisations on earth can contemplate, and that
scale has been rising rather than falling.

Follow the scarcity, as this book has done since a tin route in the Late Bronze
Age. Everything else in this industry is competitive. This is not.

The company that designs most of the relevant hardware was founded in 1993 in
Santa Clara, and spent twenty-five years selling graphics processors to people
who played video games.

Its architecture turned out to suit the mathematics of neural networks
extraordinarily well, and its CUDA software platform — released in 2007, long
before there was a market for it — meant that by the time deep learning became
important, essentially all of the research tooling had been written against
Nvidia's hardware. That software ecosystem is a switching cost, and it is at
least as important as the silicon.

What is durable: through the 2020s a single firm supplied the great majority of
merchant accelerators used for frontier model training, its allocation decisions
determined which laboratories could train what, and its market capitalisation
rose to a level that placed it among the most valuable companies ever listed.

The Standard Oil comparison is apt in the specific respect that matters. A
single firm astride a passage that everyone downstream must use, with pricing
power over an entire industry, is the same structural position — and the same
position the Central Pacific held over California freight.
It is not apt in others: Nvidia does not own the refineries, competition exists,
and the position depends on continued technical leadership rather than on
infrastructure that cannot be duplicated.

Fabrication is the deeper constraint. Nvidia
designs and does not manufacture — the fabless model that ran from Nike's contract factories through the semiconductor industry — and the
manufacturing is concentrated in Taiwan.

So the most valuable position in the American economy depends on a small number
of facilities on an island that another power claims, using lithography
equipment made by one company in the Netherlands.

ChatGPT was released on 30 November 2022 and reached a very large user base
faster than any consumer product before it.

The frequently cited figure of a hundred million users within two months
originates in an investment bank's estimate based on third-party traffic data,
not in a company disclosure, and it is repeated everywhere without that
qualification.

The clearest available test of whether governance structures constrain this
class of organisation ran for five days.

OpenAI had been founded in 2015 as a non-profit, on the explicit premise that
the development of artificial general intelligence was too consequential to be
governed by commercial incentives. It later added a capped-profit subsidiary to
raise capital, with the non-profit board retaining control. The structure was
unusual and was defended as a safeguard.

On 17 November 2023 the board removed the chief executive. Within days,
substantially all of the company's employees signed a letter threatening to
resign, the largest investor made its position clear, and a competitor offered
to hire the entire staff. On 21 November the chief executive was reinstated and
most of the board resigned.

The board did exactly what its charter empowered it to do, and it lost in under
a week to a combination of employee equity interests and investor pressure.

Whatever one thinks of the merits, the structural finding is unambiguous: a
governance mechanism designed specifically to constrain commercial logic in the
most consequential technology of the era was tested once and failed. It happened.

And the triangle reassembles.

The CHIPS and Science Act committed tens of billions in federal money to
domestic semiconductor manufacturing and research from 2022. National
laboratories procure compute at scale. Sovereign wealth funds from the Gulf have
committed very large sums to AI infrastructure. Defence agencies are among the
significant customers for frontier capabilities.

the earlier arrangement — state demand, corporate production,
university research, public money, private returns — is running again, at larger
scale, under the name of industrial policy.

Stanford is present as it has been in every chapter. Its institute for human-
centered artificial intelligence, founded in 2019, publishes the index most
widely cited in policy discussion. Its faculty found the companies. Its
graduates staff them. The university holds positions in the outcomes.

Three groups, and all three are documented.

The people who labelled the training data, working through outsourcing firms in
Kenya, in the Philippines, in India, and in Venezuela, paid a few dollars an
hour to classify text and images including material selected precisely because
it is disturbing. Reporting on this has been extensive and the arrangement is
the content moderation system applied to a new purpose.

The writers, artists, photographers, and programmers whose work was used as
training data without consent, compensation, or in most cases knowledge. The
litigation is unresolved and the underlying fact is not in dispute.

And the communities absorbing the electricity and water. Data centre demand has
become a material factor in regional power planning in several American states,
and the water used for cooling is drawn in a number of cases from stressed
systems.

The book's Introduction promised falsifiability, and this is the chapter where
the argument is most exposed, so it should say what would break it.

If compute ceases to be scarce — through algorithmic efficiency gains that
reduce training costs by orders of magnitude, through a competitor breaking the
software ecosystem lock, or through fabrication capacity expanding faster than
demand — then the chokepoint dissolves and this chapter is describing a
temporary condition rather than a structure. There is real evidence in each
direction: efficiency has improved substantially, credible competing
accelerators exist, and enormous fabrication investment is under way.

If open-weight models remain close to the frontier and can be run at modest
cost, then the capability diffuses and the position at the top is worth much
less than it currently appears.

Whether concentration lasts is a question this book cannot answer, and saying so is the condition of being taken seriously about the parts it can.

A passage that everyone must use. Publicly assisted, privately owned. Allocation decided by a small number of people who know each other, in a corridor forty miles long, with a university at the centre of it.

That is 1862, and this is 2025, and the description fits both without adjustment.

Is this a conspiracy, or is it gravity?

The Introduction made three predictions and said the book would be wrong if they
failed. This is where the verdicts are returned.

*California's great fortunes should cluster around control of bottlenecks rather
than around invention.*

The evidence across five acts is that they do, and consistently enough that the
exceptions are worth noting individually.

Sam Brannan bought the shovels; James Marshall found the gold and died poor. The
Big Four were merchants who owned the only line, not engineers; Theodore Judah,
who surveyed the route and drafted the legislation, was bought out and died at
thirty-seven. Microsoft licensed the operating system and the hardware makers
competed their margins to nothing. Douglas Engelbart demonstrated the interface
of the next fifty years in 1968 at a federally funded institute and captured
none of it. Xerox invented the graphical workstation and could not sell it.
Google's technical contribution was real, and the money came from the auction
rather than from PageRank. Nvidia's position rests as much on a software
ecosystem accumulated over fifteen years as on the chips.

The genuine counter-examples exist and should be stated. Hewlett-Packard, Intel,
Genentech, and Apple's hardware businesses were built substantially on products
rather than on positions, at least initially. The pattern is strong, not
universal.

**Verdict: supported.**

*Public investment should precede private capture rather than follow it.*

The land grants and thirty-year bonds of the Pacific Railway Act. The mission
system's imperial subsidy. Federal purchasing of the entire early output of the
integrated circuit industry. ARPANET. The National Science Foundation backbone,
transferred to commercial operators in 1995 without compensation. Federally
funded research assigned to universities by Bayh-Dole and licensed onward. The
1979 ERISA reinterpretation that admitted pension capital to venture funds. The
CHIPS Act.

In every case public money or public authority came first and private ownership
followed, and in most cases the transfer was accomplished through an instrument
rather than a payment.

**Verdict: supported, and more strongly than the first.**

*The institutions that produce the elite should be endowed by the elite they
produced.*

The Founding Grant of 1885, from a railroad fortune. Hoover's archive in 1919,
from a mining fortune, by a graduate of the first class. The Knight Management
Center in 2011, from a footwear fortune, by a man who wrote the company as a
class paper in the school the building houses. Named buildings, institutes,
professorships, and fellowship programmes across the campus, funded by graduates
whose companies the university helped create and in several cases holds equity
in.

**Verdict: supported.**

A book that only reports confirmations is a polemic. Here is where this one is
most exposed.

**The Santa Clara headnote.** Episode 3 gives prominence to an episode
that specialists consider substantially overstated. Corporate personhood
developed over decades through decided cases, and the headnote did not create
it.

**Selection versus causation.** The book's central structural claim is that the
corridor persists because chokepoints reproduce there. The competing explanation
is that talent and capital cluster for straightforwardly productive reasons —
agglomeration effects, thick labour markets, tacit knowledge — and that the
chokepoint reading is a moralised description of an ordinary economic
phenomenon. This is the strongest objection in existence to this book and it
cannot be fully answered, because the two explanations predict most of the same
observations.

**Single-source chapters.** Several chapters rest more heavily than is ideal on
one account, particularly in Episodes 1 to 4 and in the parts of Episode 7 that depend on
institutional histories written by participants.

**Counterfactuals.** The book argues that secession chose the railroad route and
that the route created the Big Four and therefore Stanford. That chain is well
evidenced at each link and is still a chain, and chains of counterfactuals get
weaker with length.

Stated fairly: California's corridor produced the transistor, the integrated
circuit, the personal computer, the internet's commercialisation, modern
biotechnology, and the current generation of machine learning. Whatever the
distributional consequences, the productive achievement is real, enormous, and
has raised living standards worldwide. Talent clusters because clustering works,
capital follows returns, and describing this as chokepoint rent extraction is a
way of not saying that the place is unusually good at making things.

The answer this book gives is that both are true and they are not in tension.
Agglomeration is real and it explains why an industry concentrates somewhere. It
does not explain why the surplus accrues to the position rather than to the
invention, why public money precedes private capture with such regularity, or
why the institution that certifies the participants is endowed by them. Those
are distributional facts, and they are what the book is about.

The Introduction set this book against Max Weber, who argued in 1905 that
capitalism's animating spirit was Protestant asceticism — worldly discipline
read as evidence of divine election.

The point of the doctrine was never economic. Its function was moral: it made
prosperity legible as virtue, so that a man who had accumulated a great deal
could understand the accumulation as a sign rather than as an appetite. It
answered the question every durable system of inequality has to answer, which is
why the winner deserved to win.

California's version keeps the structure and inverts the content. Not thrift but
leverage. Not the monastery but the garage. Not election but merit — a story in
which the fortune is evidence of insight, nerve, and willingness to work in a
shed.

And the garage story does exactly the work the doctrine of election used to do.
It is sincerely held, it is frequently true at the level of the individual, and
it systematically obscures the thing this book has spent five acts documenting:
that the shed was near a university with unsellable land, that the university
was endowed by a railroad built with public money, that the customer for the
first products was the federal government, and that the professor who told them
to stay had a written plan.

So the answer to the Introduction's question is that California's spirit of
capitalism is chokepoint rent extraction wearing frontier merit as a costume.
Not because the people in it are frauds — most of them are not — but because a
system that requires an account of its own justice will generate one, and the
one it generates will be the flattering version.

Weber thought the spirit came first and the accumulation followed. In California
it runs the other way. The accumulation came first, and the spirit was assembled
afterwards to explain it.

There was no conspiracy because none was required, and this is the conclusion
the whole book has been arriving at.

Land, research funding, legitimacy, and network compound on each other without
anyone intending an outcome. A dean concentrates resources where federal money
is going. A landowner leases to tenants who will hire his graduates. An investor
funds the founder he already knows. A donor endows the school that certified
him. Each decision is individually rational, locally defensible, and entirely
unremarkable, and the aggregate is a machine that reproduces an elite across a
hundred and forty years.

That is a more troubling finding than a conspiracy, and it is the true one. A
conspiracy has participants who can be prosecuted, documents that can be
subpoenaed, and a decision that could have gone the other way. This has none of
those. It has gravity.

## January 20, 2025

Back in the rotunda on January 20, 2025, the men who owned the indexes and the accelerators sat where the cameras could see them. Musk. Zuckerberg. Bezos. Pichai. Cook. Behind the family. Ahead of the cabinet.

Rail controlled movement. Stanford controls succession.

\newpage

# Coda: The Street Names

**Stanford, California. Dusk.**

Walk with the narrator. No companion. Only the signs.

---

Different societies name their streets after different things, and the Peninsula
names its after the people who owned it — mission founders, railroad families,
university presidents, semiconductor executives, donors. The memorial is the
monopoly, and it is the most honest document in California.

Read a street sign as a document and it tells you four things: who, when, why,
and who decided. The Peninsula's signs answer all four consistently.

Different societies commemorate different things. British streets carry empire.
French streets carry artists and writers. Most American streets carry
politicians and trees. The Peninsula names its streets after the people who
owned it, and it does so in layers that correspond, in order, to the chapters
here.

The mission layer is oldest and still on the map: Serra, Junipero Serra
Boulevard, and El Camino Real, which is a Spanish supply road that became the
region's main street. The railroad layer sits on top of it — Stanford, Crocker,
Hopkins, Huntington — and the chapter notes what kind of thing carries each
name: a university, a hotel, a library, a hill. Then the institutional layer,
with Hoover Tower, Terman, and Jordan Hall, which was renamed. Renamings are
themselves evidence; what a society does when a name becomes unusable says more
than the original naming did. And then the corporate layer: Page Mill, Sand
Hill, and the donor names on the buildings of the campus.

The connective names from Cottle's 2014 guide — Gálvez, Serra, Alvarado, Coutts,
Palm Drive, Jordan, Hansen, Knight — slot into the layer each belongs to.
Appendix A is the reference table. The argument does not depend on any single
name. It depends on the sequence, which is already a walk.

previous chapter's ledger and records what is commemorated and what is not: the
Muwekma Ohlone, the Central Pacific's crews, the people sterilised under the
1909 law, the farm workers. The absences are recorded without comment, because
the list is the argument.

The grid is a ledger. It has been legible the entire time and nobody reads it,
which is what subliminal means.

What does a civilisation look like from its street signs?

Read a street sign as a document and it answers four questions. Who is
commemorated. When. For what. And who decided.

The Peninsula's signs answer all four with unusual consistency, and the answer
is the argument of this book, recorded in the one medium that outlasts
newspapers, lawsuits, and reputations.

The comparison has to come first or the claim is meaningless, because all street
names commemorate something.

British street names carry empire and aristocracy: the regiments, the victories,
the ducal estates that were subdivided. Parisian streets carry writers,
painters, scientists, and the dates of revolutions. Dutch cities name streets
after painters. Post-war German cities renamed extensively and now carry
resisters and victims, which is its own kind of statement.

The standard American pattern is politicians, presidents, trees, and numbers. A
grid with Washington, Lincoln, Elm, Maple, and First through Twelfth is the
default of the American town, and it commemorates a national civic culture
rather than a local proprietor.

The Peninsula is different. It names its streets after the people who owned it.

Walk or drive the corridor and the sequence is chronological, because each layer
of ownership named what it built and the previous names were mostly left in
place.

**The mission layer.** Galvez Street runs from El Camino Real to Serra Street
across the front of the campus. It is named for José de Gálvez, the inspector
general who expelled the Jesuits from New Spain and then commissioned the
Portolá–Serra expedition — the administrator who had the idea, not the priest
who walked it. Junípero Serra Boulevard holds the western edge. Lasuen Mall,
the Olmsted axis through the Quad, is named for Serra's successor. Mendoza Road
is named for the first viceroy of New Spain. El Camino Real, the royal road, is
still the Peninsula's main street. The towns themselves — San Mateo, San Carlos,
San Bruno, Santa Clara, San José — are mission and saint names. The Portolá road is a drive you can still take.

**The rancho layer.** Alvarado Row was named by David Starr Jordan for Juan
Bautista Alvarado, the governor who led the 1836 revolt against Mexican
centralization; his grandfather had been on the Portolá expedition. Peter Coutts
Road, Frenchman's Road, French Lake Drive, and Ayrshire Farm Lane record the
French banker who assembled Matadero Rancho and sold it to Leland Stanford in
1881 for $140,000 — the last private transaction before the farm became a
university. Rancho names survive as neighbourhoods and parks: Las Pulgas, San
Antonio, Corte Madera, San Francisquito. A boundary once recorded is cheaper to
keep than to redraw.

**The railroad layer.** Governor's Avenue follows the trotting track of the Palo
Alto Stock Farm; the governor is Stanford himself. Electioneer is named for his
champion stallion — the horse-breeding operation that produced Eadweard
Muybridge's motion studies. Palm Drive, once called University Avenue, is the
ceremonial front door; Memorial Arch stood at its head until 1906. Museum Way
leads to the memorial Jane Stanford built for her son, now the Cantor Center.
Off campus the Big Four appear as a hill, a hotel, a library, an art museum, and
the university. The men who owned the state's transport are commemorated in the
cultural institutions they endowed.

**The institutional layer.** Hoover Tower is the tallest structure on the campus
and holds the archive begun in 1919. Nathan Abbott Way is named for the man
recruited in 1893 to found the Law School. Durand Way and Samuel Morris Way
record the engineering faculty that built propulsion science and, in Morris's
case, the Los Angeles water system; Hoover dedicated Morris's road in 1933.
Jordan Hall carried the name of the first president — ichthyologist, pacifist,
eugenicist, and the man who named half the streets in this chapter — until it
was renamed in 2020. Serra came off several buildings in the same review; the
boulevard kept him.

Renamings deserve attention, because what a society does when a name becomes
unusable is more informative than the original naming. It records the point at
which the institution decided the association had become a liability, and it
generally happens long after the facts are known.

**The corporate layer.** Hansen Way runs from Page Mill Road to El Camino
through the Research Park, named for the physicist whose klystron work seeded
Varian and the microwave industry; he died of beryllium exposure. Page Mill was
a lumber road before it was an industrial one. Porter Drive, off Page Mill, sits
in Terman's electrical-engineering district. Sand Hill Road is four minutes
from the Quad. Coyote Hill is where Xerox built PARC. Knight Way and the Knight
Management Center carry the name of the student who wrote a 1962 paper about
Japanese running shoes. Avery Mall and the Munger Graduate Residences are the
donor era in its ordinary form: a construction family, a Berkshire partner, a
name on the housing stock.

The full table is in Appendix A. The argument does not depend on any single
sign.

The sharpest section is the negative one, and it consists of going through the
previous chapter's ledger and asking what is named after each entry.

The Muwekma Ohlone, whose territory the campus occupies. There is little on the
map, and what recognition exists is recent and small.

The Chinese workers of the Central Pacific. There is no major thoroughfare, no
building on the campus that their labour endowed, and no monument at the scale
of the achievement. The most substantial commemorations are recent, modest, and
mostly the work of descendant organisations rather than of the institutions that
benefited.

The people sterilised under the 1909 law. Nothing.

The farm workers. Cesar Chavez has streets and schools across California, which
is a genuine and substantial exception, and they are overwhelmingly in the
Central Valley and in urban Latino neighbourhoods rather than on the Peninsula.

The contract workforce of the present. Nothing, and it is too early to expect
anything.

The list is the argument and it should be presented without commentary. What a
place commemorates is what it has decided to remember, and the Peninsula has
decided to remember the people who held the title.

The layer being laid down currently is worth recording while it happens, because
future readers will find it as unremarkable as the earlier layers look now.

Institutional naming has become a priced transaction with published tariffs.
Universities maintain schedules: a professorship costs this, a laboratory that,
a building the other. Hospitals, museums, concert halls, and public parks
operate comparably. A donor makes a gift and the name goes up, generally in
perpetuity, occasionally with a term.

This is not new — the Medici commissioned altarpieces, and the Victorian
industrialists put their names on libraries — but the formalisation is. What was
once a negotiated honour is now a listed product, and the effect is that the
permanent memorial landscape of a wealthy society is allocated by the same
mechanism as everything else in this book: to whoever holds the surplus.

Two consequences follow. The commemorative record of an era becomes a record of
its donors rather than of its achievements, and the two overlap only partially.
And the names are extremely difficult to remove, because removal requires an
institution to repudiate a gift it has already spent, which is why the renamings
described above took so long and were so contested.

A building name is a chokepoint on public memory, sold at a listed price, held
in perpetuity, and defended by the recipient. That is a strange sentence and it
describes standard practice at every major institution in the corridor.

Here is the claim, stated plainly.

The street grid of the San Francisco Peninsula is an accurate record of who
controlled it and in what order. It is more accurate than most published
histories, because it was not written to persuade anyone of anything. Each layer
was laid down by people naming what they had just built, for reasons that seemed
obvious at the time, and the accumulation is a chronology of ownership that
nobody edited.

It has been legible the entire time. Every person who has ever driven down El
Camino Real, turned onto Page Mill Road, and passed Hoover Tower on the way to
Sand Hill Road has read the whole argument of this book in the correct order,
without noticing.

That is what subliminal means. Not hidden — beneath the threshold at which
something is consciously registered. The signs are twelve feet up and lit at
night.

## The walk

The light is going. Campus empties. The narrator walks without a companion — only the names.

Gálvez Street runs toward Serra. Lasuen Mall cuts the Quad. El Camino Real is still the Peninsula's main street. Turn onto Alvarado Row, named by David Starr Jordan for a governor whose grandfather had walked with Portolá. Stanford Avenue leads to Jane Stanford Way — renamed from Serra Mall in 2020, the same review that took Jordan's name off a biology building while leaving the boulevard's saint in place.

Hoover Tower holds the archive. Terman and Page Mill name the engineering district.

The street signs are twelve feet up and lit at night. 

It was never a secret. It was only subliminal.
